## I. My Boyhood / I. 我的少年时光

===Original===
My father belonged to the widespread family of the Campbells, and possessed a small landed property in the north of Argyll. But although of long descent and high connection, he was no richer than many a farmer of a few hundred acres. For, with the exception of a narrow belt of arable land at its foot, a bare hill formed almost the whole of his possessions. The sheep ate over it, and no doubt found it good; I bounded and climbed all over it, and thought it a kingdom. From my very childhood, I had rejoiced in being alone. The sense of room about me had been one of my greatest delights. Hence, when my thoughts go back to those old years, it is not the house, nor the family room, nor that in which I slept, that first of all rises before my inward vision, but that desolate hill, the top of which was only a wide expanse of moorland, rugged with height and hollow, and dangerous with deep, dark pools, but in many portions purple with large-belled heather, and crowded with cranberry and blaeberry plants. Most of all, I loved it in the still autumn morning, outstretched in stillness, high uplifted towards the heaven. On every stalk hung the dew in tiny drops, which, while the rising sun was low, sparkled and burned with the hues of all the gems. Here and there a bird gave a cry; no other sound awoke the silence. I never see the statue of the Roman youth, praying with outstretched arms, and open, empty, level palms, as waiting to receive and hold the blessing of the gods, but that outstretched barren heath rises before me, as if it meant the same thing as the statue﻿—or were, at least, the fit room in the middle space of which to set the praying and expectant youth.

There was one spot upon the hill, halfway between the valley and the moorland, which was my favourite haunt. This part of the hill was covered with great blocks of stone, of all shapes and sizes﻿—here crowded together, like the slain where the battle had been fiercest; there parting asunder from spaces of delicate green﻿—of softest grass. In the centre of one of these green spots, on a steep part of the hill, were three huge rocks﻿—two projecting out of the hill, rather than standing up from it, and one, likewise projecting from the hill, but lying across the tops of the two, so as to form a little cave, the back of which was the side of the hill. This was my refuge, my home within a home, my study﻿—and, in the hot noons, often my sleeping chamber, and my house of dreams. If the wind blew cold on the hillside, a hollow of lulling warmth was there, scooped as it were out of the body of the blast, which, sweeping around, whistled keen and thin through the cracks and crannies of the rocky chaos that lay all about; in which confusion of rocks the wind plunged, and flowed, and eddied, and withdrew, as the sea-waves on the cliffy shores or the unknown rugged bottoms. Here I would often lie, as the sun went down, and watch the silent growth of another sea, which the stormy ocean of the wind could not disturb﻿—the sea of the darkness. First it would begin to gather in the bottom of hollow places. Deep valleys, and all little pits on the hillsides, were wellsprings where it gathered, and whence it seemed to overflow, till it had buried the earth beneath its mass, and, rising high into the heavens, swept over the faces of the stars, washed the blinding day from them, and let them shine, down through the waters of the dark, to the eyes of men below. I would lie till nothing but the stars and the dim outlines of hills against the sky was to be seen, and then rise and go home, as sure of my path as if I had been descending a dark staircase in my father’s house.

===Chinese===
我父亲属于支系广布的坎贝尔家族（the Campbells），在阿盖尔（Argyll）北部拥有一小块地产。尽管家世绵长、姻亲显贵，他却并不比许多坐拥数百英亩田地的农夫更富裕。因为除了山脚下一窄条可耕地之外，一座光秃秃的荒丘几乎便是他全部的家产。羊群在坡上啃食，无疑也觉得它甚好；我在上面蹦跳攀爬，跑遍了每一寸地方，把它当作一个王国。自打记事起，我便以独处为乐。四下里那种空旷开阔之感，一直是我最大的乐趣之一。因此，每当我的思绪回到那些久远的岁月，最先浮现在我内视（inward vision）之前的，并不是那座宅子，也不是一家人共聚的那间屋子，更不是我安寝的那间房，而是那座孤寂的荒丘——山顶不过是一片辽阔的高沼荒原，高处与低洼处崎岖不平，深深的暗潭暗藏险境，然而许多地段都因大朵钟形的石楠花而泛着紫色，其间蔓越橘与越橘密密丛生。我最爱它，是在那宁静的秋日清晨——它向四面静静地舒展，高高地昂向天宇。每一根草茎上都悬着细小的露珠；当初升的太阳还低低地挂着时，它们便闪烁、燃烧，迸射出各色宝石的光泽。偶尔有一只鸟啼叫一声；再没有别的声响惊扰这份寂静。每当我看见那座罗马少年的雕像——他双臂伸展，空空的手掌平平摊开，仿佛等着承接、握住众神的福泽——我眼前便会浮现出那片舒展绵延的荒凉石楠原，仿佛它与那雕像所表的是同一番意味——或者至少，它正是那方恰如其分的所在，其正中的空处，恰可安放这祈祷等候着的少年。

山上有那么一处所在，在山谷与荒原之间大约一半路程的位置，是我最爱流连的去处。山的这一部分遍布着巨大的石块，形状大小各异——有的地方挤作一堆，仿佛鏖战最烈之处横七竖八的尸首；有的地方又彼此分开，让出一块块细嫩的绿地——那是最柔软的青草。在其中一块这样的绿地中央——那处山势陡峭——横着三块巨岩：两块与其说是从山体上立起，不如说是从山体里探出；还有一块同样从山体探出，却横搭在那两块的顶上，于是在它们之下形成一个小小的石洞，洞的背壁便是山坡本身。这就是我的避难所，我的家中之家，我的书房——而在炎热的正午，又往往是我的寝室，我的梦之屋。倘若寒风在坡上刮得凛冽，这里便有一洼抚人入眠的暖意，仿佛是从那阵风的本体里剜出来的一般；那风绕着四围打旋，从四周乱石丛生的罅隙孔窍间穿过，发出尖细的啸声；风在这片乱石之中俯冲、漫流、回旋、退却，恰似海浪拍击着峭壁海岸，或在那无人知晓的崎岖海底翻涌。每当夕阳西沉，我常常躺在这里，凝望另一片海无声地涨起——那是风之狂洋也搅扰不动的一片海——黑暗的海。起初，它先在低洼处的底部聚拢。幽深的峡谷，还有山坡上所有的小坑洼，都是它汇聚的泉眼，仿佛从那里满溢而出，直到它用自身的厚重将大地湮没；继而高高升入天穹，漫过群星的面庞，将那刺目的白昼从星上洗去，让它们透过黑暗的水波，重新向下闪耀到世人的眼里。我就这样躺着，直到眼前只剩下星辰，以及映着天光的山影那模糊的轮廓，然后才起身回家，对脚下路径的把握，就像在我父亲宅中走下一道漆黑楼梯那般笃定。

===Original===
On the opposite side the valley, another hill lay parallel to mine; and behind it, at some miles’ distance, a great mountain. As often as, in my hermit’s cave, I lifted my eyes from the volume I was reading, I saw this mountain before me. Very different was its character from that of the hill on which I was seated. It was a mighty thing, a chieftain of the race, seamed and scarred, featured with chasms and precipices and over-leaning rocks, themselves huge as hills; here blackened with shade, there overspread with glory; interlaced with the silvery lines of falling streams, which, hurrying from heaven to earth, cared not how they went, so it were downwards. Fearful stories were told of the gulfs, sullen waters, and dizzy heights upon that terror-haunted mountain. In storms the wind roared like thunder in its caverns and along the jagged sides of its cliffs, but at other times that uplifted land﻿—uplifted, yet secret and full of dismay﻿—lay silent as a cloud on the horizon.

I had a certain peculiarity of constitution, which I have some reason to believe I inherit. It seems to have its root in an unusual delicacy of hearing, which often conveys to me sounds inaudible to those about me. This I have had many opportunities of proving. It has likewise, however, brought me sounds which I could never trace back to their origin; though they may have arisen from some natural operation which I had not perseverance or mental acuteness sufficient to discover. From this, or, it may be, from some deeper cause with which this is connected, arose a certain kind of fearfulness associated with the sense of hearing, of which I have never heard a corresponding instance. Full as my mind was of the wild and sometimes fearful tales of a Highland nursery, fear never entered my mind by the eyes, nor, when I brooded over tales of terror, and fancied new and yet more frightful embodiments of horror, did I shudder at any imaginable spectacle, or tremble lest the fancy should become fact, and from behind the whin-bush or the elder-hedge should glide forth the tall swaying form of the Boneless. When alone in bed, I used to lie awake, and look out into the room, peopling it with the forms of all the persons who had died within the scope of my memory and acquaintance. These fancied forms were vividly present to my imagination. I pictured them pale, with dark circles around their hollow eyes, visible by a light which glimmered within them; not the light of life, but a pale, greenish phosphorescence, generated by the decay of the brain inside. Their garments were white and trailing, but torn and soiled, as by trying often in vain to get up out of the buried coffin. But so far from being terrified by these imaginings, I used to delight in them; and in the long winter evenings, when I did not happen to have any book that interested me sufficiently, I used even to look forward with expectation to the hour when, laying myself straight upon my back, as if my bed were my coffin, I could call up from underground all who had passed away, and see how they fared, yea, what progress they had made towards final dissolution of form﻿—but all the time, with my fingers pushed hard into my ears, lest the faintest sound should invade the silent citadel of my soul. If inadvertently I removed one of my fingers, the agony of terror I instantly experienced is indescribable. I can compare it to nothing but the rushing in upon my brain of a whole churchyard of spectres. The very possibility of hearing a sound, in such a mood, and at such a time, was almost enough to paralyse me. So I could scare myself in broad daylight, on the open hillside, by imagining unintelligible sounds; and my imagination was both original and fertile in the invention of such. But my mind was too active to be often subjected to such influences. Indeed life would have been hardly endurable had these moods been of more than occasional occurrence. As I grew older, I almost outgrew them. Yet sometimes one awful dread would seize me﻿—that, perhaps, the prophetic power manifest in the gift of second sight, which, according to the testimony of my old nurse, had belonged to several of my ancestors, had been in my case transformed in kind without losing its nature, transferring its abode from the sight to the hearing, whence resulted its keenness, and my fear and suffering.

===Chinese===
在山谷的对面，另有一座山与我这座荒丘平行横亘；在它后方，数英里开外，是一座巍峨的大山。每当我在那隐士般的石洞中，从正读着的书卷上抬起眼来，便看见这座大山耸立在我面前。它的性情，与我坐着的这座荒丘截然不同。它是一个庞然伟物，是同族中的酋长，沟壑纵横、伤痕累累，以裂谷、峭壁和外倾探出的巨岩为面容，那些巨岩本身便大如山丘；此处被浓荫染得发黑，彼处又披覆一片辉煌；其间交错着一道道银线般的飞瀑，从天际急坠向大地，全不顾自己走成什么模样，只要是一路向下便罢。关于那座为恐怖所萦绕的大山，人们讲述着种种骇人听闻的故事：深渊、阴沉的死水，以及令人目眩的高处。暴风雨来时，风在它的洞穴里、在它峭壁那锯齿般的侧面上轰鸣如雷；但在别的时候，那片高高隆起的土地——高高隆起，却又幽秘、满含骇惧——便如一片云般寂静地卧在地平线上。

我的体质有一种特殊的禀性，我有几分理由相信它是得自遗传。它似乎植根于一种异乎寻常的听觉之敏锐，这种敏锐常常把周围人听不见的声响传到我耳中。这一点，我有过许多机会加以验证。然而，它也同样带给我一些我永远无法追本溯源的声响；它们或许产生于某种自然的作用，只是我缺少足够的毅力或心智的敏锐去发现罢了。由此——又或许，是由某种与此相关、却更为深远的原因——生出了一种与听觉相系的恐惧，其同类我还从未听人说过有第二例。尽管我满脑子都是高地（Highland）育儿室里那些狂野、有时可怖的传说，恐惧却从不曾从眼睛进入我的心；即便当我反复咀嚼那些恐怖故事，构想出一些新的、更加骇人的恐怖化身时，我也绝不会为任何能想象到的景象而战栗，不会担心幻想竟化作现实，那无骨鬼（the Boneless）高大摇曳的身形会从荆豆丛或接骨木篱后悄然滑出。独自躺在床上时，我常常醒着不睡，朝房间里四下张望，把我记忆与相识范围内所有亡故之人的身影，一一安置在房中各处。这些构想出的身影，在我的想象中鲜活如真。我把他们想成面色惨白，凹陷的眼眶四周晕着一圈青黑，靠着一种在他们体内幽幽闪烁的光而清晰可辨；那不是生命之光，而是一种苍白的、泛着微绿的磷光，由里面那正在朽烂的大脑所生出。他们的衣裳洁白、曳地，却已破损污浊，仿佛是因为他们屡屡想从那埋入土中的棺木里爬起，又屡屡徒劳。然而，我非但不被这些想象吓倒，反而乐在其中；在漫长的冬夜，当我恰好手边没有任何一本能让我足够入迷的书时，我甚至会满心盼望那个时辰的到来——那时我只要仰面笔直地躺好，仿佛我的床便是我的棺木，就能从地下唤起所有逝者，看看他们的境况，是的，看看他们朝着形体最终的消解已走到了哪一步——然而这整个过程里，我都把手指死死塞紧双耳，生怕哪怕最微弱的一丝声响，会侵入我灵魂那座寂静的城堡。倘若我一不留神松开了一根手指，那一瞬间我所尝到的恐惧之苦，简直无法形容。我只能把它比作整整一座墓园的鬼魅，一齐朝我的脑中涌来。处在这样的心境、这样的时刻，单单是“有可能听见一声响”这件事本身，便几乎足以让我瘫软。就这样，即便在大白天、在空旷的山坡上，我也能靠想象出一些辨不清的声响把自己吓住；而我的想象力，在编造这类声响时，既新奇独创，又源源不绝。但我的心智太过活跃，不常受这等情绪左右。说实在的，倘若这种心境不止偶一为之地出现，生活几乎就难以忍受了。随着年岁渐长，我几乎已把它们彻底摆脱。然而，有时一种可怕的忧惧仍会攫住我——也许，那显露在第二视觉（second sight）这一天赋中的预知之力（据我老保姆所言，这天赋曾属于我的好几位先祖），到了我身上只是改换了品类，却未失其本性，将栖居之所从视觉迁到了听觉，由此才生出它的敏锐，以及我的恐惧与痛苦。

## II. The Second Hearing / 二 · 第二次听闻

===Original===
One summer evening, I had lingered longer than usual in my rocky retreat: I had lain half dreaming in the mouth of my cave, till the shadows of evening had fallen, and the gloaming had deepened halfway towards the night. But the night had no more terrors for me than the day. Indeed, in such regions there is a solitariness for which there seems a peculiar sense, and upon which the shadows of night sink with a strange relief, hiding from the eye the wide space which yet they throw more open to the imagination. When I lifted my head, only a star here and there caught my eye; but, looking intently into the depths of blue-grey, I saw that they were crowded with twinkles. The mountain rose before me, a huge mass of gloom; but its several peaks stood out against the sky with a clear, pure, sharp outline, and looked nearer to me than the bulk from which they rose heavenwards. One star trembled and throbbed upon the very tip of the loftiest, the central peak, which seemed the spire of a mighty temple where the light was worshipped—crowned, therefore, in the darkness, with the emblem of the day. I was lying, as I have said, with this fancy still in my thought, when suddenly I heard, clear, though faint and far away, the sound as of the iron-shod hoofs of a horse, in furious gallop along an uneven rocky surface. It was more like a distant echo than an original sound. It seemed to come from the face of the mountain, where no horse, I knew, could go at that speed, even if its rider courted certain destruction. There was a peculiarity, too, in the sound—a certain tinkle, or clank, which I fancied myself able, by auricular analysis, to distinguish from the body of the sound. Supposing the sound to be caused by the feet of a horse, the peculiarity was just such as would result from one of the shoes being loose. A terror—strange even to my experience—seized me, and I hastened home. The sounds gradually died away as I descended the hill. Could they have been an echo from some precipice of the mountain? I knew of no road lying so that, if a horse were galloping upon it, the sounds would be reflected from the mountain to me.

===Chinese===
某个夏日傍晚，我在那片岩石避居处流连得比往常更久：我半卧在洞穴口做着白日梦，直到夜幕的阴影四合，薄暮（gloaming）向黑夜又深了一层。然而黑夜于我，并不比白昼更多几分可怖。说实在的，在这样的地方，有一种别具意味的孤寂，夜色沉沉地落在其上，竟生出一种奇异的抚慰——它遮住了眼中那片辽阔空旷，却又把它更敞开地抛给了想象。我抬起头时，只有稀疏几颗星映入眼帘；可当我凝神望进那蓝灰色的深处，才发觉那里早已簇满了闪烁的微光。那座山在我面前拔起，一团巨大的幽暗；但它的几座峰峦却以清晰、纯净、锐利的轮廓在天幕上凸显出来，显得比那浑然托举它们升向天际的庞大山体离我更近。最高、亦即居中的那座主峰之巅，颤动着、搏动着一颗星，那峰仿佛是一座崇奉光明的雄伟神庙的尖顶——于是在黑暗之中，它被加冕上了白昼的象征。如我所说，我正这样躺着，脑中还萦绕着这番遐想，忽听得一个声音，清晰，却又微弱而遥远，像是一匹钉了铁掌的马蹄，在崎岖不平的岩面上疯狂疾驰。那声音与其说是原声，不如说是远处的回响。它似乎从山壁那一面传来；而我深知，那山壁之上绝无马匹能以那种速度奔行，纵使骑手自取粉身碎骨也不能。那声音里还有一处异样——一种叮当、或铿锵的脆响，我自忖凭「听觉分析」便能把它从整体声响中辨析出来。倘若这声音确为一匹马的蹄足所致，那么这一异样，正与其中一只马蹄铁松脱所引起的效果相同。一阵恐惧——即便以我的阅历也觉陌生——攫住了我，我赶忙回家。下山途中，那声音渐渐消散。莫非是山中某处悬崖的回声？我想不出有哪条路的位置，能让一匹马在其上疾驰时，蹄声由山壁反射到我这里。

===Original===
The next day, in one of my rambles, I found myself near the cottage of my old foster-mother, who was distantly related to us, and was a trusted servant in the family at the time I was born. On the death of my mother, which took place almost immediately after my birth, she had taken the entire charge of me, and had brought me up, though with difficulty; for she used to tell me, I should never be either *folk* or *fairy*. For some years she had lived alone in a cottage, at the bottom of a deep green circular hollow, upon which, in walking over a healthy tableland, one came with a sudden surprise. I was her frequent visitor. She was a tall, thin, aged woman, with eager eyes, and well-defined clear-cut features. Her voice was harsh, but with an undertone of great tenderness. She was scrupulously careful in her attire, which was rather above her station. Altogether, she had much the bearing of a gentlewoman. Her devotion to me was quite motherly. Never having had any family of her own, although she had been the wife of one of my father’s shepherds, she expended the whole maternity of her nature upon me. She was always my first resource in any perplexity, for I was sure of all the help she could give me. And as she had much influence with my father, who was rather severe in his notions, I had had occasion to beg her interference. No necessity of this sort, however, had led to my visit on the present occasion.

===Chinese===
次日，我在一次闲游中，不知不觉走到了老乳母的小屋附近。她与我们家沾着一点远亲，我出生那年，她是家中颇受信赖的仆人。我母亲在生下我之后几乎随即辞世，她便将我全权接手抚养，虽则颇费周章；因为她常对我说，我这辈子怕是既成不了「凡人」，也成不了「仙灵」。数年来她独自住在一座小屋里，那屋坐落在一处幽深翠绿的圆形凹地之底，人若走过一片长满石楠的高原台地，会蓦然撞见它。我是她那里的常客。她是个瘦高年迈的妇人，眼神热切，五官清晰分明、轮廓硬朗。她嗓音沙哑，底里却透着极大的温柔。她对衣着一丝不苟，穿戴得比她的身份还略胜一筹。总而言之，她颇有些良家妇人的气度。她待我的情分，完全是慈母一般的。她自己膝下无儿无女——尽管她曾是我父亲一个牧羊人的妻子——便把天性中全部的母性都倾注在我身上。每逢我为难困顿，她总是我头一个想到的依靠，因为我笃定她会把能给的助力都给我。又因她在父亲面前颇有分量，而父亲治家的理念颇为严厉，我有时便得求她出面斡旋。不过，今日我登门，倒并非为此类缘故。

===Original===
I ran down the side of the basin, and entered the little cottage. Nurse was seated on a chair by the wall, with her usual knitting, a stocking, in one hand; but her hands were motionless, and her eyes wide open and fixed. I knew that the neighbours stood rather in awe of her, on the ground that she had the second sight; but, although she often told us frightful enough stories, she had never alluded to such a gift as being in her possession. Now I concluded at once that she was *seeing*. I was confirmed in this conclusion when, seeming to come to herself suddenly, she covered her head with her plaid, and sobbed audibly, in spite of her efforts to command herself. But I did not dare to ask her any questions, nor did she attempt any excuse for her behaviour. After a few moments, she unveiled herself, rose, and welcomed me with her usual kindness; then got me some refreshment, and began to question me about matters at home. After a pause, she said suddenly: “When are you going to get your commission, Duncan, do you know?” I replied that I had heard nothing of it; that I did not think my father had influence or money enough to procure me one, and that I feared I should have no such good chance of distinguishing myself. She did not answer, but nodded her head three times, slowly and with compressed lips—apparently as much as to say, “I know better.”

===Chinese===
我顺着凹地一侧奔下，走进那座小屋。保姆靠墙坐在椅上，一手照例拿着她正织的活计——一只袜子；可她的双手一动不动，双眼圆睁、定定地凝着。我知道邻居们对她颇有几分敬畏，缘由是她有「第二视觉」（the second sight）；然而，尽管她常给我们讲些够骇人的故事，她却从未暗示过自己拥有这般天赋。此刻我立刻断定，她正在「视」（seeing）。她像是猛地回过神来，用方格围巾蒙住了头，尽管竭力自制，仍发出了听得见的啜泣——这一幕证实了我的判断。但我不敢问她半句，她也不曾为自己的失态作任何辩解。过了片刻，她揭开头巾，站起身，照旧亲切地迎我；随后给我张罗了些点心，问起家里的种种。停了一阵，她忽然开口：「邓肯，你那委任状几时能下来，你可知道？」我答说毫无消息；又说我想父亲没有那么大的门路、也没有那么多钱为我谋得一职，只怕我不会有那样建功扬名的好机会。她没有作答，只是慢慢点了三次头，双唇紧抿——那神情分明是在说：「我心里有数。」

===Original===
Just as I was leaving her, it occurred to me to mention that I had heard an odd sound the night before. She turned towards me, and looked at me fixedly. “What was it like, Duncan, my dear?”

“Like a horse galloping with a loose shoe,” I replied.

“Duncan, Duncan, my darling!” she said, in a low, trembling voice, but with passionate earnestness, “you did not hear it? Tell me that you did not hear it! You only want to frighten poor old nurse: someone has been telling you the story!”

===Chinese===
就在我向她告辞之际，我忽然想起该提一句：昨夜我听见了一个古怪的声响。她转过身来，定定地看着我。「那是什么样的声响，邓肯，我的好孩子？」

「像一匹马蹄铁松脱着还在疾驰。」我答道。

「邓肯，邓肯，我的心肝！」她说，声音低而发颤，却带着热烈的恳切，「你没有真的听见吧？告诉我你没听见！你不过是想吓唬可怜的老保姆罢了：一定是有人把那桩事讲给你听了！」

===Original===
It was my turn to be frightened now; for the matter became at once associated with my fears as to the possible nature of my auricular peculiarities. I assured her that nothing was farther from my intention than to frighten her; that, on the contrary, she had rather alarmed me; and I begged her to explain. But she sat down white and trembling, and did not speak. Presently, however, she rose again, and saying, “I have known it happen sometimes without anything very bad following,” began to put away the basin and plate I had been using, as if she would compel herself to be calm before me. I renewed my entreaties for an explanation, but without avail. She begged me to be content for a few days, as she was quite unable to tell the story at present. She promised, however, of her own accord, that before I left home she would tell me all she knew.

===Chinese===
这回轮到我害怕了；因为这事立刻与我那「听觉特异」可能本质如何的种种忧虑勾连了起来。我向她保证，我绝无半点要吓她的意思；恰恰相反，倒是她把我吓着了；我求她说个明白。可她脸色发白、浑身发抖地坐下，一言不发。不过片刻之后，她又站起身来，一边说着「据我所知，这事儿有时发生了，倒也不见得就跟着什么大祸」，一边动手收拾我用过的盆碟，仿佛要强自在我面前镇定下来。我再三央求她给个解释，却无济于事。她求我暂且按捺几天，说她此刻实在讲不了这桩故事。不过她主动应承：在我离家之前，她定会把所知的一切都告诉我。

===Original===
The next day a letter arrived announcing the death of a distant relation, through whose influence my father had had a lingering hope of obtaining an appointment for me. There was nothing left but to look out for a situation as tutor.

===Chinese===
次日，一封信送来，宣告了一位远房亲戚的死讯，而父亲原本一直指望借这位亲戚的门路为我谋个差事。眼下别无他法，唯有出去寻一份家庭教师（tutor）的职位了。

## III. My Old Nurse's Story / III. 我老保姆的故事

===Original===
I was now almost nineteen. I had completed the usual curriculum of study at one of the Scotch universities; and, possessed of a fair knowledge of mathematics and physics, and what I considered rather more than a good foundation for classical and metaphysical acquirement, I resolved to apply for the first suitable situation that offered. But I was spared the trouble. A certain Lord Hilton, an English nobleman, residing in one of the midland counties, having heard that one of my father’s sons was desirous of such a situation, wrote to him, offering me the post of tutor to his two boys, of the ages of ten and twelve. He had been partly educated at a Scotch university; and this, it may be, had prejudiced him in favour of a Scotch tutor; while an ancient alliance of the families by marriage was supposed by my nurse to be the reason of his offering me the situation. Of this connection, however, my father said nothing to me, and it went for nothing in my anticipations. I was to receive a hundred pounds a year, and to hold in the family the position of a gentleman, which might mean anything or nothing, according to the disposition of the heads of the family. Preparations for my departure were immediately commenced. I set out one evening for the cottage of my old nurse, to bid her goodbye for many months, probably years. I was to leave the next day for Edinburgh, on my way to London, whence I had to repair by coach to my new abode—almost to me like the land beyond the grave, so little did I know about it, and so wide was the separation between it and my home. The evening was sultry when I began my walk, and before I arrived at its end, the clouds rising from all quarters of the horizon, and especially gathering around the peaks of the mountain, betokened the near approach of a thunderstorm. This was a great delight to me. Gladly would I take leave of my home with the memory of a last night of tumultuous magnificence; followed, probably, by a day of weeping rain, well suited to the mood of my own heart in bidding farewell to the best of parents and the dearest of homes. Besides, in common with most Scotchmen who are young and hardy enough to be unable to realise the existence of coughs and rheumatic fevers, it was a positive pleasure to me to be out in rain, hail, or snow.

===Chinese===
此时我年将十九。我已在苏格兰一所大学修毕了寻常的课程；凭着尚算扎实的数学与物理学识，又自忖于古典之学与思辨之学，已打下胜过"良好基础"的根基，便决意去谋头一个合宜的差事。然而这番周折，竟有人替我省了。有一位希尔顿勋爵（Lord Hilton），英格兰人，贵族，居于中部诸郡（the midland counties），听闻我父亲有个儿子正想寻这样一份差事，便写信给他，许我去做他家两个男孩——一个十岁、一个十二岁——的家庭教师。他自己在苏格兰的大学里读过一阵书，说不定正因如此，才偏向于要一位苏格兰籍的先生；而我的老保姆则以为，他之所以把这份差事给我，乃因两家之间有一桩由婚姻结成的远年旧谊。关于这层瓜葛，父亲对我只字未提，我在憧憬前程时也全不曾把它放在心上。我每年将得一百镑薪俸，并在那人家中居于"绅士"之位——这话或意味着什么都有，或意味着什么都没有，全看这家主事人的脾性。动身的诸般准备即刻着手做起。一日傍晚，我动身往老保姆的小屋去，为的是与她作别——这一别恐要历经数月，甚或数年。次日我便要启程赴爱丁堡（Edinburgh），取道伦敦（London），再从那里乘驿车赶往新的居所——那地方在我心中，几乎如同坟茔彼岸之地，我于它几乎一无所知，它与我的家之间又隔着迢迢天涯。我起程时傍晚闷热，未至终点，四面八方已升起云来，在那座山的群峰之上聚得尤紧，分明预示着雷暴将至。这于我却是一桩大大的快事。我乐于带着这样一个夜晚的记忆告别故园——一夜狂乱壮丽的喧嚣——其后多半还会继之以一个雨泪滂沱的白昼，正合我这颗心向最好的双亲、最可爱的家园道别时的心境。况且，凡年轻壮健、根本体认不到咳嗽与风湿热为何物的苏格兰人，多以为冒雨、冲雹、踏雪而行乃是确确实实的乐事，我亦与众人无异。

===Original===
“I am come to bid you goodbye, Margaret; and to hear the story which you promised to tell me before I left home: I go tomorrow.”

“Do you go so soon, my darling? Well, it will be an awful night to tell it in; but, as I promised, I suppose I must.”

===Chinese===
"我是来与你告别的，玛格丽特（Margaret）；也是来听你答应过临行前要讲给我听的那个故事——我明日就走。"

"你这么快就要走吗，我的心肝？也罢，挑这么个可怖的夜晚来讲它，可既然我应承过你，想来是非讲不可了。"

===Original===
At the moment, two or three great drops of rain, the first of the storm, fell down the wide chimney, exploding in the clear turf-fire.

“Yes, indeed you must,” I replied.

===Chinese===
正说着，两三滴大雨点——这场暴风雨的头一阵——落进宽宽的烟囱里，噼啪炸开在那明亮的草皮火上。

"是呀，你确实得讲。"我答道。

===Original===
After a short pause, she commenced. Of course she spoke in Gaelic; and I translate from my recollection of the Gaelic; but rather from the impression left upon my mind, than from any recollection of the words. She drew her chair near the fire, which we had reason to fear would soon be put out by the falling rain, and began.

===Chinese===
略顿了顿，她便开了口。她自然说的是盖尔语（Gaelic）；我此处是由记忆中的盖尔语转译而来——但与其说是追忆原话，不如说是依着那番话在我心头留下的印象。她把椅子挪近炉火——那火眼看就要被飘进的雨浇灭了——便讲了起来。

===Original===
“How old the story is, I do not know. It has come down through many generations. My grandmother told it to me as I tell it to you; and her mother and my mother sat beside, never interrupting, but nodding their heads at every turn. Almost it ought to begin like the fairy tales, ‘Once upon a time,’—it took place so long ago; but it is too dreadful and too true to tell like a fairy tale.—There were two brothers, sons of the chief of our clan, but as different in appearance and disposition as two men could be. The elder was fair-haired and strong, much given to hunting and fishing; fighting too, upon occasion, I dare say, when they made a foray upon the Saxon, to get back a mouthful of their own. But he was gentleness itself to everyone about him, and the very soul of honour in all his doings. The younger was very dark in complexion, and tall and slender compared to his brother. He was very fond of book-learning, which, they say, was an uncommon taste in those times. He did not care for any sports or bodily exercises but one; and that, too, was unusual in these parts. It was horsemanship. He was a fierce rider, and as much at home in the saddle as in his study-chair. You may think that, so long ago, there was not much fit room for riding hereabouts; but, fit or not fit, he rode. From his reading and riding, the neighbours looked doubtfully upon him, and whispered about the black art. He usually bestrode a great powerful black horse, without a white hair on him; and people said it was either the devil himself, or a demon-horse from the devil’s own stud. What favoured this notion was, that, in or out of the stable, the brute would let no other than his master go near him. Indeed, no one would venture, after he had killed two men, and grievously maimed a third, tearing him with his teeth and hoofs like a wild beast. But to his master he was obedient as a hound, and would even tremble in his presence sometimes.

===Chinese===
"这故事有多古老，我也不晓得。它历经许多代人流传下来。我祖母怎样讲给我听，我便怎样讲给你听；她的母亲和我的母亲坐在一旁，从不插嘴，只在每一个转折处连连点头。它几乎该像童话那样起头——'从前'——因为那是太久太久以前的事了；可它又太可怖、太真实，不能当童话来讲。——从前有兄弟二人，是我们氏族首领的儿子，可二人的相貌与性情，竟有如天壤之别。兄长金发，体魄雄健，酷嗜狩猎与垂钓；遇着出征，我想也必不甘后人——那时候他们常去劫掠撒克逊人（the Saxon），好夺回一口本该属于自己的东西。然而他对身边众人，却又温存体贴至极，所行所为，无不以荣誉为立身之本。弟弟肤色黧黑，较之乃兄，身量高而单薄。他极好读书，据说在那个年代，这是少见的癖好。种种游猎武戏他一概不喜，唯独钟情一样；而那一样，在这些地方也是稀罕的——便是马术。他是个暴烈的骑手，在马鞍之上，犹如在书斋的座椅上一般安适。你或以为，在那么久远的年月，这附近并无多少宜于驰骋的去处；可是宜与不宜，他只管骑。因了他的好读书与好驰马，邻里看他都满腹狐疑，私底下嘀咕着什么邪术黑巫。他胯下常是一匹高大神骏的黑马，通体不见一根杂毛；人们便说，那要么就是魔鬼本尊，要么就是魔鬼自家马厩里牵出来的一匹妖马。助长这种说法的是：无论是在厩中还是在厩外，那畜生除了主人，谁也不肯挨近。说实话，在它踢死两人、又将第三人撕得血肉模糊——用牙用蹄如野兽一般——之后，再没人敢上前了。可它在主人面前，却驯顺得像条猎犬，有时甚至在他跟前瑟瑟发抖。

===Original===
“The youth’s temper corresponded to his habits. He was both gloomy and passionate. Prone to anger, he had never been known to forgive. Debarred from anything on which he had set his heart, he would have gone mad with longing if he had not gone mad with rage. His soul was like the night around us now, dark, and sultry, and silent, but lighted up by the red levin of wrath and torn by the bellowings of thunder-passion. He must have his will: hell might have his soul. Imagine, then, the rage and malice in his heart, when he suddenly became aware that an orphan girl, distantly related to them, who had lived with them for nearly two years, and whom he had loved for almost all that period, was loved by his elder brother, and loved him in return. He flung his right hand above his head, swore a terrible oath that if he might not, his brother should not, rushed out of the house, and galloped off among the hills.

===Chinese===
"这少年的性情，正与他的习性相副。他阴郁而又暴戾。动辄发怒，从无宽恕之名。凡是他心头所系之物，若被横加阻断，他若不因渴慕而狂，便要因暴怒而疯。他的灵魂就像此刻围裹着我们的夜——幽暗、闷热、死寂——却被怒火的红电照得通明，被雷鸣般的激情撕扯得支离破碎。他非达目的不可：哪怕将灵魂交予地狱。那么，你且想象他心头是何等的狂怒与怨毒——当他骤然发觉，一个与他们沾着一点远亲的孤女，在他们家中住了将近两年，而他也爱了她几乎整整两年，竟被他的兄长所爱，并且也爱他的兄长。他把右手往头顶一挥，发下一个可怖的毒誓：既然他得不到，他的兄弟也休想得到；随即冲出屋外，纵马驰入了群山之间。

===Original===
“The orphan was a beautiful girl, tall, pale, and slender, with plentiful dark hair, which, when released from the snood, rippled down below her knees. Her appearance formed a strong contrast with that of her favoured lover, while there was some resemblance between her and the younger brother. This fact seemed, to his fierce selfishness, ground for a prior claim.

===Chinese===
"那孤女生得极美，身量修长，肤色苍白，体态轻盈，一头浓密的黑发，一旦从发带中解放出来，便如波涛般垂至膝下。她的容止与她那位蒙她青睐的情人恰成强烈对照，而与那弟弟之间，倒有几分相似。这一层，在他那暴烈的私心看来，便是他先着人、先有主张的凭据。

===Original===
“It may appear strange that a man like him should not have had instant recourse to his superior and hidden knowledge, by means of which he might have got rid of his rival with far more of certainty and less of risk; but I presume that, for the moment, his passion overwhelmed his consciousness of skill. Yet I do not suppose that he foresaw the mode in which his hatred was about to operate. At the moment when he learned their mutual attachment, probably through a domestic, the lady was on her way to meet her lover as he returned from the day’s sport. The appointed place was on the edge of a deep, rocky ravine, down in whose dark bosom brawled and foamed a little mountain torrent. You know the place, Duncan, my dear, I dare say.”

(Here she gave me a minute description of the spot, with directions how to find it.)

===Chinese===
"像他这等人，竟没有立刻诉诸自己所通晓的那隐秘高超之学——借那手段，他本可更有把握、更少凶险地除去情敌——这听来或许奇怪；但我料想，那一刻他的激情盖过了他对自家伎俩的自觉。可我也并不以为，他当时便已预见到他的仇恨将以何种方式发作。在他得知二人两情相悦的那一刻——多半是从一个仆人口中得知的——那女子正动身去迎她打了一日野味归来的情人。二人相约之地，在一道幽深多石的岩壑边上，壑底幽暗的怀抱中，一条山涧奔腾着、翻起白沫。那地方，邓肯，我的心肝，我想你是晓得的。"

（此处她把这地点细细向我描绘了一番，又教我如何才能寻到它。）

===Original===
“Whether anyone saw what I am about to relate, or whether it was put together afterwards, I cannot tell. The story is like an old tree—so old that it has lost the marks of its growth. But this is how my grandmother told it to me.—An evil chance led him in the right direction. The lovers, startled by the sound of the approaching horse, parted in opposite directions along a narrow mountain-path on the edge of the ravine. Into this path he struck at a point near where the lovers had met, but to opposite sides of which they had now receded; so that he was between them on the path. Turning his horse up the course of the stream, he soon came in sight of his brother on the ledge before him. With a suppressed scream of rage, he rode headlong at him, and ere he had time to make the least defence, hurled him over the precipice. The helplessness of the strong man was uttered in one single despairing cry as he shot into the abyss. Then all was still. The sound of his fall could not reach the edge of the gulf. Divining in a moment that the lady, whose name was Elsie, must have fled in the opposite direction, he reined his steed on his haunches. He could touch the precipice with his bridle-hand half outstretched; his sword-hand half outstretched would have dropped a stone to the bottom of the ravine. There was no room to wheel. One desperate practicability alone remained. Turning his horse’s head towards the edge, he compelled him, by means of the powerful bit, to rear till he stood almost erect; and so, his body swaying over the gulf, with quivering and straining muscles, to turn on his hind-legs. Having completed the half-circle, he let him drop, and urged him furiously in the opposite direction. It must have been by the devil’s own care that he was able to continue his gallop along that ledge of rock.

===Chinese===
"我说下来这些，究竟有没有人亲眼瞧见，还是事后才被人拼凑出来的，我无从晓得。这故事就像一棵老树——老得连生长的痕迹都已磨灭。可我祖母便是这样讲给我听的。——一股邪缘引他走了正路。那对情人被渐近的马蹄声惊动，便沿着崖边一条狭窄的山径，各向相反的方向避去。他恰在二人相会之处附近扑上这条小径，而此时他们已退向径的两端；于是他便居于二人之间。他策马溯流而上，不多时便望见兄长在前方的岩架上。他压低一声狂怒的尖叫，纵马直冲过去，趁兄长来不及作丝毫抵挡，便把他掀下了悬崖。那壮士的无力回天，尽凝作一声绝望的呼号，随他一同坠入深渊。而后万籁俱寂。他坠落的声响，连崖边都到不了。他霎时便料定，那名叫埃尔西（Elsie）的女子，必已朝相反方向逃去，便猛地勒马，叫它坐回后蹄上。他勒缰的那只手微微伸出，几乎可触到悬崖；持剑的那只手若也微微一伸，便能将一块石头直落到壑底。根本没有回旋的余地。唯有一桩拼死可行之计尚存。他把马头朝向崖边，借着那副厉害的马衔铁逼它人立而起，几乎直立；而后，马身悬在深渊之上，筋肉颤栗绷紧，就凭两条后腿转了半个圈。半圈既成，他放它落下，发疯般地朝相反方向催它疾驰。他能沿着那道岩架继续飞奔，必是得了魔鬼亲自照看。

===Original===
“He soon caught sight of the maiden. She was leaning, half fainting, against the precipice. She had heard her lover’s last cry, and although it had conveyed no suggestion of his voice to her ear, she trembled from head to foot, and her limbs would bear her no farther. He checked his speed, rode gently up to her, lifted her unresisting, laid her across the shoulders of his horse, and, riding carefully till he reached a more open path, dashed again wildly along the mountainside. The lady’s long hair was shaken loose, and dropped trailing on the ground. The horse trampled upon it, and stumbled, half dragging her from the saddlebow. He caught her, lifted her up, and looked at her face. She was dead. I suppose he went mad. He laid her again across the saddle before him, and rode on, reckless whither. Horse, and man, and maiden were found the next day, lying at the foot of a cliff, dashed to pieces. It was observed that a hind-shoe of the horse was loose and broken. Whether this had been the cause of his fall, could not be told; but ever when he races, as race he will, till the day of doom, along that mountainside, his gallop is mingled with the clank of the loose and broken shoe. For, like the sin, the punishment is awful: he shall carry about for ages the phantom-body of the girl, knowing that her soul is away, sitting with the soul of his brother, down in the deep ravine, or scaling with him the topmost crags of the towering mountain-peaks. There are some who, from time to time, see the doomed man careering along the face of the mountain, with the lady hanging across the steed; and they say it always betokens a storm, such as this which is now raving around us.”

===Chinese===
"他很快便望见了那少女。她正半晕半醒地倚在悬崖上。她听见了情人最后那一声呼号，虽说那声音传到她耳中时已不似他的嗓音，她却从头到脚直打哆嗦，两条腿再也挪不动一步。他收住速度，缓缓策马近前，将她毫不挣扎地抱起，横搁在马的肩隆上，小心翼翼地骑行着，直到寻见一条较宽敞的路，便又疯狂地顺着山坡疾驰而去。那女子长长的头发散落下来，拖曳在地。马蹄踏住了那头发，一个趔趄，险些把她从鞍弓上扯下来。他一把接住她，将她托起，端详她的脸。她已经死了。我料他是发了疯。他又把她横搁在身前的鞍上，继续策马前行，全不顾奔向何方。第二天，人、马与少女俱被发现，横在一处崖脚之下，摔得粉身碎骨。有人留意到，马的一只后蹄铁已经松脱碎裂。这是否便是它坠马的根由，无从得知；可每逢他奔驰——而他必将奔驰，直到末日审判那日——顺着那道山坡，他的疾驰里总夹杂着那松脱碎裂的马蹄铁叮当作响之声。因为，与那罪愆一般无二，这刑罚亦是可怖：他要在悠悠百代里驮着那女子的幻身游荡，明知她的灵魂早已不在——而是与他的兄长同坐那深壑之底，又或与他一同攀上那摩天危峰的最险峻处。不时有人瞧见那受永罚之人策马掠过山面，那女子横悬于马背之上；人们说，这总预示着一场风暴——就像此刻在我们四周咆哮的这一场。"

===Original===
I had not noticed till now, so absorbed had I been in her tale, that the storm had risen to a very ecstasy of fury.

===Chinese===
直到此刻我才留意到——方才我太沉迷于她的故事——那暴风雨已猖獗到了如痴如狂的地步。

===Original===
“They say, likewise, that the lady’s hair is still growing; for, every time they see her, it is longer than before; and that now such is its length and the headlong speed of the horse, that it floats and streams out behind, like one of those curved clouds, like a comet’s tail, far up in the sky; only the cloud is white, and the hair dark as night. And they say it will go on growing till the Last Day, when the horse will falter and her hair will gather in; and the horse will fall, and the hair will twist, and twine, and wreathe itself like a mist of threads about him, and blind him to everything but her. Then the body will rise up within it, face to face with him, animated by a fiend, who, twining her arms around him, will drag him down to the bottomless pit.”

===Chinese===
"人们还说，那女子的头发至今仍在生长；因为每回他们瞧见她，那头发总比上回更长；又说如今那头发已长得——加上马那不顾一切的狂奔——竟飘飘荡荡曳在身后，宛如天际高处那些弯弯的云，宛如彗星的尾巴；只不过那云是白的，而那头发黑如暗夜。他们还说，那头发要一直长下去，直长到最后审判之日；那时马将踉跄失蹄，她的发也将收拢而来；马将倒下，那发将缠绕、纠结，如一缕游丝般的雾，将他层层裹住，叫他眼前除她之外什么都看不见。于是那幻身便要在发中直立起来，与他面面相对，为一个恶魔所凭附；那恶魔伸出双臂缠住他，要把他拖入无底深渊。"

===Original===
I may mention something which now occurred, and which had a strange effect on my old nurse. It illustrates the assertion that we see around us only what is within us: marvellous things enough will show themselves to the marvellous mood.—During a short lull in the storm, just as she had finished her story, we heard the sound of iron-shod hoofs approaching the cottage. There was no bridle-way into the glen. A knock came to the door, and, on opening it, we saw an old man seated on a horse, with a long slenderly-filled sack lying across the saddle before him. He said he had lost the path in the storm, and, seeing the light, had scrambled down to inquire his way. I perceived at once, from the scared and mysterious look of the old woman’s eyes, that she was persuaded that this appearance had more than a little to do with the awful rider, the terrific storm, and myself who had heard the sound of the phantom-hoofs. As he ascended the hill, she looked after him, with wide and pale but unshrinking eyes; then turning in, shut and locked the door behind her, as by a natural instinct. After two or three of her significant nods, accompanied by the compression of her lips, she said:—

===Chinese===
我且说一桩此时发生的事，它在我那老保姆身上产生了奇异的影响。这也正好印证了一句话——我们周遭所见，无非我们内心所有：心境奇异，便有足够的奇异之事向它显现。——就在她讲完故事之后，暴风雨短暂的歇息间，我们听见钉了铁掌的马蹄声朝小屋逼近。这条山谷原无马道可入。有人敲门，开了一看，只见一个老人骑在马上，身前的鞍子上横搁着一只细长、装得半满的口袋。他说他在风暴中迷失了路，望见这点灯火，便摸黑下来问个路。我登时便从老妇人那双又惊又秘的眼神里看出，她已认定：这番现身，与那可怖的骑手、与这场骇人的风暴、与我这听见过幽灵马蹄声的人，断非只有些许干系。他往山上去时，她目送着他，眼睛睁得老大、脸色惨白，却并不畏缩；随即转身进屋，顺手关上门、落了锁，仿佛出于一种本能。她意味深长地点了两三下头，又紧紧抿住嘴唇，这才开口道——

===Original===
“He need not think to take me in, wizard as he is, with his disguises. I can see him through them all. Duncan, my dear, when you suspect anything, do not be too incredulous. This human demon is of course a wizard still, and knows how to make himself, as well as anything he touches, take a quite different appearance from the real one; only every appearance must bear some resemblance, however distant, to the natural form. That man you saw at the door was the phantom of which I have been telling you. What he is after now, of course, I cannot tell; but you must keep a bold heart, and a firm and wary foot, as you go home tonight.”

===Chinese===
"他休想骗得过我——任他是个巫师，任他怎样乔装改扮。他那些障眼法，我件件都看得穿。邓肯，我的心肝，往后你心里犯疑的时候，可别太过不信邪。这人为魔，如今自然仍是个巫师，深谙怎样叫自己——又叫他所沾的一切——显出一副与真形全然两样的模样；只是无论哪副模样，总得与那本来的真形有几分相似，哪怕隔得极远。方才你在门口瞧见的那人，便是我适才对你讲的那幽灵。他如今图的是什么，我自然说不上来；可你今夜回家，务须把心放得胆大，把脚步放得又稳又警觉。"

===Original===
I showed some surprise, I do not doubt; and, perhaps, some fear as well; but I only said, “How do you know him, Margaret?”

“I can hardly tell you,” she replied; “but I do know him. I think he hates me. Often, of a wild night, when there is moonlight enough by fits, I see him tearing around this little valley, just on the top edge—all round; the lady’s hair and the horses mane and tail driving far behind, and mingling, vaporous, with the stormy clouds. About he goes, in wild careering gallop; now lost as the moon goes in, then visible far round when she looks out again—an airy, pale-grey spectre, which few eyes but mine could see; for, as far as I am aware, no one of the family but myself has ever possessed the double gift of seeing and hearing both. In this case I hear no sound, except now and then a clank from the broken shoe. But I did not mean to tell you that I had ever seen him. I am not a bit afraid of him. He cannot do more than he may. His power is limited; else ill enough would he work, the miscreant.”

===Chinese===
我不怀疑，自己面上露出了几分惊讶；或许也带着几分惧意；但我只说了一句："你是怎么认得他的，玛格丽特？"

"我几乎说不上来，"她答道，"可我确是认得他。我想他是恨我的。常常在风狂之夜，月光一阵阵时明时灭，我便瞧见他在这一带小山谷的顶沿——沿着边缘——团团飞驰；那女子的长发与马的鬃尾远远地曳在身后，飘飘茫茫，与那翻涌的阴云混作一处。他一圈一圈地跑，狂奔乱驰；月一入云，他便隐去，待月再探出头来，他远远地又在那一头现了身——一具空灵的、淡灰色的鬼影，除了我这双眼，少有人能看见；因为据我所知，这一族里，除了我之外，再无旁人兼有这视与听两般并赋的异禀。在这种情形下，我听不见什么声响，只偶尔听得那碎裂的蹄铁叮当一下。可我方才本不打算告诉你，我曾见过他。我一点也不怕他。他所能做的，超不过他所能够。他的力量是有限的；否则那恶徒不知要造出多少孽来。"

===Original===
“But,” said I, “what has all this, terrible as it is, to do with the fright you took at my telling you that I had heard the sound of the broken shoe? Surely you are not afraid of only a storm?”

“No, my boy; I fear no storm. But the fact is, that that sound is seldom heard, and never, as far as I know, by any of the blood of that wicked man, without betokening some ill to one of the family, and most probably to the one who hears it—but I am not quite sure about that. Only some evil it does portend, although a long time may elapse before it shows itself; and I have a hope it may mean someone else than you.”

===Chinese===
"可是，"我说，"这些事纵然可怖，究竟同你那回受的惊有什么相干？——我告诉你我听见了那碎裂马蹄铁的声响，你便大惊失色。你总不会是单单怕一场风暴吧？"

"不，我的孩子；我不怕什么风暴。可实情是：那声响少有响起之时，而据我所知，凡是那恶人之血脉里的人听见了它，从无一次不是为这一族中的某个人——且最可能就是听见它的那个人——预示着某种祸事；不过这一层我也拿不太准。只是它必有所预示，总归是某种祸事，纵然那祸事要过很久才显露出来；而我心里还存着一个指望，但愿它指的不是你。"

===Original===
“Do not wish that,” I replied. “I know no one better able to bear it than I am; and I hope, whatever it may be, that I only shall have to meet it. It must surely be something serious to be so foretold—it can hardly be connected with my disappointment in being compelled to be a pedagogue instead of a soldier.”

“Do not trouble yourself about that, Duncan,” replied she. “A soldier you must be. The same day you told me of the clank of the broken horseshoe, I saw you return wounded from battle, and fall fainting from your horse in the street of a great city—only fainting, thank God. But I have particular reasons for being uneasy at your hearing that boding sound. Can you tell me the day and hour of your birth?”

===Chinese===
"别那样想，"我答道，"我认得的人里，再没有谁比我更配当此任了；我只盼，无论那是什么，都由我一个人来承当便好。既能如此被预先告知，那必是桩要紧的事——总不至于同我那一桩失意有关吧：我竟被迫去做个教书匠，而不能从军。"

"这个你不必挂心，邓肯，"她答道，"你必是要做军人的。你把那碎裂马蹄铁的叮当声告诉我的同一天，我便瞧见你带着伤从战场上归来，在一条大街上从马上晕落下来——只是晕厥罢了，感谢上帝。可你听见这凶兆之声，我之所以格外不安，自有特别的缘故。你能告诉我，你是何年何月何日何时生的吗？"

===Original===
“No,” I replied. “It seems very odd when I think of it, but I really do not know even the day.”

“Nor anyone else; which is stranger still,” she answered.

“How does that happen, nurse?”

===Chinese===
"不能，"我答道，"想起来也真怪，可我竟连日子都不晓得。"

"旁人也不晓得；这就更奇了。"她答道。

"这是怎么说，保姆？"

===Original===
“We were in terrible anxiety about your mother at the time. So ill was she, after you were just born, in a strange, unaccountable way, that you lay almost neglected for more than an hour. In the very act of giving birth to you, she seemed to the rest around her to be out of her mind, so wildly did she talk; but I knew better. I knew that she was fighting some evil power; and what power it was, I knew full well; for twice, during her pains, I heard the click of the horseshoe. But no one could help her. After her delivery, she lay as if in a trance, neither dead, nor at rest, but as if frozen to ice, and conscious of it all the while. Once more I heard the terrible sound of iron; and, at the moment, your mother started from her trance, screaming, ‘My child! my child!’ We suddenly became aware that no one had attended to the child, and rushed to the place where he lay wrapped in a blanket. Uncovering him, we found him black in the face, and spotted with dark spots upon the throat. I thought he was dead; but, with great and almost hopeless pains, we succeeded in making him breathe, and he gradually recovered. But his mother continued dreadfully exhausted. It seemed as if she had spent her life for her child’s defence and birth. That was you, Duncan, my dear.

===Chinese===
"那时候，为你母亲我们正焦急得要命。你刚刚落生，她便病得古怪、来得蹊跷，没法说得清，于是你竟几乎被撇在一旁无人照看，足有一个多钟头。正当她生你之际，在旁人看来，她简直像失了心智，嘴里说的胡话癫狂得很；可我心里明白。我晓得她正在与某种邪力相搏；那是什么力量，我了然于心——因为在她的阵痛之中，我两回听见了那马蹄铁的咔嗒声。可谁也帮不了她。分娩之后，她躺在那里，如同入了定，既非已死，也并未安息，倒像被冻成了冰，且事事心里清楚。我又一回听见了那铁器的可怖声响；就在那一刻，你母亲从那入定之中惊起，尖叫着：'我的孩子！我的孩子！'我们这才猛然发觉，竟没人照看过这孩子，便一齐冲到他躺着、裹在一条毯子里的地方。揭开毯子一看，他满脸青紫，喉间点点黑斑。我以为他已经死了；可费尽九牛二虎之力，几乎不抱希望，我们总算叫他有了呼吸，他便渐渐缓了过来。但他母亲却仍然虚弱不堪。看那光景，仿佛她为了护住孩子、为了生下孩子，已把自己的性命一股脑儿用尽了。那便是你啊，邓肯，我的心肝。

===Original===
“I was in constant attendance upon her. About a week after your birth, as near as I can guess, just in the gloaming, I heard yet again the awful clank—only once. Nothing followed till about midnight. Your mother slept, and you lay asleep beside her. I sat by the bedside. A horror fell upon me suddenly, though I neither saw nor heard anything. Your mother started from her sleep with a cry, which sounded as if it came from far away, out of a dream, and did not belong to this world. My blood curdled with fear. She sat up in bed, with wide staring eyes and half-open rigid lips, and, feeble as she was, thrust her arms straight out before her with great force, her hands open and lifted up, with the palms outwards. The whole action was of one violently repelling another. She began to talk wildly as she had done before you were born, but, though I seemed to hear and understand it all at the time, I could not recall a word of it afterwards. It was as if I had listened to it when half asleep. I attempted to soothe her, putting my arms round her, but she seemed quite unconscious of my presence, and my arms seemed powerless upon the fixed muscles of hers. Not that I tried to constrain her, for I knew that a battle was going on of some kind or other, and my interference might do awful mischief. I only tried to comfort and encourage her. All the time, I was in a state of indescribable cold and suffering, whether more bodily or mental I could not tell. But at length I heard yet again the clank of the shoe A sudden peace seemed to fall upon my mind—or was it a warm, odorous wind that filled the room? Your mother dropped her arms, and turned feebly towards her baby. She saw that he slept a blessed sleep. She smiled like a glorified spirit, and fell back exhausted on the pillow. I went to the other side of the room to get a cordial. When I returned to the bedside, I saw at once that she was dead. Her face smiled still, with an expression of the uttermost bliss.”

===Chinese===
"我时刻守在她身边。约莫在你出生一周之后——我能记起的大概就是这个时辰——恰在暮色四合之时，我又听见了那可怖的叮当声——只响了一下。其后直到半夜，并无别的事。你母亲睡着了，你也在她身旁睡着。我坐在床边。一种恐惧忽然攫住了我，虽说我既没看见什么，也没听见什么。你母亲从睡中惊起，发出一声叫喊，那声音仿佛来自极远的地方，来自一场梦乡，不属于这个世界。我吓得血都凝住了。她在床上坐起来，双目圆睁、呆呆瞪视，双唇半开、僵直，明明虚弱至极，却以极大的力气把两臂笔直地伸到身前，双手张开、高举，掌心朝外。这整个动作，分明是一个人在拼命推开另一个。她又像你出生前那样说起胡话来，可尽管当时我似乎句句听得分明、字字了然，事后却一个字也记不起来。那情形，就仿佛我是在半睡半醒之间听见的。我试着去安抚她，伸出双臂去搂她，可她似乎全然不觉我在跟前，我的双臂落在她那僵直的筋肉上，也仿佛全无气力。我倒也并非想强行约束她，因为我晓得正有一场相搏在进行，不知是何种类，我贸然插手，怕要闯下大祸。我只是想给她一点安慰与鼓励。自始至终，我都处在一种难以言传的寒冷与苦楚之中，究竟是身受多些还是心受多些，我也说不上来。但终于，我又一次听见了那马蹄铁的叮当声。我的心里忽然落下一片安宁——抑或，是一股暖洋洋、带着香气的风盈满了屋子？你母亲垂下双臂，有气无力地转向她的婴孩。她看见他正睡着一觉安稳的酣睡。她笑了起来，如同一个得了荣耀的灵，随即力竭地倒回枕上。我走到屋子另一头去取一剂提神的药酒。等我回到床边，一眼便看出，她已经死了。她的脸上仍挂着笑，一副至福无极的神情。"

===Original===
Nurse ceased, trembling as overcome by the recollection; and I was too much moved and awed to speak. At length, resuming the conversation, she said: “You see it is no wonder, Duncan, my dear, if, after all this, I should find, when I wanted to fix the date of your birth, that I could not determine the day or the hour when it took place. All was confusion in my poor brain. But it was strange that no one else could, any more than I. One thing only I can tell you about it. As I carried you across the room to lay you down, for I assisted at your birth, I happened to look up to the window. Then I saw what I did not forget, although I did not think of it again till many days after—a bright star was shining on the very tip of the thin crescent moon.”

===Chinese===
老保姆住了口，浑身颤抖，仿佛被那回忆压倒了；而我亦感动、敬畏得太深，说不出话来。良久，她才重又开口，接着方才的话头说道："邓肯，我的心肝，你瞧，经历了这一切之后，到我想确定你出生的日子、却连它落在哪一日、哪一时也定不下来，这又何足为奇呢。我那可怜的脑子里，一团乱麻。可怪的是，旁人竟也同我一样，谁也定不准。只有一桩事，我可以告诉你。我那时正抱着你穿过屋子，要把你放下——你出生时便是我接的生——我无意间抬头朝窗子一望。于是我看见了那样东西，至今不曾忘怀，虽说事后许多天里我并未再想起它——一轮细细的弯月，那尖尖的月角上，正亮着一颗星。"

===Original===
“Oh, then,” said I, “it is possible to determine the day and the very hour when my birth took place.”

“See the good of book-learning!” replied she. “When you work it out, just let me know, my dear, that I may remember it.”

“That I will.”

===Chinese===
"啊，那么，"我说，"我这出生的日子和时辰，倒是可以推算得出来了。"

"你瞧读书的妙处吧！"她答道，"你算了出来，可要告诉我一声，我的心肝，好让我也记着。"

"我一定。"

===Original===
A silence of some moments followed. Margaret resumed:—

“I am afraid you will laugh at my foolish fancies, Duncan; but in thinking over all these things, as you may suppose I often do, lying awake in my lonely bed, the notion sometimes comes to me: What if my Duncan be the youth whom his wicked brother hurled into the ravine, come again in a new body, to live out his life on the earth, cut short by his brother’s hatred? If so, his persecution of you, and of your mother for your sake, is easy to understand. And if so, you will never be able to rest till you find your fere, wherever she may have been born on the face of the earth. For born she must be, long ere now, for you to find. I misdoubt me much, however, if you will find her without great conflict and suffering between, for the Powers of Darkness will be against you; though I have good hope that you will overcome at last. You must forgive the fancies of a foolish old woman, my dear.”

===Chinese===
沉默了片刻。玛格丽特又开口道：

"我怕你要笑我这老太婆的痴心妄想呢，邓肯；可把这些事翻来覆去地想——你也料得到，我独个儿醒躺在冷清清的床上时，常这样想——有时便浮起这样一个念头：万一我的邓肯，就是当年被他那恶毒的兄弟掷下深壑的那个少年，转世投胎，换了一副新肉身，来到这世上，要把被兄弟的仇恨生生截断的那一辈子活完呢？若当真如此，他这样苦苦缠着你，又为了你的缘故缠着你的母亲，便不难明白了。若当真如此，那么，除非你寻见你的命定伴侣（fere）——无论她生在天下哪个角落——你便永世不得安生。因为她必是早已降生在世，好等你去寻的。只是我满心忧虑，怕你寻她之前，少不得要历一场大冲突、大苦楚，因为黑暗的权势必与你为敌；不过我亦存着好的盼头，信你终能克胜。你得宽恕一个糊涂老太婆的这些痴念，我的心肝。"

===Original===
I will not try to describe the strange feelings, almost sensations, that arose in me while listening to these extraordinary utterances, lest it should be supposed I was ready to believe all that Margaret narrated or concluded. I could not help doubting her sanity; but no more could I help feeling very peculiarly moved by her narrative.

Few more words were spoken on either side, but after receiving renewed exhortations to carefulness on my way home, I said goodbye to dear old nurse, considerably comforted, I must confess, that I was not doomed to be a tutor all my days; for I never questioned the truth of that vision and its consequent prophecy.

===Chinese===
听着这些异乎寻常的话，我心中涌起的那种种奇异感受——几乎可说是种种感觉——我不愿费力去描摹，免得有人以为我已轻信了玛格丽特所讲述、所推断的一切。我不禁怀疑她神志是否清明；可我也同样不禁为她的讲述所深深触动，生出一种格外异样的心绪。

彼此都没再多说什么；但在我又领受了一番路上务须小心的再三叮咛之后，我便向亲爱的老保姆道了别。我得承认，心里着实宽慰了不少——我总算不至于注定要当一辈子教书先生了；因为我从不曾怀疑那异象及其随之而来的预言是真。

===Original===
I went out into the midst of the storm, into the alternating throbs of blackness and radiance; now the possessor of no more room than what my body filled, and now isolated in worldwide space. And the thunder seemed to follow me, bellowing after me as I went.

===Chinese===
我走入风暴正中，走入那黑与光一阵阵交替的悸动之中；时而除了我这具身子所占的方寸之地，再无立足之处；时而又孤零零地抛在天地之间、无垠的空旷里。而那雷声仿佛紧追着我，一路在我身后轰然咆哮。

===Original===
Absorbed in the story I had heard, I took my way, as I thought, homewards. The whole country was well known to me. I should have said, before that night, that I could have gone home blindfold. Whether the lightning bewildered me and made me take a false turn, I cannot tell; for the hardest thing to understand, in intellectual as well as moral mistakes, is—how we came to go wrong. But after wandering for some time, plunged in meditation, and with no warning whatever of the presence of inimical powers, a brilliant lightning-flash showed me that at least I was not near home. The light was prolonged for a second or two by a slight electric pulsation; and by that I distinguished a wide space of blackness on the ground in front of me. Once more wrapped in the folds of a thick darkness, I dared not move. Suddenly it occurred to me what the blackness was, and whither I had wandered. It was a huge quarry, of great depth, long disused, and half filled with water. I knew the place perfectly. A few more steps would have carried me over the brink. I stood still, waiting for the next flash, that I might be quite sure of the way I was about to take before I ventured to move. While I stood, I fancied I heard a single hollow plunge in the black water far below. When the lightning came, I turned, and took my path in another direction.

===Chinese===
我一面沉浸在那刚刚听来的故事里，一面自以为正踏向归途。这一带乡野我再熟不过。若是换在那一夜之前，我本会说，便是蒙着眼我也走得回家。究竟是不是那闪电叫我花了眼、转错了弯，我说不上来；因为无论智识上的还是德行上的过错，最难明白的，便是——我们究竟怎样走错了那一步。可我冥想着胡乱走了一阵，全没有半点预兆示警说有敌对的力量在近旁，忽然一道耀眼的电光使我看见：我至少并不在家附近。那电光被一阵微弱的电流搏动延续了一两秒；借着这点光，我辨出前方地面上有一大片黑黝黝的空处。重又被浓稠的黑暗裹住，我动也不敢动。我忽然想起那片黑是什么，又想起自己走到了何处。那是一座极大的采石场，深得很，久已废弃，半积着水。这地方我再熟不过。再多走几步，我便要越过它的边沿了。我站定不动，等着下一道电光，好把将要迈出的路看个十分分明，再敢挪步。正站着，我恍惚听见下方远远的黑水之中，孤零零地"扑通"一响。等电光亮起，我便转过身，朝另一个方向寻路而去。

===Original===
After walking for some time across the heath, I fell. The fall became a roll, and down a steep declivity I went, over and over, arriving at the bottom uninjured.

Another flash soon showed me where I was—in the hollow valley, within a couple of hundred yards from nurse’s cottage. I made my way towards it. There was no light in it, except the feeblest glow from the embers of her peat fire. “She is in bed,” I said to myself, “and I will not disturb her.” Yet something drew me towards the little window. I looked in. At first I could see nothing. At length, as I kept gazing, I saw something, indistinct in the darkness, like an outstretched human form.

===Chinese===
在荒原上走了一阵，我跌了一跤。这一跌化作一个翻滚，我便顺着一段陡坡骨碌碌地滚了下去，到了坡底，竟毫发无伤。

很快又一道电光使我看见自己身在何处——在这凹下去的山谷里，离保姆的小屋不出两百码。我朝它走去。屋里没有一点灯火，只有泥炭火的余烬透出最微弱的一丝红光。"她已经睡了，"我自语道，"我不去搅扰她。"然而有一样东西把我引向那扇小窗。我朝里张望。起初我什么也看不见。终于，在我凝望不止之间，我看见了一样东西，在黑暗里影影绰绰，像一具摊开了的人形。

===Original===
By this time the storm had lulled. The moon had been up for some time, but had been quite concealed by tempestuous clouds. Now, however, these had begun to break up; and, while I stood looking into the cottage, they scattered away from the face of the moon, and a faint vapoury gleam of her light, entering the cottage through a window opposite that at which I stood, fell directly on the face of my old nurse, as she lay on her back, outstretched upon chairs, pale as death, and with her eyes closed. The light fell nowhere but on her face. A stranger to her habits would have thought she was dead; but she had so much of the appearance she had had on a former occasion, that I concluded at once she was in one of her trances. But having often heard that persons in such a condition ought not to be disturbed, and feeling quite sure she knew best how to manage herself, I turned, though reluctantly, and left the lone cottage behind me in the night, with the deathlike woman lying motionless in the midst of it.

===Chinese===
此时风暴已经平息。月升已有一阵，却一直被翻涌的阴云遮得严严实实。可此刻这些云开始四散开来；正当我站在那里朝屋里张望时，它们从月亮的面上纷纷退去，一缕淡淡的、如雾如烟的月光，从我站着那扇窗的对面一扇窗子照进小屋，正正落在老保姆的脸上——她仰面躺着，摊开在几把椅子上，脸色白如死人，双目紧闭。那光只落在她脸上，别处一无所及。不熟她习性的人，定会以为她已经死了；可她这副模样，与从前某一次她所呈现的模样太相像了，于是我立刻断定，她又是陷入了那种入定之中。但我常听人说，处于这种状况的人不宜受惊扰，又笃信她自己最懂得怎样调理，便虽不情愿，还是转过身，把那座孤零零的小屋留在身后的夜色里——那个死一般的女人，一动不动地躺在它的正中央。

===Original===
I found my way home without any further difficulty, and went to bed, where I soon fell asleep, thoroughly wearied, more by the mental excitement I had been experiencing than by the amount of bodily exercise I had gone through.

My sleep was tormented with awful dreams; yet, strange to say, I awoke in the morning refreshed and fearless. The sun was shining through the chinks in my shutters, which had been closed because of the storm, and was making streaks and bands of golden brilliancy upon the wall. I had dressed and completed my preparations long before I heard the steps of the servant who came to call me.

===Chinese===
我没再费什么周折便寻路回了家，上床之后，不一会儿便沉沉睡去——我已是疲惫到了极点，可这份累，与其说来自我所经受的那番体力奔走，不如说来自我所经历的那番心神激荡。

这一夜的睡眠被可怖的梦境苦苦搅扰；然而奇怪的是，早晨醒来，我竟精神焕发，了无惧意。阳光从因防暴风而关得严严的百叶窗缝隙里透进来，在墙上投下一道一道、一条一条灿烂的金光。来叫我起床的仆人的脚步声尚未响起，我早已穿戴齐整、把行装都打理停当了。

===Original===
What a wonderful thing waking is! The time of the ghostly moonshine passes by, and the great positive sunlight comes. A man who dreams, and knows that he is dreaming, thinks he knows what waking is; but knows it so little, that he mistakes, one after another, many a vague and dim change in his dream for an awaking. When the true waking comes at last, he is filled and overflowed with the power of its reality. So, likewise, one who, in the darkness, lies waiting for the light about to be struck, and trying to conceive, with all the force of his imagination, what the light will be like, is yet, when the reality flames up before him, seized as by a new and unexpected thing, different from and beyond all his imagining. He feels as if the darkness were cast to an infinite distance behind him. So shall it be with us when we wake from this dream of life into the truer life beyond, and find all our present notions of being, thrown back as into a dim, vapoury region of dreamland, where yet we thought we knew, and whence we looked forward into the present. This must be what Novalis means when he says: “Our life is not a dream; but it may become a dream, and perhaps ought to become one.”

===Chinese===
醒来，这是何等奇妙的一件事！那鬼魅般的月光时分过去了，那确凿宏大的日光来临了。一个做梦的人，倘若知道自己在做梦，便自以为懂得何谓醒来；可他其实懂得极少，竟把梦里那一个个模糊朦胧的变迁，一个接一个地错当成觉醒。待真正的醒来终于降临，他便被那现实的力量所充满、所漫溢。同样，一个人在黑暗里躺着，等候那即将被点亮的光，竭尽想象之力去揣想那光将是什么模样——可当那真实的光焰在他面前腾起，他仍被攫住，仿佛被一件崭新而意外的事物所攫住，与他的一切揣想都不同、都更远。他只觉得，仿佛那黑暗被抛到了身后无穷无尽的地方。及至我们从这个名为人生的梦里醒来，进入彼岸那更为真实的人生，发觉我们此刻所有关于"存在"的种种念头，都像被抛回了一片朦胧、如雾的梦土——而在那片梦土上，我们当初还自以为知晓，还从那里朝现在的光景翘首盼望——情形也必如此。这想必便是诺瓦利斯（Novalis）那句话的意思："我们的生活不是一场梦；但它可能成为一场梦，或许也本该成为一场梦。"

===Original===
And so I looked back upon the strange history of my past; sometimes asking myself—“Can it be that all this realty happened to the same *me*, who am now thinking about it in doubt and wonder?”

===Chinese===
我便这样回望我往昔那段离奇的历程；有时不免自问——"这一切真真切切的往事，难道当真都发生在同一个'我'身上吗？——就是此刻正满心疑讶地思量着它们的这个'我'？"

## IV. Hilton Hall / IV. 希尔顿庄园

===Original===
As my father accompanied me to the door, where the gig, which was to carry me over the first stage of my journey, was in waiting, a large target of hide, well studded with brass nails, which had hung in the hall for time unknown﻿—to me, at least﻿—fell on the floor with a dull bang. My father started, but said nothing; and, as it seemed to me, rather pressed my departure than otherwise. I would have replaced the old piece of armour before I went, but he would not allow me to touch it, saying, with a grim smile﻿—

“Take that for an omen, my boy, that your armour must be worn over the conscience, and not over the body. Be a man, Duncan, my boy. Fear nothing, and do your duty.”

===Chinese===
父亲陪着我走到门口，那辆要载我走过旅程头一段路的轻便马车已候在那里。就在此时，一面宽大的兽皮圆盾——其上密密钉着黄铜圆钉，在大厅里已挂了不知几多年头，至少在我是不知其始——“咚”的一声闷响，落到了地上。父亲猛地一惊，却什么也没说；而且在我看来，他倒像是更急着催我动身。我本想临行前把这件老甲胄重新挂回去，他却不许我碰它，只带着一丝冷峻的笑说道——

“孩子，便把这事当作一个预兆吧：你的甲胄，当穿在良心之上，不可穿在身体之上。要做个顶天立地的男子汉，邓肯，我的孩子。什么都不必怕，尽你的本分就是。”

===Original===
A grasp of the hand was all the goodbye I could make; and I was soon rattling away to meet the coach for Edinburgh and London. Seated on the top, I was soon buried in a reverie, from which I was suddenly startled by the sound of tinkling iron. Could it be that my adversary was riding unseen alongside of the coach? Was that the clank of the ominous shoe? But I soon discovered the cause of the sound, and laughed at my own apprehensiveness. For I observed that the sound was repeated every time that we passed any trees by the wayside, and that it was the peculiar echo they gave of the loose chain and steel work about the harness. The sound was quite different from that thrown back by the houses on the road. I became perfectly familiar with it before the day was over.

I reached London in safety, and slept at the house of an old friend of my father, who treated me with great kindness, and seemed altogether to take a liking to me. Before I left he held out a hope of being able, some day or other, to procure for me what I so much desired﻿—a commission in the army.

===Chinese===
紧握了一握，这便是我所能道出的全部道别；不多时我便哐啥上路，去赶那班驶往爱丁堡与伦敦的驿车。我坐在车顶，不多久便沉入一阵遐想，忽被一阵铁器的叮当声所惊起。莫非我那对头正隐着身形，与驿车并辔而行？莫非那是那不祥马蹄铁的叮当声？但我很快便弄清了那声响的来历，不禁自笑这般疑神疑鬼。因为我留意到，每逢车行经过路旁的树木，那声响便复响一回；原来那不过是松垂的链条与马具上的钢铁件，在树间所激起的一种独特的回声。这与路旁房屋抛回的回声截然不同。未等这一日过完，我已对它再熟不过。

我平安抵达伦敦，在父亲一位老友的宅中歇宿。这位老友待我极为厚道，且似乎整体上对我颇生好感。临行之前，他给我留下了一线指望：说不定哪一天，他能为我谋得我那样渴盼的东西——军中的一纸委任状。

===Original===
After spending a day or two with him, and seeing something of London, I climbed once more on the roof of a coach; and, late in the afternoon, was set down at the great gate of Hilton Hall. I walked up the broad avenue, through the final arch of which, as through a huge Gothic window, I saw the hall in the distance. Everything about me looked strange, rich, and lovely. Accustomed to the scanty flowers and diminutive wood of my own country, what I now saw gave me a feeling of majestic plenty, which I can recall at will, but which I have never experienced again. Behind the trees which formed the avenue, I saw a shrubbery, composed entirely of flowering plants, almost all unknown to me. Issuing from the avenue, I found myself amid open, wide, lawny spaces, in which the flowerbeds lay like islands of colour. A statue on a pedestal, the only white thing in the surrounding green, caught my eye. I had seen scarcely any sculpture; and this, attracting my attention by a favourite contrast of colour, retained it by its own beauty. It was a Dryad, or some nymph of the woods, who had just glided from the solitude of the trees behind, and had sprung upon the pedestal to look wonderingly around her. A few large brown leaves lay at her feet, borne thither by some eddying wind from the trees behind. As I gazed, filled with a new pleasure, a drop of rain upon my face made me look up. From a grey, fleecy cloud, with sun-whitened border, a light, gracious, plentiful rain was falling. A rainbow sprang across the sky, and the statue stood within the rainbow. At the same moment, from the base of the pedestal rose a figure in white, graceful as the Dryad above, and neither running, nor appearing to walk quickly, yet fleet as a ghost, glided past me at a few paces, distance, and, keeping in a straight line for the main entrance of the hall, entered by it and vanished.

===Chinese===
与他盘桓了一两日，又略略观览了一番伦敦，我再次攀上驿车的车顶；及至将近黄昏时分，我在希尔顿庄园（Hilton Hall）的大门前下了车。我沿那条宽阔的林荫道前行，穿过最后一道拱门——那拱门宛如一扇巨大的哥特式窗——便远远望见了那座宅邸。我四下里所见的一切，都显得陌生、丰饶而可爱。看惯了故土那般稀疏零落的花、那般矮小的林地，此刻眼前所见，给了我一种堂皇丰足之感——此感我至今能随心唤回，却再不曾重新体味过。在构成林荫道的那两排树后面，我看见一片灌木丛，全由花木组成，其中几乎无一是我所识得。走出林荫道，我发觉自己置身于一片开阔、宽敞、草坪般的空地之中，其间花床错落，宛如一座座色彩的岛屿。一座立在台座上的雕像——周遭碧绿中唯一一点白——攫住了我的目光。我从前几乎不曾见过什么雕塑；这一座，先是借一种我所偏爱的色调反差引我注目，复又凭它自身之美把我留住。那是一尊德律阿得斯（Dryad），或说是某位林中仙女，方才从身后树丛的幽寂里溜将出来，跃上台座，正惊奇地四下打量。她足下散落着几片棕色的大叶，是被身后某阵回旋的风自树间卷来的。我正满怀一种崭新的欣悦凝望着，一滴雨落在我脸上，引我抬头。一朵灰白如絮的云，云边被日头照得发白，正落下一阵轻柔、温婉、丰沛的雨。一道彩虹跃过天际，那雕像便立在彩虹之中。也就在同一刻，台座底部升起一道白色的身影，与头顶那尊德律阿得斯一般曼妙，既不似奔跑，也不似疾走，却快如鬼魅，在我数步开外一掠而过，径直朝宅邸正门奔去，入门而没。

===Original===
I followed in the direction of the mansion, which was large, and of several styles and ages. One wing appeared especially ancient. It was neglected and out of repair, and had in consequence a desolate, almost sepulchral look, an expression heightened by the number of large cypresses which grew along its line. I went up to the central door and knocked. It was opened by a grave, elderly butler. I passed under its flat arch, as if into the midst of the waiting events of my story. For, as I glanced around the hall, my consciousness was suddenly saturated, if I may be allowed the expression, with the strange feeling﻿—known to everyone, and yet so strange﻿—that I had seen it before; that, in fact, I knew it perfectly. But what was yet more strange, and far more uncommon, was, that, although the feeling with regard to the hall faded and vanished instantly, and although I could not in the least surmise the appearance of any of the regions into which I was about to be ushered, I yet followed the butler with a kind of indefinable expectation of seeing something which I had seen before; and every room or passage in that mansion affected me, on entering it for the first time, with the same sensation of previous acquaintance which I had experienced with regard to the hall. This sensation, in every case, died away at once, leaving that portion such as it might be expected to look to one who had never before entered the place.

===Chinese===
我朝那座宅邸的方向走去。宅子很大，兼具几个时代、几种风格的面貌。其中一翼显得格外古旧。它无人照管、年久失修，因而一副荒凉、几乎阴森如墓的神气；沿它一线所植的众多高大柏树，更把这神情衬得浓重。我走到正门前叩了叩门。来开门的是一位神情庄重的年长男管家。我从那道平拱下走过，仿佛走进了我这段故事里那正等候上演的种种事件的正中。因为，就在我环顾大厅之际，我的意识忽然被一种奇异之感所浸透——容我姑且这样形容——那感觉人人都曾体味，却又如此离奇：我仿佛从前见过它；事实上，我对它了如指掌。然而更离奇、也远为少见的却是：尽管关于大厅的这种感觉顷刻便淡去、消散，尽管我对于即将被引见的任何一个去处将是什么模样全然无从揣想，我仍带着一种说不清道不明的期待，随在男管家身后，仿佛预着自己又要见到什么曾见过的东西；而那宅子里每一间屋、每一条廊，头一遭踏入时，都给了我同样的“似曾相识”之感——正如我在大厅所经历的那般。而这感觉，回回都是一现即隐，把那一处地方，还作一个从未踏足其中者所能料想的那般模样。

===Original===
I was received by the housekeeper, a little, prim, benevolent old lady, with colourless face and antique headdress, who led me to the room prepared for me. To my surprise, I found a large wood-fire burning on the hearth; but the feeling of the place revealed at once the necessity for it; and I scarcely needed to be informed that the room, which was upon the ground floor, and looked out upon a little solitary grass-grown and ivy-mantled court, had not been used for years, and therefore required to be thus prepared for an inmate. My bedroom was a few paces down a passage to the right.

===Chinese===
接待我的是女管家——一位矮小、拘谨整洁、面善心慈的老太太，面色寡淡，头饰古旧——她把我领到为我备好的那间屋子。出乎我意料，我看见壁炉里正燃着一炉旺旺的柴火；然而这屋子给人的感觉，当即就显出了生这炉火的必要；几乎不待旁人相告，我也猜得到：这间位于底层的屋子，窗对着一个冷清的、长满荒草、爬满常春藤的小庭院，已多年无人居住，所以须得如此一番布置，方能住进人来。我的卧房就在右手边一条走廊下去几步路的地方。

===Original===
Left alone, I proceeded to make a more critical survey of my room. Its look of ancient mystery was to me incomparably more attractive than any show of elegance or comfort could have been. It was large and low, panelled throughout in oak, black with age, and worm-eaten in many parts﻿—otherwise entire. Both the windows looked into the little court or yard before mentioned. All the heavier furniture of the room was likewise of black oak, but the chairs and couches were covered with faded tapestry and tarnished gilding, apparently the superannuated members of the general household of seats. I could give an individual description of each, for every atom in that room, large enough for discernable shape or colour, seems branded into my brain. If I happen to have the least feverishness on me, the moment I fall asleep, I am in that room.

===Chinese===
剩下我独自一人，我便着手把这间屋子更细细地打量一番。它那古老而幽秘的神气，于我而言，比任何雅致或舒适的排场都更令我倾心——简直无可比拟。屋子又大又低，四壁通体镶着橡木护板，年深日久已呈黑色，许多地方更被虫蛀——除此之外，仍算完好。两扇窗都对着前述的那个小院或庭院。屋里所有较沉重的家具，也同样都是黑橡木所制；然而椅子和长榻上蒙着的，却是褪了色的织锦与失了光的描金，看去显然是全宅众座椅具里那批“告老退休”的成员。我大可对它们逐一细描，因为那间屋子里每一个大到足以辨清形色的小物件，仿佛都烙进了我的脑中。只要我身上略起半点烧热，一合眼入睡，我便又回到了那间屋子里。

## V. Lady Alice / V. 爱丽丝小姐

===Original===
When the bell rang for dinner, I managed to find my way to the drawing-room, where were assembled Lady Hilton, her only daughter, a girl of about thirteen, and the two boys, my pupils. Lady Hilton would have been pleasant, could she have been as natural as she wished to appear. She received me with some degree of kindness; but the half-cordiality of her manner towards me was evidently founded on the impassableness of the gulf between us. I knew at once that we should never be friends; that she would never come down from the lofty tableland upon which she walked; and that if, after being years in the house, I should happen to be dying, she would send the housekeeper to me. All right, no doubt; I only say that it was so. She introduced to me my pupils; fine, open-eyed, manly English boys, with something a little overbearing in their manner, which speedily disappeared in relation to me. Lord Hilton was not at home. Lady Hilton led the way to the dining-room; the elder boy gave his arm to his sister, and I was about to follow with the younger, when from one of the deep bay windows glided out, still in white, the same figure which had passed me upon the lawn. I started, and drew back. With a slight bow, she preceded me, and followed the others down the great staircase. Seated at table, I had leisure to make my observations upon them all; but most of my glances found their way to the lady who, twice that day, had affected me like an apparition. What is time, but the airy ocean in which ghosts come and go!

===Chinese===
晚饭铃响时，我好不容易摸到了客厅，那里已经聚着希尔顿夫人（Lady Hilton）、她唯一的女儿——一个约莫十三岁的姑娘——还有那两个男孩，也就是我的学生。希尔顿夫人本可以是个令人愉快的人，只要她能像她所希望展现的那般自然。她带着几分和气接待了我；但她对我那种半热不冷的态度，分明是建立在我们之间那道不可逾越的鸿沟之上。我当即就明白：我们永远成不了朋友；她绝不会从她所行走的那片高高在上的台地上走下来；即便我在这宅子里住了许多年，有一日濒临死亡，她也只会把女管家打发到我床前。这自然无可厚非；我只不过如实说罢了。她把我的学生引见给我：那是两个清秀、眼神明亮、颇有男子气的英格兰男孩，举止间带着些许盛气凌人，不过在我面前这股气焰很快便消退了。希尔顿勋爵（Lord Hilton）不在家。希尔顿夫人领头往餐厅走去；大一点的男孩把胳膊伸给他的妹妹，我正要带着小的那个跟上去，这时，从一扇深深的凸窗里飘出一个身影——仍是一身白衣——正是白天在草坪上从我身旁经过的那个人。我一惊，不由得退后一步。她微微颔首，走在我前面，跟着其余的人下了那道大楼梯。入席之后，我有闲暇细细打量他们每一个人；但我的目光，大半都不由自主地落到了那位小姐身上——那一天里，她已两度像幽灵一般撼动了我。时间究竟是什么，不正是鬼魅们来来去去的那片空灵的海洋吗！

===Original===
She was about twenty years of age; rather above the middle height, and rather slight in form; her complexion white rather than pale, her face being only less white than the deep marbly whiteness of her arms. Her eyes were large, and full of liquid night﻿—a night throbbing with the light of invisible stars. Her hair seemed raven-black, and in quantity profuse. The expression of her face, however, generally partook more of vagueness than any other characteristic. Lady Hilton called her Lady Alice; and she never addressed Lady Hilton but in the same ceremonious style.

I afterwards learned from the old housekeeper, that Lady Alice’s position in the family was a very peculiar one. Distantly connected with Lord Hilton’s family on the mother’s side, she was the daughter of the late Lord Glendarroch, and stepdaughter to Lady Hilton, who had become Lady Hilton within a year after Lord Glendarroch’s death. Lady Alice, then quite a child, had accompanied her stepmother, to whom she was moderately attached, and who had been allowed to retain undisputed possession of her. She had no near relatives, else the fortune I afterwards found to be at her disposal would have aroused contending claims to the right of guardianship.

===Chinese===
她约莫二十岁的年纪；身量略高于中等，体态颇为纤细；她的肤色与其说是苍白，不如说是白皙，而她面庞的白，只比她双臂那种深沉如大理石般的白略逊一筹。她的眼睛很大，盛满了流动的夜——那是一个搏动着无数看不见的星光之夜。她的头发看去乌黑如鸦，发量极丰。然而她脸上的神情，通常更多地带着一种恍惚迷离，胜过任何别的特质。希尔顿夫人唤她爱丽丝小姐（Lady Alice）；而她称呼希尔顿夫人，也总是用同一种客套郑重的口气。

后来我从那位老女管家口中得知，爱丽丝小姐在这个家里的地位十分特殊。她母系一支与希尔顿勋爵家有些远亲关系，是已故格兰达洛赫勋爵（Lord Glendarroch）的女儿，也是希尔顿夫人的继女——希尔顿夫人在格兰达洛赫勋爵去世后不到一年，便成了希尔顿夫人。爱丽丝小姐当时还是个小小的孩童，随她的继母一同过来；她对继母算是有几分依恋，而继母也被获准无可争议地保有对她的抚养。她再没有更近的亲属，否则，我后来发现归她支配的那笔财产，必会引出争夺监护权的种种诉求。

===Original===
Although she was in many respects kindly treated by her stepmother, certain peculiarities tended to her isolation from the family pursuits and pleasures. Lady Alice had no accomplishments. She could neither spell her own language, nor even read it aloud. Yet she delighted in reading to herself, though, for the most part, books which Mrs. Wilson characterised as very odd. Her voice, when she spoke, had a quite indescribable music in it; yet she neither sang nor played. Her habitual motion was more like a rhythmical gliding than an ordinary walk, yet she could not dance. Mrs. Wilson hinted at other and more serious peculiarities, which she either could not, or would not describe; always shaking her head gravely and sadly, and becoming quite silent, when I pressed for further explanation; so that, at last, I gave up all attempts to arrive at an understanding of the mystery by her means. Not the less, however, I speculated on the subject.

One thing soon became evident to me: that she was considered not merely deficient as to the power of intellectual acquirement, but in a quite abnormal intellectual condition. Of this, however, I could myself see no sign. The peculiarity, almost oddity, of some of her remarks, was evidently not only misunderstood, but, with relation to her mental state, misinterpreted. Such remarks Lady Hilton generally answered only by an elongation of the lips intended to represent a smile. To me, they appeared to indicate a nature closely allied to genius, if not identical with it﻿—a power of regarding things from an original point of view, which perhaps was the more unfettered in its operation from the fact that she was incapable of looking at them in the ordinary commonplace way. It seemed to me, sometimes, as if her point of observation was outside of the sphere within which the thing observed took place; and as if what she said, had a relation, occasionally, to things and thoughts and mental conditions familiar to her, but at which not even a definite guess could be made by me. I am compelled to acknowledge, however, that with such utterances as these mingled now and then others, silly enough for any drawing-room young lady; which seemed again to be accepted by the family as proofs that she was not *altogether* out of her right mind. She was gentle and kind to the children, as they were still called; and they seemed reasonably fond of her.

===Chinese===
尽管她继母在许多方面待她不薄，但若干特殊的禀性，却使她与一家人日常的消遣和乐事隔绝开来。爱丽丝小姐没有任何才艺。她既不能正确拼写自己的母语，甚至也不能把它大声朗读出来。然而她却酷爱默读，尽管读的多半是些被威尔逊太太（Mrs. Wilson）称作“古怪得很”的书。她开口说话时，声音里有一种难以形容的乐感；可她既不唱歌，也不弹奏。她日常的步态，与其说是寻常的行走，不如说更像一种合乎节奏的飘移，可她却不会跳舞。威尔逊太太还暗示了另一些更为要紧的怪异之处，却要么不能、要么不肯明说；每当我追问得更紧，她总是神情凝重而哀伤地摇着头，继而缄口不言；于是我最终放弃了借她之口探明这桩谜团的全部努力。然而，我对这件事的揣测，却并未因此稍减。

很快有一件事在我看来便了然了：在众人眼中，她不仅是心智学习能力有所欠缺，而且处于一种颇不正常的心智状态。然而这一点，我自己却看不出任何迹象。她有些话语所带的那种特殊、近乎古怪的意味，显然不仅被人误解，而且就她的精神状态而言，被作了错误的解读。这类话希尔顿夫人通常只用一个拉长嘴唇、意在表示微笑的动作来作答。在我看来，它们所指涉的，是一种与天才若合符节、乃至本质同一的天性——一种从独创的视角去观照事物的能力，而这能力之所以运作得如此无拘无束，或许正因为她是无法以寻常平庸的方式去看待事物的。有时我觉得，仿佛她的观察之点，正落在那被观察事物发生所在的领域之外；又仿佛她所说的，偶尔与她所熟稔的某些事物、念头和精神状态相关涉，而对这些，我连一个确切的猜测也难以做出。然而我不能不承认，在这样的言谈之间，时而也夹杂着另一些话，其浅薄足以媲美任何客厅里的娇小姐；而这些话，似乎又被这家人当作她神志并非*完全*失常的证据。她对那些孩子们——人们仍旧这样称呼他们——温婉而和善；而他们也似乎颇有几分喜欢她。

===Original===
There was something to me exceedingly touching in the solitariness of this girl; for no one spoke to her as if she were like other people, or as if any heartiness were possible between them. Perhaps no one could have felt quite at home with her but a mother, whose heart had been one with hers from a season long anterior to the development of any repulsive oddity. But her position was one of peculiar isolation, for no one really approached her individual being; and that she should be unaware of this loneliness, seemed to me saddest of all. I soon found, however, that the most distant attempt on my part to show her attention, was either received with absolute indifference, or coldly repelled without the slightest acknowledgment.

But I return to the first night of my sojourn at Hilton Hall.

===Chinese===
这个姑娘身上那种孤零零的意味，于我而言有某种格外令人动容之处；因为没有人跟她说话时，是把她当作与常人一般无二的，也没有人仿佛觉得彼此之间能有半分真心。除了一个母亲，也许再没有人能在她身边感到完全自在——而那位母亲的心，早在她身上生出任何拒人千里的怪癖之前许久，便已与她同心相连。可她的处境却是一种格外彻底的孤绝，因为没有人真正触碰到她那个独立的自我；而她竟似浑然不觉这份孤独，这在我看来，是最令人心酸之处。然而我很快便发觉，但凡我稍作姿态，想要向她略献殷勤，所得到的回应要么是彻头彻尾的漠然，要么便是冷淡的拒斥，连一丝领情的表示也没有。

且让我回到我在希尔顿庄园（Hilton Hall）度过的第一个夜晚。

## VI. My Quarters / VI. 我的住处

===Original===
After making arrangements for commencing work in the morning, I took my leave, and retired to my own room, intent upon carrying out with more minuteness the survey I had already commenced: several cupboards in the wall, and one or two doors, apparently of closets, had especially attracted my attention. Strange was its look as I entered—as of a room hollowed out of the past, for a memorial of dead times. The fire had sunk low, and lay smouldering beneath the white ashes, like the life of the world beneath the snow, or the heart of a man beneath cold and grey thoughts. I lighted the candles which stood upon the table, but the room, instead of being brightened looked blacker than before, for the light revealed its essential blackness.

As I cast my eyes around me, standing with my back to the hearth (on which, for mere companionship’s sake, I had just heaped fresh wood), a thrill ran suddenly throughout my frame. I felt as if, did it last a moment longer, I should become aware of another presence in the room; but, happily for me, it ceased before it had reached that point; and I, recovering my courage, remained ignorant of the cause of my fear, if there were any, other than the nature of the room itself. With a candle in my hand, I proceeded to open the various cupboards and closets. At first I found nothing remarkable about any of them. The latter were quite empty, except the last I came to, which had a piece of very old elaborate tapestry hanging at the back of it. Lifting this up, I saw what seemed at first to be panels, corresponding to those which formed the room; but on looking more closely, I discovered that this back of the closet was, or had been, a door. There was nothing unusual in this, especially in such an old house; but the discovery roused in me a strong desire to know what lay behind the old door. I found that it was secured only by an ordinary bolt, from which the handle had been removed. Soothing my conscience with the reflection that I had a right to know what sort of place had communication with my room, I succeeded, by the help of my deer-knife, in forcing back the rusty bolt; and though, from the stiffness of the hinges, I dreaded a crack, they yielded at last with only a creak.

===Chinese===
安排妥当，明早便要开始授课，我于是告辞，回到自己房中，打算把方才已开了个头的查看再细细做上一遍：墙上几个壁橱，还有一两扇看样子是通向小储物间的门，格外引起了我的注意。我走进去时，它的模样透着古怪——仿佛是从往昔里凿出来的一间屋子，专为缅怀已逝的岁月而设。炉火已暗了下去，在白灰底下闷烧着，一如白雪之下的世间生机，又如一个男人在阴冷灰暗的思绪之下的那颗心。我点燃了桌上立着的蜡烛，可这屋子非但没有亮起来，反倒显得比先前更黑，因为光把它的幽暗照出了底。

我背对炉火站着（方才不过是图个作伴，我又往炉里添了些新柴），朝四下里打量——忽然，一阵战栗流遍我全身。我觉得，这战栗若是再延续片刻，我便会察觉到房中还有另一个存在；但所幸它在到达那一步之前便止住了。我重又鼓起勇气，依然不知自己究竟在怕什么——若当真有什么可怕的，那也不过是这屋子本身的脾性罢了。我手执一支蜡烛，逐一去开那几个壁橱和储物间。起初，哪一间都没发现什么特别。那几间储物间都空空如也，唯有我最后走到的那一间，里头的背面挂着一幅极古旧、织工繁复的挂毯。我掀起挂毯，起先看见的似乎是镶板，与构成这间屋子四壁的镶板一般无二；可再细看，我发现这储物间的背壁，正是一扇门，或曾经是一扇门。这本没什么稀奇，尤其是在这样一座老宅里；但这一发现，却在我心头勾起一股强烈的渴望，想知道这扇旧门背后究竟是什么。我发现它只用一道普通的门闩闩住，门闩上的把手已被人拆去。我安慰自己的良心说，与我这屋子相通的到底是什么去处，我自有权知晓——这样开解过自己之后，我借一把猎鹿刀之助，硬是把那生锈的门闩顶了回去；尽管铰链僵涩，我生怕它嘎啦一声裂开，可它们到头来只吱呀一响，便松开了。

===Original===
The opening door revealed a large hall, empty utterly, save of dust and cobwebs, which festooned it in all quarters, and gave it an appearance of unutterable desolation. The now familiar feeling, that I had seen the place before, filled my mind the first moment, and passed away the next. A broad, right-angled staircase, with massive banisters, rose from the middle of the hall. This staircase could not have originally belonged to the ancient wing which I had observed on my first approach, being much more modern; but I was convinced, from the observations I had made as to the situation of my room, that I was bordering upon, if not within, the oldest portion of the pile. In sudden horror, lest I should hear a light footfall upon the awful stair, I withdrew hurriedly, and having secured both the doors, betook myself to my bedroom; in whose dingy four-post bed, with its carving and plumes reminding me of a hearse, I was soon ensconced amidst the snowiest linen, with the sweet and clean odour of lavender. In spite of novelty, antiquity, speculation, and dread, I was soon fast asleep; becoming thereby a fitter inhabitant of such regions, than when I moved about with restless and disturbing curiosity, through their ancient and deathlike repose.

I made no use of my discovered door, although I always intended doing so; especially after, in talking about the building with Lady Hilton, I found that I was at perfect liberty to make what excursions I pleased into the deserted portions.

===Chinese===
门一开，现出一座宽敞的大厅，空无一物，唯有灰尘与蛛网，在四面结成垂帷一般，给这厅堂添上一股说不出的荒凉。那如今已令我熟稔的感觉——仿佛我曾来过此地——头一刹那占满我心，下一刹那便又散去。一道宽阔的、转角成直角的楼梯，带着粗重的栏杆，从大厅正中升起。这道楼梯不可能原本就属于我初次抵达时留意到的那座古旧侧翼，它要比那新得多；可我从自己这屋子所处的方位推断，确信我正贴着、甚至已然置身于这座宅邸最古老的部分。我忽生一阵恐惧，生怕在那可怕的楼梯上传来轻轻的脚步声，便匆匆退了出去，把两扇门都闩好，回到自己的卧房；在那张昏暗的四柱床上——床上的雕花与羽饰叫我想起灵车——我很快便安顿下来，埋进最雪白的亚麻布之间，满嗅着薰衣草那甜净的清香。尽管有新奇、古老、揣测与忧惧，我还是很快沉沉睡去；如此一来，我反倒比先前带着焦躁扰人的好奇，穿行于它们那古老而如死一般的寂静之中时，更配做这些地方的主人。

我不曾动用我发现的那扇门，尽管我一直打算这么做；尤其是在与希尔顿夫人谈起这宅子之后，我发现尽可随意去那些废弃的部分探游，无人拘束。

===Original===
My pupils turned out to be teachable, and therefore my occupation was pleasant. Their sister frequently came to me for help, as there happened to be just then an interregnum of governesses: soon she settled into a regular pupil.

After a few weeks Lord Hilton returned. Though my room was so far from the great hall, I heard the clank of his spurs on its pavement. I trembled; for it sounded like the broken shoe. But I shook off the influence in a moment, heartily ashamed of its power over me. Soon I became familiar enough both with the sound and its cause; for his lordship rarely went anywhere except on horseback, and was booted and spurred from morning till night.

He received me with some appearance of interest, which immediately stiffened and froze. Beginning to shake hands with me as if he meant it, he instantly dropped my hand, as if it had stung him.

His nobility was of that sort which stands in constant need of repair. Like a weakly constitution, it required keeping up, and his lordship could not be said to neglect it; for he seemed to find his principal employment in administering continuous doses of obsequiousness to his own pride. His rank, like a coat made for some large ancestor, hung loose upon him: he was always trying to persuade himself that it was an excellent fit, but ever with an unacknowledged misgiving.
