于是我们在幽灵之室的门口作别。我望着她的白衣，那一点白仅只在黑暗中搅起一阵微微的摇曳，此外再无别的动静；即便在我明知她必然已经拐过弯、走下通往她自己房间的那段台阶之后许久，我仿佛仍看得见那点微光在晃动。随后我转过身，摸索着回到自己的房间。

===Original===
We often met after this in the haunted room. Indeed my spirit haunted it all day and all night long. And when we met amid the shadows, we were wrapped in the mantle of love, and from its folds looked out fearless on the ghostly world about us. Ghosts or none, they never annoyed us. Our love was a talisman, yea, an elixir of life, which made us equal to the twice-born—the disembodied dead. And they were as a wall of fear about us, to keep far off the unfriendly foot and the prying eye.

===Chinese===
此后我们便时常在这间幽灵之室中相会。事实上，我的魂灵日日夜夜都盘桓于此，不肯离去。当我们在阴影中相逢，便裹进了爱的氅衣，从它的褶皱间无畏地望着我们四周那鬼影憧憧的世界。无论那里究竟有没有鬼，他们从未惊扰过我们。我们的爱是一枚护符，甚而是一剂生命的灵药，使我们足以与那再生者——那脱离了肉身的亡魂——比肩而立。而他们则如同一道畏惧之墙环绕着我们，将不怀好意的脚步与窥探的目光远远隔开。

===Original===
In the griefs that followed, I often thought with myself that I would gladly die for a thousand years, might I then awake for one night in the haunted chamber, a ghost, among the ghosts who crowded its stained moonbeams, and see my dead Alice smiling across the glimmering rays, and beckoning me to the old nook, she, too, having come awake out of the sleep of death, in the dream of the haunted chamber. "Might we but sit there," I said, "through the night, as of old, and love and comfort each other, till the moon go down, and the pale dawn, which is the night of the ghosts, begin to arise, then gladly would I go to sleep for another thousand years, in the hope that when I next became conscious of life, it might be in another such ghostly night, in the chamber of the ghosts."

===Chinese===
在后来接踵而至的那些悲痛里，我常常独自思忖：只要能在这间幽灵之室中再醒来一夜——化作一个鬼魂，跻身于那些簇拥在它彩色月光里的群鬼之间，看见我那已死的爱丽丝隔着明灭的光线含笑望我，向我招手，唤我回到那个旧日的角落，而她自己，也是在幽灵之室的这场大梦里，从死亡的沉睡中一同醒来的——那我便甘心死去一千年。“但愿我们能像从前那样，在那里坐上一整夜，”我说，“彼此相爱，彼此宽慰，直坐到月亮西沉，直坐到那惨淡的黎明——那黎明于幽魂而言便是黑夜——开始升起；那我便欣然再度沉睡上一个千年，只盼下一次再有了生命的知觉时，仍是在这样一个鬼魅的夜里，在这群鬼之室之中。”

## XVI. The Clanking Shoe / XVI. 叮当作响的马蹄铁

===Original===
Time passed. We began to feel very secure in that room, watched as it was by the sleepless sentry, Fear. One night I ventured to take a light with me.

“How nice to have a candle!” she said as I entered. “I hope they are all in bed, though. It will drive some of them into fits if they see the light.”

“I wanted to show you something I found in the library today.”

“What is it?”

I opened a book, and showed her a paper inside it, with some verses written on it.

===Chinese===
时光流逝。我们开始觉得在那间房里十分安稳，那房间既由恐惧这位不眠的哨兵守卫着。一天夜里，我大着胆子带了一支灯火前去。

“能有支蜡烛真好！”我一进门，她便说，“不过，我盼他们都已经上床睡了。要是被谁瞧见这光，会把他们中几个吓得抽风过去的。”

“我想给你看一样今天我在藏书室里找到的东西。”

“什么东西？”

我打开一本书，给她看夹在里面的那张纸，上头写着几行诗句。

===Original===
“Whose writing is that?” I asked.

“Yours, of course. As if I did not know your writing!”

“Will you look at the date?”

“*Seventeen hundred and ninety-three!* You are making game of me, Duncan. But the paper does look yellow and old.”

===Chinese===
“这是谁的字迹？”我问。

“当然是你的。好像我不认得你的字似的！”

“你看看那个日期？”

“*一七九三年*！你在拿我寻开心吧，邓肯。不过这纸瞧着倒真是又黄又旧。”

===Original===
“I found it as you see it, in that book. It belonged to Lord Hilton’s brother. The verses are a translation of part of the poem beside which they lie—one by Von Salis, who died shortly before that date at the bottom. I will read them to you, and then show you something else that is strange about them. The poem is called ‘Psyche’s Sorrow.’ Psyche means the soul, Alice.”

===Chinese===
“我找到它时，它就是你眼下看到的这副模样，夹在那本书里。那本书原是希尔顿勋爵的兄弟的。这几行诗，是翻译了与它并排的那首诗的一部分——那首诗出自冯·萨利斯（Von Salis）的手笔，他就在底下那个日期之前不久辞世。我读给你听，然后再让你瞧瞧它还有一处古怪的地方。那首诗题为《心灵之恸》（‘Psyche’s Sorrow’）。‘Psyche’（普赛克）就是灵魂的意思，爱丽丝。”

===Original===
“Psyche’s sighing all her prison darkens;
She is moaning for the far-off stars;
Fearing, hoping, every sound she hearkens—
Fate may now be breaking at her bars.

Bound, fast bound, are Psyche’s airy pinions:
High her heart, her mourning soft and low—
Knowing that in sultry pain’s dominions
Grow the palms that crown the victor’s brow;

That the empty hand the wreath encloses;
Earth’s cold winds but make the spirit brave;
Knowing that the briars bear the roses,
Golden flowers the waste deserted grave.

In the cypress-shade her myrtle groweth;
Much she loves, because she much hath borne;
Love-led, through the darksome way she goeth—
On to meet him in the breaking morn.

She can bear—”

===Chinese===
“普赛克的叹息将她整个牢笼染暗；
她为那遥不可及的星辰哀泣；
怀着恐惧，怀着希望，她谛听每一丝声响——
命运此刻也许正撞断她的铁栏。

普赛克那轻盈的双翼被紧紧缚住：
她的心高扬，她的哀歌轻柔低回——
她深知，在酷热痛苦的疆域里，
正生长着加冕胜者额头的棕榈；

深知空空的手掌也能拢住花环；
尘世的寒风只会叫灵魂越发勇敢；
深知荆棘之上会开出玫瑰，
荒寂的弃冢也会绽放金色的花朵。

在柏树的浓荫下，她的桃金娘生长；
她爱得深，因为她承受得多；
在爱的引领下，她穿过幽暗的路途——
前去，在破晓的晨光里与他相会。

她能承受——”

===Original===
“Here the translation ceases, you see; and then follows the date, with the words in German underneath it—‘How weary I am!’ Now what is strange, Alice, is, that this date is the very month and year in which I was born.”

She did not reply to this with anything beyond a mere assent. Her mind was fixed on the poem itself. She began to talk about it, and I was surprised to find how thoroughly she entered into it and understood it. She seemed to have crowded the growth of a lifetime into the last few months. At length I told her how unhappy I had felt for some time, at remaining in Lord Hilton’s house, as matters now were.

===Chinese===
“你看，译诗到这里就断了；接下来便是那个日期，底下还附了一行德文——‘我好疲倦啊！’爱丽丝，离奇的是，这个日期，正正是我出生的那个年月。”

她对这番话的回应，不过是淡淡地应了一声。她的心神全落在那首诗本身上。她开始谈论起它来，而令我惊讶的是，她领会得何等透彻，懂得何等深切。她仿佛把一生的成长，都压缩进了过去的这几个月里。终于，我告诉她，时局既已如此，我还留在希尔顿勋爵家中，这一阵子心里有多不安。

===Original===
“Then you must go,” she said, quite quietly.

This troubled me.

“You do not mind it?”

“No. I shall be very glad.”

“Will you go with me?” I asked, perplexed.

“Of course I will.”

===Chinese===
“那你就得走了。”她说，语气十分平静。

这话叫我心中一沉。

“你不介意我走？”

“不。我会很高兴的。”

“你愿意跟我一道走吗？”我困惑地问。

“当然愿意。”

===Original===
I did not know what to say to this, for I had no money, and of course I should have none of my salary. She divined at once the cause of my hesitation.

“I have a diamond bracelet in my room,” she said, with a smile, “and a few guineas besides.”

“How shall we get away?”

“Nothing is easier. My old nurse, whom I mentioned to you before, lives at the lodge gate.”

“Oh! I know her very well,” I interrupted. “But she’s not Scotch?”

“Indeed she is. But she has been with our family almost all her life. I often go to see her, and sometimes stay all night with her. You can get a carriage ready in the village, and neither of us will be missed before morning.”

===Chinese===
我不知该对此说什么才好，因为我身无分文，而那份薪俸自然也是半个便士都拿不到的。她立刻便猜透了我犹豫的缘由。

“我房里有一只钻石手镯，”她带着笑说，“此外还有几个几尼。”

“我们怎么走得了呢？”

“再容易不过了。我从前跟你提过的那位老保姆，就住在庄园大门的门房里。”

“哦！她我熟得很，”我插嘴道，“可她不是苏格兰人吧？”

“她确实是苏格兰人。不过她几乎一辈子都跟在我们家里。我常常去看她，有时还在她那儿过夜。你可以在村子里把马车备好，明天早上之前，谁也不会发觉你我都不在了。”

===Original===
I looked at her in renewed surprise at the decision of her invention. She covered her face, as she seldom did now, but went on:

“We can go to London, where you will easily find something to do. Men always can there. And when I come of age—”

“Alice, how old are you?” I interrupted.

===Chinese===
我又惊又讶地望着她，为她那番筹谋的果决。她用手遮住了脸——这是她如今很少有的举动——却接着说下去：

“我们可以去伦敦，在那儿你很容易找到事做。男人在那里总找得到营生。等我到了年纪——”

“爱丽丝，你今年几岁了？”我插嘴问。

===Original===
“Nineteen,” she answered. “By the way,” she resumed, “when I think of it—how odd!—that”—pointing to the date on the paper—“is the very month in which I too was born.”

I was too much surprised to interrupt her, and she continued:

“I never think of my age without recalling one thing about my birth, which nurse often refers to. She was going up the stair to my mother’s room, when she happened to notice a bright star, not far from the new moon. As she crossed the room with me in her arms, just after I was born, she saw the same star almost on the tip of the opposite horn. My mother died a week after. Who knows how different I might have been if she had lived!”

===Chinese===
“十九。”她答道。“对了，”她接着说，“我想起来了——好生奇怪！——那个”——她指着纸上那个日期——“也正正是我出生的那个月份。”

我惊讶得无从插嘴，她便继续说下去：

“每逢想起自己的年纪，我总会记起一桩关于我降生的事，那是保姆常常提起的。那会儿她正上楼去我母亲的房间，偶然留意到一颗明亮的星，离那一弯新月不远。等我出生后不久，她抱着我穿过房间时，又看见了同一颗星，几乎贴在新月另一端的尖角上。我母亲过了一周便去世了。谁知道，倘若她活着，我又会是多么不同的一副模样！”

===Original===
It was long before I spoke. The awful and mysterious thoughts roused in my mind by the revelations of the day held me silent. At length I said, half thinking aloud:

“Then you and I, Alice, were born the same hour, and our mothers died together.”

===Chinese===
良久，我才开口。这一日所揭示的种种真相，在我心中唤起那些可畏而神秘的念头，叫我默然无语。终于，我半像自言自语地说：

“那么，爱丽丝，你和我，是同一个时辰生的，而我们的母亲，又是一同死去的。”

===Original===
Receiving no answer, I looked at her. She was fast asleep, and breathing gentle, full breaths. She had been sitting for some time with her head lying on my shoulder and my arm around her. I could not bear to wake her.

We had been in this position perhaps for half an hour, when suddenly a cold shiver ran through me, and all at once I became aware of the far-off gallop of a horse. It drew nearer. On and on it came—nearer and nearer. Then came the clank of the broken shoe!

At the same moment, Alice started from her sleep and, springing to her feet, stood an instant listening. Then crying out, in an agonised whisper—“The horse with the clanking shoe!” she flung her arms around me. Her face was white as the spectral moon which, the moment I put the candle out, looked in through a clear pane beside us; and she gazed fearfully, yet wildly-defiant, towards the door. We clung to each other. We heard the sound come nearer and nearer, till it thundered right up to the very door of the room, terribly loud. It ceased. But the door was flung open, and Lord Hilton entered, followed by servants with lights.

===Chinese===
不见她回答，我便看她。她已经沉沉睡去，呼吸轻柔而绵长。方才好一阵子，她一直把头靠在我肩上，我臂膀环着她。我不忍心叫醒她。

我们就这样保持着这个姿势，约莫过了半个钟头，忽然一阵寒颤传遍我全身，我猛然察觉到远处有一匹马在疾驰。它越来越近。一路奔来——越来越近。紧接着，传来了那只破裂马蹄铁叮当作响的声响！

就在同一瞬间，爱丽丝从睡中惊起，一跃站定，凝神听了片刻。随即她用痛苦万状的气声呼喊出声——“那匹挂着叮当作响马蹄铁的马！”她一把搂住了我。她的脸白得犹如那鬼魅般的月亮——我刚把蜡烛吹熄，月亮便从我们身旁一扇明净的窗格里探望进来；她满眼恐惧，却又带着一种狂乱的桀骜，朝门口望去。我们紧紧相拥。我们听见那声响越来越近，直到它轰然直冲到这间房的门前，响得骇人。声音停了。然而房门被猛地推开，希尔顿勋爵闯了进来，身后跟着举着灯火的仆人们。

===Original===
I have but a very confused remembrance of what followed. I heard a vile word from the lips of Lord Hilton; I felt my fingers on his throat; I received a blow on the head; and I seem to remember a cry of agony from Alice as I fell. What happened next I do not know.

When I came to myself, I was lying on a wide moor, with the night wind blowing about me. I presume that I had wandered thither in a state of unconsciousness, after being turned out of the Hall, and that I had at last fainted from loss of blood. I was unable to move for a long time. At length the morning broke, and I found myself not far from the Hall. I crept back, a mile or two, to the gates, and having succeeded in rousing Alice’s old nurse, was taken in with many lamentations, and put to bed in the lodge. I had a violent fever; and it was all the poor woman could do to keep my presence a secret from the family at the Hall.

===Chinese===
接下来发生的事，我只记得一片混乱。我听见希尔顿勋爵嘴里吐出一个下流的字眼；我感到自己的手指掐上了他的喉咙；我头上挨了一记重击；而在我倒下时，我似乎还记得爱丽丝发出一声痛苦的尖叫。再后来发生了什么，我便无从知晓了。

等我苏醒过来时，我正躺在一片辽阔的荒原上，夜风在我四周吹拂。我猜想，我是被赶出庄园之后，在神志不清的境地中游荡到了那里，最后因失血过多而昏厥。有很长一段时间我动弹不得。终于天亮了，我发觉自己离庄园并不远。我爬了一两英里，回到庄园大门，好不容易叫醒了爱丽丝的那位老保姆，便在一阵阵哀怜叹息声中被收留进去，安顿在门房的床上。我发了一场凶猛的高热；那可怜的女人费尽心机，才把我在此的消息瞒住了庄园里的一家人。

===Original===
When I began to mend, my first question was about Alice. I learned, though with some difficulty—for my kind attendant was evidently unwilling to tell me all the truth—that Alice, too, had been very ill; and that, a week before, they had removed her. But she either would not or could not tell me where they had taken her. I believe she could not. Nor do I know for certain to this day.

Mrs. Blakesley offered me the loan of some of her savings to get me to London. I received it with gratitude, and as soon as I was fit to travel, made my way thither. Afraid for my reason, if I had no employment to keep my thoughts from brooding on my helplessness, and so increasing my despair, and determined likewise that my failure should not make me burdensome to anyone else, I enlisted in the Scotch Greys, before letting any of my friends know where I was. Through the help of one already mentioned in my story, I soon obtained a commission. From the field of Waterloo, I rode into Brussels with a broken arm and a sabre-cut in the head.

===Chinese===
等我病体渐有起色，我开口问的头一件事，便是爱丽丝的消息。我费了些周折才打听出来——因为那位好心照看我的人，显然不愿把实情全告诉我——爱丽丝也病得很重；而且，在一周之前，他们已经把她移走了。可她要么是不肯，要么是不能告诉我，他们究竟把她抬到了哪里。我相信她是不能。便是到今日，我也未能确切知晓。

布莱克斯利太太（Mrs. Blakesley）主动提出把她的一点积蓄借给我，好让我前往伦敦。我满怀感激地收下了，一等身子经得起舟车劳顿，便动身去了那里。我唯恐自己若无营生可做，便会任由心思在无助之感上反复煎熬，从而加深绝望，终至心智失常；又立意不让自己的落魄成为旁人的累赘，于是在让任何一位朋友得知我的下落之前，我便投身入了苏格兰灰骑兵团（Scotch Greys）。多亏我在故事前文中已经提过的那位故人之助，我很快便谋得了一个军官的委任。从滑铁卢（Waterloo）的战场上，我骑马进了布鲁塞尔（Brussels），一条胳膊已经断了，头上还带着一道军刀伤（sabre-cut）。

===Original===
As we passed along one of the streets, through all the clang of iron-shod hoofs on the stones around me, I heard the ominous clank. At the same moment, I heard a cry. It was the voice of my Alice. I looked up. At a barred window I saw her face; but it was terribly changed. I dropped from my horse. As soon as I was able to move from the hospital, I went to the place, and found it was a lunatic asylum. I was permitted to see the inmates, but discovered no one resembling her. I do not now believe that she was ever there. But I may be wrong. Nor will I trouble my reader with the theories on which I sought to account for the vision. They will occur to himself readily enough.

===Chinese===
当我们沿着一条街道行进时，透过四下里铁掌马蹄踏在石路上那一片叮当的喧声，我听见了那不祥的当啷声。就在同一刹那，我听见一声惊呼。那是我的爱丽丝的声音。我抬起头。在一扇装着铁栏的窗户后面，我看见了她的脸；然而那张脸已变得骇人地不同。我翻身跌下马来。等我一能够从医院里挪动身子，便赶到那个地方，发觉那是一所疯人院。我被允许探看里面的病患，却没发现任何像她的人。如今我并不相信她当初真在那里。但我也可能弄错了。至于我当年竭力用以解释那番幻象的种种推想，我也不愿拿来烦扰我的读者。那些推想，读者自己是不难想见的。

===Original===
For years and years I know not whether she was alive or dead. I sought her far and near. I wandered over England, France, and Germany, hopelessly searching; listening at tables-d’hôte; lurking about madhouses; haunting theatres and churches; often, in wild regions, begging my way from house to house; I did not find her.

===Chinese===
一年又一年，我始终不知她是死是活。我天涯海角地寻她。我浪迹于英格兰（England）、法兰西（France）与德意志（Germany），徒然地寻访；在旅馆的公共餐桌旁竖起耳朵听；在疯人院四周潜伏逡巡；在剧院与教堂里流连不去；又常常在荒僻的乡野，挨家挨户乞食度日；我终究没能寻见她。

===Original===
Once I visited Hilton Hall. I found it all but deserted. I learned that Mrs. Wilson was dead, and that there were only two or three servants in the place. I managed to get into the house unseen, and made my way to the haunted chamber. My feelings were not so keen as I had anticipated, for they had been dulled by long suffering. But again I saw the moon shine through those windows of stained glass. Again her beams were crowded with ghosts. She was not amongst them. “My lost love!” I cried; and then, rebuking myself, “No; she is not lost. They say that Time and Space exist not, save in our thoughts. If so, then that which has been, is, and the Past can never cease. She is mine, and I shall find her—what matters it where, or when, or how? Till then, my soul is but a moonlighted chamber of ghosts; and I sit within, the dreariest of them all. When she enters, it will be a home of love. And I wait—I wait.”

===Chinese===
有一次，我重返了希尔顿庄园。我发觉它几乎已遭废弃。我得知威尔逊太太（Mrs. Wilson）已经去世，整座宅子里只剩下两三个仆人。我设法潜入屋中而不为人所见，一路走到了那间闹鬼的屋子。我的心境并不如我预想的那般锥心彻骨，因为漫长的苦难早已将它磨钝了。但我又看见了月光透过那些彩绘玻璃窗洒进来。我又看见她的清辉里挤满了鬼影。她不在其中。“我失去的爱人啊！”我喊道；随即又责备自己，“不；她并未失去。人们说，时间与空间本不存在，只除却在我们的念头里。若当真如此，那么凡是曾经存在过的，便是存在着的，往昔永远不会消逝。她是我的，我终会找到她——在何处、在何时、以何种方式，又有什么要紧？在那之前，我的灵魂不过是一间月华照彻的鬼影之屋；而我坐在其中，是所有鬼影里最凄凉的一个。等她走进来，这里就会变成一座爱之家。我等待着——我等待着。”

===Original===
I sat and brooded over the Past, till I fell asleep in the phantom-peopled night. And all the night long they were about me—the men and women of the long past. And I was one of them. I wandered in my dreams over the whole house, habited in a long old-fashioned gown, searching for one who was Alice, and yet would be someone else. From room to room I wandered till weary, and could not find her. At last, I gave up the search, and, retreating to the library, shut myself in. There, taking down from the shelf the volume of Von Salis, I tried hard to go on with the translation of “Psyche’s Sorrow,” from the point where the student had left it, thinking it, all the time, my own unfinished work.

When I woke in the morning, the chamber of ghosts, in which I had fallen asleep, had vanished. The sun shone in through the windows of the library; and on its dusty table lay Von Salis, open at “Psyche’s Trauer.” The sheet of paper with the translation on it, was not there. I hastened to leave the house, and effected my escape before the servants were astir.

===Chinese===
我坐着，在往事中冥想，直到在那群鬼攒动的夜里沉沉睡去。整整一夜，他们都在我四周——那些久远岁月里的男男女女。而我自己，也是他们中的一个。我在梦里游荡遍了整座宅子，身上穿着一件旧式的长袍，寻访一个既是爱丽丝、又像是另一个什么人的身影。我从这个房间游荡到那个房间，直到筋疲力尽，却始终寻她不着。终于，我放弃了搜寻，退回藏书室，把自己关在里面。在那儿，我从架上取下那本冯·萨利斯的诗集，竭力想从那位学生当年停笔之处，接着把《心灵之恸》的译文续完，并且自始至终，都把它当作我自己未竟的功课。

及至清晨我醒来，我入睡时那间鬼影攒动的屋子，已然消失了。阳光透过藏书室的窗户照了进来；在那落满灰尘的桌上，摊着冯·萨利斯那本诗集，正翻在《心灵之恸》（“Psyche’s Trauer”）那一页。而那张写着译文的纸，却不在那里了。我赶忙离开宅子，在仆人们起身之前，便已安然脱身。

===Original===
Sometimes I condensed my whole being into a single intensity of will—that she should come to me; and sustained it, until I fainted with the effort. She did not come. I desisted altogether at last, for I bethought me that, whether dead or alive, it must cause her torture not to be able to obey it.

Sometimes I questioned my own sanity. But the thought of the loss of my reason did not in itself trouble me much. What tortured me almost to the madness it supposed was the possible fact, which a return to my right mind might reveal—that there never had been a Lady Alice. What if I died, and awoke from my madness, and found a clear blue air of life, a joyous world of sunshine, a divine wealth of delight around and in me—but no Lady Alice—she having vanished with all the other phantoms of a sick brain! “Rather let me be mad still,” I said, “if mad I am; and so dream on that I have been blessed. Were I to wake to such a heaven, I would pray God to let me go and live the life I had but dreamed, with all its sorrows, and all its despair, and all its madness, that when I died again, I might know that such things had been, and could never be awaked from, and left behind with the dream.” But I was not mad, any more than Hamlet; though, like him, despair sometimes led me far along the way at the end of which madness lies.

===Chinese===
有时，我把整个生命凝聚成一念专挚的心志——愿她来到我身边；并且苦苦撑持，直到我为此昏厥过去。她没有来。我终于彻底放弃了，因为我想到，无论她是死是活，不能顺从这一念召唤，于她必是一种折磨。

有时，我也会怀疑自己是否神志健全。然而，丧失理智这一念头本身，并不怎样令我忧心。真正把我折磨到几近癫狂的——正是这一念头所预设的那种癫狂——是一个可能的事实：一旦我恢复了清醒的心智，这事实也许便会显露——那就是，世上从来不曾有过一位爱丽丝小姐。倘若我死了，从癫狂中醒来，发觉眼前是一片澄澈蔚蓝、充盈生气的天空，一个阳光明媚的欢乐世界，一种神圣而丰盈的喜悦，将我环绕、将我充盈——却唯独没有爱丽丝小姐——她已随着这一颗病脑所生的其余种种幻影，一并消散了，那又当如何！“那就让我索性疯下去吧，”我说，“若我果然是疯的；好教我继续做着那曾经蒙福的梦。倘若我醒来，面对的竟是那样一个天堂，我定要祈求上帝，让我回去过那我以为只是梦见过的一生——连同它全部的悲伤、全部的绝望、全部的癫狂——好教我再次死去之时，能确知这一切都曾真实发生过，且永不能再被醒转、被当作一场梦抛在身后。”然而我并未发疯，正如哈姆雷特（Hamlet）未曾发疯一样；只不过，和他一样，绝望有时会把我引上一条长长的路，而那条路的尽头，便是癫狂。

## XVII. The Physician / XVII. 医生

===Original===
I was now Captain Campbell, of the Scotch Greys, contriving to live on my half pay, and thinking far more about the past than the present or the future. My father was dead. My only brother was also gone, and the property had passed into other hands. I had no fixed place of abode, but went from one spot to another, as the whim seized me—sometimes remaining months, sometimes removing next day, but generally choosing retired villages about which I knew nothing.

I had spent a week in a small town on the borders of Wales, and intended remaining a fortnight longer, when I was suddenly seized with a violent illness, in which I lay insensible for three weeks. When I recovered consciousness, I found that my head had been shaved, and that the cicatrice of my old wound was occasionally very painful. Of late I have suspected that I had some operation performed upon my skull during my illness; but Dr. Ruthwell never dropped a hint to that effect. This was the friend whom, when first I opened my seeing eyes, I beheld sitting by my bedside, watching the effect of his last prescription. He was one of the few in the profession, whose love of science and love of their fellows combined, would be enough to chain them to the art of healing, irrespective of its emoluments. He was one of the few, also, who see the marvellous in all science, and, therefore, reject nothing merely because the marvellous may seem to predominate in it. Yet neither would he accept anything of the sort as fact, without the strictest use of every experiment within his power, even then remaining often in doubt. This man conferred honour by his friendship; and I am happy to think that before many days of recovery had passed, we were friends indeed. But I lay for months under his care before I was able to leave my bed.

===Chinese===
此时我已是坎贝尔上尉（Captain Campbell），编在苏格兰灰骑兵团（the Scotch Greys），靠着半薪勉力度日，心头所系的，远不是当下或将来，倒是那已经过去的岁月。父亲已谢世。我唯一的兄弟也已辞世，那份祖产便旁落他人之手。我没有固定的居所，凭着性子由一处迁往另一处——有时一住数月，有时次日便走，但总爱挑那些我一无所知的偏僻村落。

我在威尔士（Wales）边境的一座小镇上住了一周，本打算再盘桓半月，却忽然染上一场凶险的重病，一连三周不省人事。待我恢复了知觉，发现自己已被剃去了头发，那道旧伤的瘢痕时不时地作痛。到了近年我才疑心，生病期间我的颅骨怕是动了什么手术；然而鲁思韦尔医生（Dr. Ruthwell）对此从未吐露过半点口风。我那双复明的眼初次睁开时所看见的，便是这位朋友坐在我的床畔，端详着他最新一处方的药效。同行之中，能因对学问的热爱与对同类的热爱合在一处，便足以将人系在疗救之艺上、不计其报酬丰薄的，本属寥寥，他便是其中之一。在一切学问中都能窥见神奇、因而绝不因某事之中神奇之气似乎过盛便将其一笔抹杀的，也属寥寥，他亦是其中之一。然而，任是何等神奇之事，若非以他力所能及的种种实验严加检验，他也绝不轻易认作事实；纵然如此，他仍常常存疑。此人的友情本身就是一种荣宠；而我甚为欣慰地记得，病愈方才数日，我们便已成了真正的朋友。但我在他的照拂下一躺便是数月，方能下床。

===Original===
He attributed my illness to the consequences of the sabre-cut, and my recovery to the potency of the drugs he had exhibited. I attributed my illness in great measure to the constant contemplation of my early history, no longer checked by any regular employment; and my recovery in equal measure to the power of his kindness and sympathy, helping from within what could never have been reached from without.

He told me that he had often been greatly perplexed with my symptoms, which would suddenly change in the most unaccountable manner, exhibiting phases which did not, as far as his knowledge went, belong to any variety of the suffering which gave the prevailing character to my ailment; and after I had so far recovered as to render it safe to turn my regard more particularly upon my own case, he said to me one day,

===Chinese===
他将我的病归咎于那道刀伤（sabre-cut）的后患，将我的痊愈归功于他所施用诸药的效力。而我则把自己的病，很大程度上归因于对早年往事不断的反刍沉思——再没有什么固定的营生能将它遏止；又把自己的痊愈，同样程度上归因于他那善意与同情的感化之力，从内里扶持着那从外头永难触及之处。

他对我说，他常常为我的症状大惑不解：那些症状会以最莫名其妙的方式骤然改易，呈现出——就他所知——并不属于我这场病所主导的那种疾苦的任何变类的症相；待我好转到可以放心将心思更专门地转回到自己这场病上来之后，有一天他对我说，

===Original===
“You would laugh at me, Campbell, were I to confess some of the bother this illness of yours has occasioned me; enough, indeed, to overthrow any conceit I ever had in my own diagnosis.”

“Go on,” I answered; “I promise not to laugh.”

He little knew how far I should be from laughing. “In your case,” he continued, “the *pathognomonic*, if you will excuse medical slang, was every now and then broken by the intrusion of altogether foreign symptoms.”

I listened with breathless attention.

“Indeed, on several occasions, when, after meditating on your case till I was worn out, I had fallen half asleep by your bedside, I came to myself with the strangest conviction that I was watching by the bedside of a woman.”

“Thank Heaven!” I exclaimed, starting up, “She lives still.”

===Chinese===
「坎贝尔，要是我向你招认，你这场病给我添了多少麻烦，你准会笑我；那麻烦之多，着实足以推翻我对自家诊断本领曾有过的任何自诩。」

「说下去吧，」我答道，「我保证不笑。」

他哪里晓得，我离笑出来该有多远。「你这场病里，」他接着说，「那些特征性病征（pathognomonic）——请恕我用了句医学行话——时不时地会被全然无关的症状闯进来打断。」

我屏息凝神地听着。

「真的，有好几回，我在你的病况前反复思量，直到精疲力竭，便在你的床畔半睡半醒地打了盹，猛然醒转时，心里怀着一种最古怪不过的念头：我守着的，竟是一个女人的病床。」

「谢天谢地！」我失声呼道，霍地坐起，「她竟还活着。」

===Original===
I need not describe the doctor’s look of amazement, almost consternation; for he thought a fresh access of fever was upon me, and I had already begun to rave. For his reassurance, however, I promised to account fully for my apparently senseless excitement; and that evening I commenced the narrative which forms the preceeding part of this story. Long before I reached its close, my exultation had vanished, and, as I wrote it for him, it ended with the expressed conviction that she must be dead. Ere long, however, the hope once more revived. While, however, the narrative was in progress, I gave him a summary, which amounted to this:—

===Chinese===
医生那副惊诧、近乎骇然的神情，我毋庸赘述；他只当是发热又犯了新一轮，而我也确已开始说胡话。为安他的心，我答应会把我那看似毫无来由的激动，原原本本地交代清楚；于是那天傍晚，我便着手讲述构成本篇故事前文的那段往事。还没等讲到末尾，我的欣喜便已消散；及至我把它写给他看时，它是以这样一个确信作结的：她必已不在人世。然而不久之后，希望重又复苏。不过，在叙述进行之中，我给了他一个提要，大意如下：——

===Original===
I had loved a lady—loved her still. I did not know where she was, and had reason to fear that her mind had given way under the suffering of our separation. Between us there existed, as well, the bond of a distant blood relationship; so distant, that but for its probable share in the production of another relationship of a very marvellous nature, it would scarcely have been worth alluding to. This was a kind of psychological attraction, which, when justified and strengthened by the spiritual energies of love, rendered the immediate communication of certain feelings, both mental and bodily, so rapid, that almost the consciousness of the one existed for the time in the mental circumstances of the other. Nay, so complete at times was the communication, that I even doubted her testimony as to some strange correspondence in our past history on this very ground, suspecting that, my memory being open to her retrospection, she saw my story, and took it for her own. It was, therefore, easy for me to account for Dr. Ruthwell’s scientific bewilderment at the symptoms I manifested.

===Chinese===
我曾爱上一位小姐——至今依然爱她。我不知她身在何处，却有理由担心，她的心智已在分离的苦楚之下崩垮。在我俩之间，还存在着一重远亲的血缘之缘；这血缘远到，若不是因为另一重性质极为玄妙的关系之形成或许有它一份，它简直不值一提。那是一种心灵上的相吸，当它为爱的精神伟力所印证、所加固时，便使某些感受——无论心力上的还是身体上的——在彼此间的当下相通迅捷到如此地步：几乎一个人的意识，一时之间便活在另一人的心境之中。不特如此，这相通有时竟完足到这般田地：单凭这一层，我连她对两人往事中某些离奇吻合的讲述都起了疑心，怀疑是我的记忆向她的回望敞开了，她看见了我的故事，便当作了自己的。因此，要解释鲁思韦尔医生对我所表现症状的学理上的困惑，于我而言并不费难。

===Original===
As my health revived, my hope and longing increased. But although I loved Lady Alice with more entireness than even during the latest period of our intercourse, a certain calm endurance had supervened, which rendered the relief of fierce action no longer necessary to the continuance of a sane existence. It was as if the concentrated orb of love had diffused itself in a genial warmth through the whole orb of life, imparting fresh vitality to many roots which had remained leafless in my being. For years the field of battle was the only field that had borne the flower of delight; now nature began to live again for me.

One day, the first on which I ventured to walk into the fields alone, I was delighted with the multitude of the daisies peeping from the grass everywhere—the first attempts of the earth, become conscious of blindness, to open eyes, and see what was about and above her. Everything is wonderful after the resurrection from illness. It is a resurrection of all nature. But somehow or other I was not satisfied with the daisies. They did not seem to me so lovely as the daisies I used to see when I was a child. I thought with myself, “This is the cloud that gathers with life, the dimness that passion and suffering cast over the eyes of the mind.” That moment my gaze fell upon a single, solitary, red-tipped daisy. My reasoning vanished, and my melancholy with it, slain by the red tips of the lonely beauty. This was the kind of daisy I had loved as a child; and with the sight of it, a whole field of them rushed back into my mind; a field of my father’s where, throughout the multitude, you could not have found a white one. My father was dead; the fields had passed into other hands; but perhaps the red-tipped gowans were left. I must go and see. At all events, the hill that overlooked the field would still be there, and no change would have passed upon *it*. It would receive me with the same familiar look as of old, still fronting the great mountain from whose sides I had first heard the sound of that clanking horseshoe, which, whatever might be said to account for it, had certainly had a fearful connection with my joys and sorrows both. Did the ghostly rider still haunt the place? or, if he did, should I hear again that sound of coming woe? Whether or not, I defied him. I would not be turned from my desire to see the old place by any fear of a ghostly marauder, whom I should be only too glad to encounter, if there were the smallest chance of coming off with the victory.

As soon as my friend would permit me, I set out for Scotland.

===Chinese===
随着元气渐复，我的希望与渴念一同滋长。然而，尽管我爱爱丽丝小姐（Lady Alice），甚至比我们交往的最后那段日子爱得更为完满，一种沉静的隐忍已悄然降临，使我再不必借激烈的行为来纾解、以求继续过一种神志清明的日子。恍若那凝聚如一轮的爱，已化作和煦的暖意，漫流贯穿于生命的整个球体之中，将新的生机注入我体内许多长久以来寸叶不生的根须。多年来，唯有战场这片土地，曾开出过欢乐之花；如今，大自然重新为我活了过来。

有一天——我头一回壮着胆子独自走到田野里——漫山遍野从草丛里探出头来的雏菊，叫我满心欢喜：那是大地方才察觉自己原是盲目，初次试着睁开眼，去看看她身周与头顶的事物。大病初愈，万事万物都奇妙如许。那是一场万物的复活。可不知怎的，我对那些雏菊并不满意。它们在我眼中，似乎不如我幼时所见的那般可爱。我心下自忖：「这便是随生命一道聚拢的阴翳，是激情与苦痛蒙在心智之眼上的那层朦胧。」就在那一刻，我的目光落在一朵孤零零的、红尖的雏菊上。我的那套道理随之消散，连同我的感伤一道，被那孤独的美人红红的尖端所斩杀。这正是我幼时所爱的那种雏菊；随着看见它，整整一片那样的雏菊一齐涌回我脑海：那是我父亲的一块田，在密密匝匝的一片里，你简直找不出一朵白的来。父亲已谢世；那些田地已易手他人；然而那红尖的雏菊（gowans）或许还在。我必须去看看。无论如何，俯瞰着那块田的那座荒丘必仍在那里，它本身断不会有了什么改变。它会带着与昔日一般无二的熟悉神情迎受我，依旧面朝着那座大山——正是从那大山的山坡上，我头一回听见那叮当作响的马蹄铁（clanking horseshoe）的声响，无论人们作何解释来交代它，它都确凿无疑地与我的悲与喜有过可怖的牵连。那幽灵般的骑手可还出没于彼处？又或者，纵然他在，我可还会再听见那预示祸事将至的声响？不管怎样，我都不怕他。任何对一个幽灵劫掠者的惧怕，都不能叫我打消重访故地的念头；若有一丝一毫得胜而归的可能，我倒巴不得与这等角色狭路相逢一场。

朋友一准许，我便动身前往苏格兰。

## XVIII. Old Friends / XVIII. 故人

===Original===
I made the journey by easy stages, chiefly on the back of a favourite black horse, which had carried me well in several fights, and had come out of them scarred, like his master, but sound in wind and limb. It was night when I reached the village lying nearest to my birth place.

When I woke in the morning, I found the whole region filled with a white mist, hiding the mountains around. Now and then a peak looked through, and again retired into the cloudy folds. In the wide, straggling street, below the window at which I had made them place my breakfast-table, a periodic fair was being held; and I sat looking down on the gathering crowd, trying to discover some face known to my childhood, and still to be recognized through the veil which years must have woven across the features. When I had finished my breakfast, I went down and wandered about among the people. Groups of elderly men were talking earnestly; and young men and maidens who had come to be fee’d, were joking and laughing. They stared at the Sassenach gentleman, and, little thinking that he understood every word they uttered, made their remarks upon him in no very subdued tones. I approached a stall where a brown old woman was selling gingerbread and apples. She was talking to a man with long, white locks. Near them was a group of young people. One of them must have said something about me; for the old woman, who had been taking stolen glances at me, turned rather sharply towards them, and rebuked them for rudeness.

===Chinese===
我一路分程而行，从容不迫，多半骑着一匹心爱的黑马。这匹马几番随我冲锋陷阵，从战火里出来时也和它的主人一样带着几处伤痕，可心肺完健、四肢无恙。待我抵达那座离我出生之地最近的村庄时，已是夜色深沉。

次日清晨醒来，只见四野都被一片白雾笼罩，把周遭的群山尽数遮没。偶尔有一座峰峦从雾中探出头来，转瞬又退回那云雾的褶皱之中。在我让人把早餐桌摆好的那扇窗下，一条宽阔而散乱的街道上，正开着定期的集市；我便坐着俯瞰那越聚越多的人群，想从中认出某张童年时相识、纵使岁月必已在面容上织就一层帷幕、却仍能辨识的脸来。吃过早饭，我下楼走到人群里四处闲逛。一群群上了年纪的男人正谈得入神；那些赶来受雇的年轻男女，则在一旁打趣说笑。他们盯着这位撒克逊人（Sassenach）模样的绅士瞧，全然想不到他竟听得懂他们嘴里吐出的每一个字，便用并不怎么压低的嗓门对他评头论足。我走到一个摊子跟前，一位皮肤黧黑的老妇人正在那里卖姜饼和苹果。她正与一个披着一头长白发的男人说话。他们近旁围着一群年轻人。其中一个想必是说了句什么关于我的话；因为那老妇人——她方才一直在偷眼打量我——颇为突兀地转过身朝着他们，责备他们无礼。

===Original===
“The gentleman is no Sassenach,” she said. “He understands everything you are saying.”

This was spoken in Gaelic, of course. I turned and looked at her with more observance. She made me a courtesy, and said, in the same language:

“Your honour will be a Campbell, I’m thinking.”

“I am a Campbell,” I answered, and waited.

“Your honour’s Christian name wouldn’t be Duncan, sir?”

“It is Duncan,” I answered; “but there are many Duncan Campbells.”

“Only one to me, your honour; and that’s yourself. But you will not remember me?”

===Chinese===
「这位绅士可不是撒克逊人，」她说，「你们说的每一个字他都听得懂。」

这番话自然是用盖尔语（Gaelic）说的。我转过身，更加留心地打量起她来。她朝我行了一个屈膝礼，用同一种语言说道：

「老爷您，我这么琢磨着，该是坎贝尔家的人吧。」

「我正是坎贝尔家的人，」我答道，便等着她说下去。

「老爷您的教名，该不会是邓肯吧，先生？」

「正是邓肯，」我答道，「不过世上叫邓肯·坎贝尔的多得很。」

「于我却只有一位，老爷；那便是您本人。可您怕是不记得我了吧？」

===Original===
I did not remember her. Before long, however, urged by her anxiety to associate her Present with my Past, she enabled me to recall in her timeworn features those of a servant in my father’s house when I was a child.

“But how could you recollect me?” I said.

“I have often seen you since I left your father’s, sir. But it was really, I believe, that I hear more about you than anything else, every day of my life.”

“I do not understand you.”

“From old Margaret, I mean.”

“Dear old Margaret! Is she alive?”

===Chinese===
我的确不记得她了。然而不多时，在她那急于将自己的如今与我的往昔勾连起来的热切催促之下，我终于从她那被岁月磨蚀的面容里，唤回了一个我幼年时父亲家中女仆的影子。

「可你怎么会认得我呢？」我说。

「自打离开您父亲家以后，我也常能见着您哪，先生。可说实话，只怕是这一辈子里，我每天听的、比什么都多的，就是关于您的事儿。」

「我不大懂你的意思。」

「我是说，从老玛格丽特那里听来的。」

「亲爱的老玛格丽特！她还健在？」

===Original===
“Alive and hearty, though quite bedridden. Why, sir, she must be within near sight of a hundred.”

“Where does she live?”

“In the old cottage, sir. Nothing will make her leave it. The new laird wanted to turn her out; but Margaret muttered something at which he grew as white as his shirt, and he has never ventured across her threshold again.”

“How do you see so much of her, though?”

“I never leave her, sir. She can’t wait on herself, poor old lady. And she’s like a mother to me. Bless her! But your honour will come and see her?”

“Of course I will. Tell her so when you go home.”

===Chinese===
「健在着哪，精神也足，只是已全然卧床不起了。嗳呀，先生，她离一百岁怕是近在眼前了。」

「她住在哪儿？」

「还住在那座老石屋里哪，先生。怎么劝都不肯挪窝。新来的领主（laird）要把她赶出去；可玛格丽特嘟囔了几句什么，竟把他吓得脸白得跟他的衬衫一个样，打那以后，他就再没敢跨过她那道门槛了。」

「可你又是怎么能常常见到她的呢？」

「我就没离开过她身边，先生。她自己照料不了自己啦，可怜的老太太。她待我就跟亲娘一样。愿上帝保佑她！可老爷您会来看看她吧？」

「我自然要去。你回家时就这么对她说。」

===Original===
“Will you honour me by sleeping at my house, sir?” said the old man to whom she had been talking. “My farm is just over the brow of the hill, you know.”

I had by this time recognised him, and I accepted his offer at once.

“When might we look for you, sir?” he asked.

“When shall you be home?” I rejoined.

“This afternoon, sir. I have done my business already.”

“Then I shall be with you in the evening, for I have nothing to keep me here.”

===Chinese===
「先生肯赏光到舍下歇宿一夜么？」方才与她说话的那个老人开口道，「我的农庄就在这山脊那一边，您是晓得的。」

这时我也已认出他来，便当即应下了他的邀请。

「先生，我们什么时候可以恭候您光临呢？」他问。

「你什么时候到家？」我反问道。

「今天下午，先生。我的事已经办完了。」

「那我傍晚便到府上叨扰，因为我没有什么事要在此处逗留。」

===Original===
“Will you take a seat in my gig?”

“No, thank you. I have my own horse with me. You can take him in too, I dare say?”

“With pleasure, sir.”

We parted for the meantime. I rambled about the neighbourhood till it was time for an early dinner.

===Chinese===
「先生坐我的轻便马车去可好？」

「不了，多谢。我自己骑了马来。我想，你那儿也能一并容得下它吧？」

「乐意之至，先生。」

我们就此暂且作别。我在邻近一带四处闲逛，直逛到该用一顿略早的正餐的时辰。

## XIX. Old Constancy / XIX. 老忠驹

===Original===
The fog cleared off; and, as the hills began to throw long, lazy shadows, their only embraces across the wide valleys, I mounted and set out on the ride of a few miles which should bring me to my old acquaintance's dwelling.

I lingered on the way. All the old places demanded my notice. They seemed to say, "Here we are—waiting for you." Many a tuft of harebells drew me towards the roadside, to look at them and their children, the blue butterflies, hovering over them; and I stopped to gaze at many a wild rosebush, with a sunset of its own roses. The sun had set to me, before I had completed half the distance. But there was a long twilight, and I knew the road well.

===Chinese===
雾散了；当群山开始投下长长的、慵懒的影子——那是它们隔着宽阔山谷所能给出的唯一拥抱——我便跨上马，启程赶那几英里的路，去往我那位老相识的住处。

我一路流连。所有旧时熟稔的所在，都似在唤我驻足。它们仿佛在说：「我们在这儿——正等着你呢。」一簇簇风铃草把我引到路旁，去看它们，看它们的孩儿——那在花上翩翩盘旋的蓝蝴蝶；我又屡屡驻足，凝望一丛丛野蔷薇，每一丛都仿佛自有一抹由它满树蔷薇映成的夕照。我还没走完一半路程，太阳在我这儿便已落了山。好在暮色尚长，而这条路我又熟稔于心。

===Original===
My horse was an excellent walker, and I let him walk on, with the reins on his neck; while I, lost in a dream of the past, was singing a song of my own making, with which I often comforted my longing by giving it voice.

The autumn winds are sighing
Over land and sea;
The autumn woods are dying
Over hill and lea;
And my heart is sighing, dying,
Maiden, for thee.

The autumn clouds are flying
Homeless over me;
The homeless birds are crying
In the naked tree;
And my heart is flying, crying,
Maiden, to thee.

My cries may turn to gladness,
And my flying flee;
My sighs may lose the sadness,
Yet sigh on in me;
All my sadness, all my gladness,
Maiden, lost in thee.

===Chinese===
我的马极善徐行，我便由它信步向前，缰绳松松地搭在它颈上；而我则沉入一场往昔的旧梦，唱起一首自作的歌——我常借放声歌唱，来抚慰心中的渴念。

秋风正低叹，
掠过陆与海；
秋林正凋残，
漫过丘与野；
而我心亦低叹，亦凋残，
姑娘啊，为你。

秋云正飞渡，
无依过我穹；
无巢孤鸟啼，
枯树空枝中；
而我心亦飞渡，亦悲啼，
姑娘啊，奔向你。

我啼泣或转欢欣，
我飞渡或已远遁；
我低叹或忘哀愁，
却仍在心底低叹；
我所有哀愁，所有欢欣，
姑娘啊，尽失于你。

===Original===
I was roused by a heavy drop of rain upon my face. I looked up. A cool wave of wind flowed against me. Clouds had gathered; and over the peak of a hill to the left, the sky was very black. Old Constancy threw his head up, as if he wanted me to take the reins, and let him step out. I remembered that there used to be an awkward piece of road somewhere not far in front, where the path, with a bank on the left side, sloped to a deep descent on the right. If the road was as bad there as it used to be, it would be better to pass it before it grew quite dark. So I took the reins, and away went old Constancy. We had just reached the spot, when a keen flash of lightning broke from the cloud overhead, and my horse instantly stood stock-still, as if paralysed, with his nostrils turned up towards the peak of the mountain. I sat as still as he, to give him time to recover himself. But all at once, his whole frame was convulsed, as if by an agony of terror. He gave a great plunge, and then I felt his muscles swelling and knotting under me, as he rose on his hind legs, and went backwards, with the scaur behind him. I leaned forward on his neck to bring him down, but he reared higher and higher, till he stood bolt upright, and it was time to slip off, lest he should fall upon me. I did so; but my foot alighted upon no support. He had backed to the edge of the shelving ground, and I fell, and went to the bottom. The last thing I was aware of, was the thundering fall of my horse beside me.

===Chinese===
一滴沉甸甸的雨落在我脸上，把我惊醒。我抬头望去。一阵凉风如潮水般迎面涌来。云已聚拢；左边一座山峰的上空，天色黑如浓墨。老忠驹（Old Constancy）猛地昂起头，仿佛在催我收起缰绳、由它放蹄疾行。我记得前方不远处，从前有一段颇难走的路：那里小径左边是一道土坡，右边却向下斜接一处陡深的下坡。倘若那段路还和从前一样难行，那最好趁天未全黑便赶过去。于是我收起缰绳，老忠驹撒开蹄子便跑。我们刚到那处所在，一道凛冽的闪电便从头顶的乌云中劈落，我的马霎时呆立不动，仿佛被吓瘫了一般，鼻孔朝着山峦的峰顶翕张。我同它一样纹丝不动地坐着，好让它从容缓过神来。可倏忽之间，它整个身躯猛地痉挛起来，仿佛被一种痛彻的恐惧攫住。它使劲向前一纵，随即我便感到身下它的肌肉鼓胀、纠结成块——它用后腿直立起来，朝后退去，而身后便是那道陡崖。我俯身压向它的颈，想把它按下去，它却越扬越高，直至整个直挺挺地立起，此时我已该趁早滑下马背，免得它翻倒压在我身上。我纵身滑下；可我的脚却落了空。它已退到那片阶状坡地的边缘，我便直坠而下，落到谷底。我最后还意识到的，是身旁我的马如雷的坠地之声。

===Original===
When I came to myself, it was dark. I felt stupid and aching all over; but I soon satisfied myself that no bones were broken. A mass of something lay near me. It was poor Constancy. I crawled to him, laid my hand on his neck, and called him by his name. But he made no answer in that gentle, joyful speech—for it was speech in old Constancy—with which he always greeted me, if only after an hour's absence. I felt for his heart. There was just a flutter there. He tried to lift his head, and gave a little kick with one of his hind legs. In doing so, he struck a bit of rock, and the clank of the iron made my flesh creep. I got hold of his leg in the dark, and felt the shoe. *It was loose.* I felt his heart again. The motion had ceased. I needed all my manhood to keep from crying like a child; for my charger was my friend. How long I lay beside him, I do not know; but, at length, I heard the sound of wheels coming along the road. I tried to shout, and, in some measure, succeeded; for a voice, which I recognised as that of my farmer-friend, answered cheerily. He was shocked to discover that his expected guest was in such evil plight. It was still dark, for the rain was falling heavily; but, with his directions, I was soon able to take my seat beside him in the gig. He had been unexpectedly detained, and was now hastening home with the hope of being yet in time to welcome me.

===Chinese===
待我苏醒过来，天已全黑。我昏昏沉沉，浑身作痛；但很快我便确认，骨头一根也没断。身旁横着一团黑影。那是可怜的忠驹。我爬向它，把手搭上它的颈，唤着它的名字。可它没有用那种温存而欢欣的应答——因为老忠驹的应答确乎是一种话语——它每次迎接我时总以此相待，哪怕我们只分别了一个钟头。我去摸它的心口。那里只剩一下微弱的悸动。它挣扎着抬起头，又用一条后腿轻轻蹬了一下。这一蹬，蹄铁磕在一块岩石上，那铁器的叮当声令我汗毛直竖。我在黑暗中抓住它的腿，摸到了那块马蹄铁。*它松了。*我又去摸它的心口。那悸动已经止息。我须竭尽全部的男子气概，才能忍住不像个孩子似的放声大哭；因为我的坐骑便是我的朋友。我在它身旁躺了多久，自己也说不清；但终于，我听见大路上传来了车轮声。我尽力呼喊，多少算是喊出了声；因为一个声音欢快地应答——我听出那是我那位农夫朋友。他见盼望的客人竟落到这般凄惨境地，大为震惊。天仍旧黑着，雨正下得紧；但有他一路指引，我不一会儿便在那辆双轮轻马车里坐到了他身旁。他先前被一桩意外的事耽搁了，此刻正急急赶回家，巴望着还能赶得及迎候我。

===Original===
Next morning, after the luxurious rest of a heather-bed, I found myself not much the worse for my adventure, but heart-sore for the loss of my horse.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，在石楠铺就的床上奢侈地酣眠一场之后，我发觉自己经此一番历险并无大碍，只是为失去了我的马而痛彻于心。

## XX. Margaret / XX. 玛格丽特

===Original===
Early in the forenoon, I came in sight of the cottage of Margaret. It lay unchanged, a grey, stone-fashioned hut, in the hollow of the mountain-basin. I scrambled down the soft green brae, and soon stood within the door of the cottage. There I was met and welcomed by Margaret’s attendant. She led me to the bed where my old nurse lay. Her eyes were yet undimmed by years, and little change had passed upon her countenance since I parted with her on that memorable night. The moment she saw me, she broke out into a passionate lamentation such as a mother might utter over the maimed strength and disfigured beauty of her child.

“What ill has he done—my bairn—to be all night the sport of the powers of the air and the wicked of the earth? But the day will dawn for my Duncan yet, and a lovely day it will be!”

Then looking at me anxiously, she said,

“You’re not much the worse for last night, my bairn. But woe’s me! His grand horse, that carried him so, that I blessed the beast in my prayers!”

===Chinese===
将近上午时分，玛格丽特的小屋映入我的眼帘。它依旧是从前的模样，一座灰色的石砌小屋，卧在山间盆地的凹处。我攀下那片柔软青翠的山坡，不多时便站到了小屋的门内。玛格丽特的伴妇迎上来招呼我，引我走到老保姆躺卧的床前。她的双眸尚未被岁月蒙上翳障，自从那个难忘之夜与她分别以来，她的容颜几乎没有什么改变。她一见我，便迸出一阵痛切的哀号，仿佛一位母亲看见自己孩子残损的气力、毁损的容颜时所发出的那种悲恸。

"我的孩儿做了什么孽——竟要整夜受那空中诸般势力与地上恶人的作弄戏耍？可属于我的邓肯的那一天终会到来的，那将是个何等美好的日子！"

随后她忧心地望着我，说道：

"昨夜倒没把你折损多少，我的孩儿。可苦啊！他那匹骏马，那样驮着他，我在祷告里还曾为那畜生祈福呢！"

===Original===
I knew that no one could have yet brought her the news of my accident.

“You saw me fall, then, nurse?” I said.

“That I did,” she answered. “I see you oftener than you think. But there was a time when I could hardly see you at all, and I thought you were dead, my Duncan.”

I stooped to kiss her. She laid the one hand that had still the power of motion upon my head, and dividing the hair, which had begun to be mixed with grey, said: “Eh! The bonny grey hairs! My Duncan’s a man in spite of them!”

===Chinese===
我知道，还没有人能把我遇险的消息带给她。

"这么说，您看见我摔下来了，保姆？"我说。

"我确是看见了，"她答道。"我看见你的次数，比你以为的多得多。可曾有一段时间，我几乎完全看不见你，我以为你已经死了，我的邓肯。"

我俯身去吻她。她把那只尚能活动的手放在我头上，拨开已开始夹杂银丝的头发，说道："哎！好一头漂亮的白发！我的邓肯到底是个男子汉了！"

===Original===
She searched until she found the scar of the sabre-cut.

“Just where I thought to find it!” she said. “That was a terrible day; worse for me than for you, Duncan.”

“You saw me *then*!” I exclaimed.

“Little do folks know,” she answered, “who think I’m lying here like a live corpse in its coffin, what liberty my soul—and that’s just me—enjoys. Little do they know what I see and hear. And there’s no witchcraft or evildoing in it, my boy; but just what the Almighty made me. Janet, here, declares she heard the cry that I made, when this same cut, that’s no so well healed yet, broke out in your bonny head. I saw no sword, only the bursting of the blood from the wound. But sit down, my bairn, and have something to eat after your walk. We’ll have time enough for speech.”

===Chinese===
她摸索着，直到找见了那道刀伤（sabre-cut）的疤痕。

"果然就在我料想的地方！"她说。"那是个可怕的日子；对我来说比你更难熬啊，邓肯。"

"您当时竟能看见！"我失声叫道。

"那些以为我躺在这里、活像棺材里一具活尸的人，哪里晓得我的灵魂——那才是真正的我——享有着何等的自由，"她答道。"他们哪里晓得我看见什么、听见什么。这可不是什么巫术邪术，我的孩子；这不过是全能者造我时给我的本性。这儿的珍妮特说，当初这处至今还没大好的伤口在你那漂亮脑袋上裂开时，她听见了我的喊叫。我没有看见刀剑，只看见血从伤口里迸出来。可你先坐下吧，孩儿，走了这一路，吃点东西。咱们说话的日子还长着呢。"

===Original===
Janet had laid out the table with fare of the old homely sort, and I was a boy once more as I ate the well-known food. Every now and then I glanced towards the old face. Soon I saw that she was asleep. From her lips broke murmured sounds, so partially connected that I found it impossible to remember them; but the impression they left on my mind was something like this,

“Over the water. Yes; it is a rough sea—green and white. But over the water. There is a path for the pathless. The grass on the hill is long and cool. Never horse came there. If they once sleep in that grass, no harm can hurt them more. Over the water. Up the hill.” And then she murmured the words of the psalm: “He that dwelleth in the secret place.”

For an hour I sat beside her. It was evidently a sweet, natural sleep, the most wonderful sleep of all, mingled with many a broken dream-rainbow. I rose at last, and, telling Janet that I would return in the evening, went back to my quarters; for my absence from the midday meal would have been a disappointment to the household.

===Chinese===
珍妮特在桌上摆好了那种旧日家常的饭菜，我吃着这些熟悉的吃食，仿佛又回到了少年。我不时望一眼那张苍老的面容。不久便见她睡着了。从她唇间溢出喃喃的低语，断断续续，我根本记不住；但它们在我心头留下的印象，大约是这样的——

"过了水面。是的；那是一片汹涌的海——碧绿夹着雪白。可要过到水面那一边。无路之处自有路。山上的草又长又清凉。从来没有马到过那里。它们只要在那片草上一睡，再没有什么祸患能加害它们了。过了水面。上山去。"接着她喃喃念起《诗篇》的句子："那住在至高者隐密处的……"

我在她身旁坐了一个钟头。那显然是一场甘美而自然的睡眠——最奇妙的一种睡眠，其间交织着一道又一道破碎的梦之虹。我终于起身，告诉珍妮特我傍晚再来，便回到了住处；因为若不在主人家用午饭，会令那一家人失望的。

===Original===
When I returned to the cottage, I found Margaret only just awaked, and greatly refreshed. I sat down beside her in the twilight, and the following conversation began:

“You said, nurse, that, some time ago, you could not see me. Did you know nothing about me all that time?”

“I took it to mean that you were ill, my dear. Shortly after you left us, the same thing happened first; but I do not think you were ill then.”

“I should like to tell you all my story, dear Margaret,” I said, conceiving a sudden hope of assistance from one who hovered so near the unseen that she often flitted across the borders. “But would it tire you?”

“Tire me, my child!” she said, with sudden energy. “Did I not carry you in my bosom, till I loved you more than the darling I had lost? Do I not think about you and your fortunes, till, sitting there, you are no nearer to me than when a thousand miles away? You do not know my love to you, Duncan. I have lived upon it when, I daresay, you did not care whether I was alive or dead. But that was all one to my love. When you leave me now, I shall not care much. My thoughts will only return to their old ways. I think the sight of the eyes is sometimes an intrusion between the heart and its love.”

===Chinese===
待我回到小屋，发现玛格丽特刚刚醒转，精神大为恢复。我在暮色中坐到她身旁，于是有了下面这番对话：

"您说过，保姆，前些时候您一度看不见我。那段日子里，您对我的情形一无所知吗？"

"我只当那是你病了的意思，亲爱的。你离开我们之后不久，头一回出现这种情形；可我那时并不觉得你是在生病。"

"我想把我全部的遭遇讲给您听，亲爱的玛格丽特，"我说，忽然生出一线指望，想从这位如此贴近那不可见之境、常常飞越阴阳交界的人那里得到援手。"可您会不会太累？"

"累着我，我的孩子！"她说，忽然来了精神。"难道我没有把你揣在心窝里，直到爱你胜过我所失去的那个心肝宝贝？难道我没有日日惦念着你和你的遭际，以至于你此刻坐在那里，与我相隔并不比千里之外更近？你不晓得我待你的这份情，邓肯。我靠着它活到现在，而我敢说，那段日子里你并不在乎我是死是活。可那于我的爱毫无妨碍。这会儿你走了，我也不会太在意。我的心念不过是回到从前的老路上去罢了。我倒觉得，眼睛之所见，有时反而是横在心与其所爱之间的一重阻碍。"

===Original===
Here was philosophy, or something better, from the lips of an old Highland seeress! For me, I felt it so true, that the joy of hearing her say so turned, by a sudden metamorphosis, into freak. I pretended to rise, and said:

“Then I had better go, nurse. Goodbye.”

She put out her one hand, with a smile that revealed her enjoyment of the poor humour, and said, while she held me fast:

“Nay, nay, my Duncan. A little of the scarce is sometimes dearer to us than much of the better. I shall have plenty of time to think about you when I can’t see you, my boy.” And her philosophy melted away into tears, that filled her two blue eyes.

“I was only joking,” I said.

“Do you need to tell me that?” she rejoined, smiling. “I am not so old as to be stupid yet. But I want to hear your story. I am hungering to hear it.”

===Chinese===
这便是一位高地老女先知口中说出的哲理——或者比哲理更深的东西！于我而言，我感到这话如此真切，以至于听她这么说的那份喜悦，竟陡然一转，化作了一阵促狭。我装作要起身的样子，说道：

"那我最好还是走吧，保姆。再见了。"

她伸出那只手，嘴角露出一丝笑意——那笑意显出她其实很受用这蹩脚的玩笑——一面紧紧攥住我，说道：

"不，不，我的邓肯。有时，稀少的一点点，比丰足的好东西更叫我们珍重。等我再也看不见你的时候，有的是工夫去想你呢，我的孩子。"话未说完，她的那番哲理已化作了泪水，盈满她那一双蓝眼睛。

"我只是说着玩的，"我说。

"这还用你告诉我？"她含笑应道。"我还没有老到糊涂的地步呢。可我要听你的故事。我正饥渴地盼着听呢。"

===Original===
“But,” I whispered, “I cannot speak about it before anyone else.”

“I will send Janet away. Janet, I want to talk to Mr. Campbell alone.”

“Very well, Margaret,” answered Janet, and left the room.

“Will she listen?” I asked.

“She dares not,” answered Margaret, with a smile; “she has a terrible idea of my powers.”

The twilight grew deeper; the glow of the peat-fire became redder; the old woman lay still as death. And I told all the story of Lady Alice. My voice sounded to myself as I spoke, not like my own, but like its echo from the vault of some listening cave, or like the voices one hears beside as sleep is slowly creeping over the sense. Margaret did not once interrupt me. When I had finished she remained still silent, and I began to fear I had talked her asleep.

===Chinese===
"可是，"我压低声音说，"这事我不能当着旁人的面讲。"

"我去叫珍妮特出去。珍妮特，我要跟坎贝尔先生单独说说话。"

"好的，玛格丽特，"珍妮特答着，退了出去。

"她不会偷听吧？"我问。

"她不敢的，"玛格丽特含笑答道，"她对我的能耐可怕得厉害呢。"

暮色愈深；泥炭炉火的红光愈显炽烈；老人纹丝不动地躺着，如死一般安静。于是我把爱丽丝小姐的全部始末原原本本地讲了出来。说话时，我自己的声音听在耳中，不像是我本人在说，倒像是从某个侧耳倾听的洞穴穹顶下传来的回声，又像是睡意缓缓漫上知觉时、人在枕边听见的那种声音。玛格丽特一次也没有打断我。我说完了，她仍默然无语，我开始担心是不是说得让她睡着了。

===Original===
“Can you help me?” I said.

“I think I can,” she answered. “Will you call Janet?” I called her.

“Make me a cup of tea, Janet. Will you have some tea with me, Duncan?”

Janet lighted a little lamp, and the tea was soon set out, with “flour-scons” and butter. But Margaret ate nothing; she only drank her tea, lifting her cup with her one trembling hand. When the remains of our repast had been removed, she said:—

“Now, Janet, you can leave us; and on no account come into the room till Mr. Campbell calls you. Take the lamp with you.”

Janet obeyed without a word of reply, and we were left once more alone, lighted only by the dull glow of the fire.

===Chinese===
"您能帮帮我吗？"我说。

"我想我能，"她答道。"你叫一下珍妮特好吗？"我把她叫了来。

"给我沏杯茶吧，珍妮特。你陪我喝杯茶吗，邓肯？"

珍妮特点起一盏小灯，茶点不多时便摆了上来，还有面粉司康饼和黄油。可玛格丽特什么也没吃；她只是喝茶，用那只颤抖的手端起杯子。等残席撤去，她说道——

"好了，珍妮特，你可以出去了；在坎贝尔先生叫你之前，无论如何不要进这间屋子。把灯也带走。"

珍妮特一言不发地照办了，屋里又只剩下我们两个，只靠着炉火那幽暗的红光照亮。

===Original===
The night had gathered cloudy and dark without, reminding me of that night when she told me the story of the two brothers. But this time no storm disturbed the silence of the night. As soon as Janet was gone, Margaret said:—

“Will you take the pillow from under my head, Duncan, my dear?”

I did so, and she lay in an almost horizontal position. With the living hand she lifted the powerless arm, and drew it across her chest, outside the bedclothes. Then she laid the other arm over it, and, looking up at me, said:—

“Kiss me, my bairn; I need strength for what I am going to do for your sake.”

I kissed her.

“There now!” she said, “I am ready. Goodbye. Whatever happens, do not speak to me; and let no one come near me but yourself. It will be wearisome for you, but it is for your sake, my Duncan. And don’t let the fire out. Don’t leave me.”

===Chinese===
窗外夜色阴沉浓暗，让我想起当年她给我讲两兄弟故事的那个夜晚。可这一回，没有风暴惊扰夜的寂静。珍妮特一走，玛格丽特便说道——

"你把我头底下的枕头拿走好吗，邓肯，好孩子？"

我照做了，她便几乎平躺着。她用那只尚有知觉的手托起那条不能动弹的手臂，把它牵到被外，横放在胸前。然后又把另一条手臂搭在上面，仰起脸望着我，说道——

"吻吻我，我的孩儿；为了你，我要去做的事，需要气力。"

我吻了她。

"好啦！"她说，"我准备好了。再见了。无论发生什么，都不要跟我说话；除了你自己，不要让任何人靠近我。这对你来说会很熬人，可这是为了你啊，我的邓肯。还有，别让炉火灭了。别离开我。"

===Original===
I assured her I would attend to all she said. She closed her eyes, and lay still. I went to the fire, and sat down in a high-backed armchair, to wait the event.—There was plenty of fuel in the corner. I made up the fire, and then, leaning back, with my eyes fixed on it, let my thoughts roam at will. Where was my old nurse now? What was she seeing or encountering? Would she meet our adversary? Would she be strong enough to foil him? Was she dead for the time, although some bond rendered her return from the regions of the dead inevitable?—But she might never come back, and then I should have no tidings of the kind which I knew she had gone to see, and which I longed to hear!

I sat thus for a long time. I had again replenished the fire—that is all I know about the lapse of the time—when, suddenly, a kind of physical repugnance and terror seized me, and I sat upright in my chair, with every fibre of my flesh protesting against some—shall I call it presence?—in its neighbourhood. But my real self repelled the invading cold, and took courage for any contest that might be at hand. Like Macbeth, I only inhabited trembling; *I* did not tremble. I had withdrawn my gaze from the fire, and fixed it upon the little window, about two feet square, at which the dark night looked in. Why or when I had done so I knew not.

What I next relate, I relate only as what seemed to happen. I do not altogether trust myself in the matter, and think I was subjected to a delusion of some sort or other. My feelings of horror grew as I looked through or rather at the window, till, notwithstanding all my resolution and the continued assurance that nothing could make me turn my back on the cause of the terror, I was yet so far *possessed* by a feeling I could neither account for nor control, that I felt my hair rise upon my head, as if instinct with individual fear of its own—the only instance of the sort in my experience.—In such a condition, the sensuous nerves are so easily operated upon, either from within or from without, that all certainty ceases.

===Chinese===
我向她保证，她说的每一句我都会照办。她合上双眼，静静躺着。我走到炉火旁，在一把高背扶手椅上坐下，静候事态的发展。——墙角有的是柴火。我把炉火添旺，然后向后一靠，目光凝定在火上，任思绪自由地游荡。我的老保姆此刻在哪里？她正在看见什么、遭遇什么？她会遇见我们的那个对头吗？她有没有足够的力量挫败他？她是不是暂时死去了，尽管有某种羁绊注定她终将从亡者之域折返？——可她也许再也不回来了，那我就永远无从得知她此去正是要探看、而我又那样渴望知晓的消息了！

我就这样坐了很久。我又添了一回火——这是我对那段时光流逝的全部记忆——忽然间，一种生理上的厌恶与恐惧攫住了我，我在椅子上猛地坐直，浑身上下每一根肌纤维都在抗拒某种——我该称之为"临在"的东西？——它就在近旁。但我真正的自我击退了那侵入的寒意，鼓起了勇气，以应对可能到来的任何较量。如同麦克白那样，我不过是栖息在一具战栗的躯壳里；战栗的并不是*我*。我不知何时已将目光从炉火上移开，定定地望向那扇约莫两尺见方的小窗——黑夜正从那里朝里张望。我何以如此、何时如此，自己全然不知。

接下来我要讲述的，我只当作似乎是如此发生的事来记述。在这件事上我并不能完全信赖自己，我认为自己受了某种或此或彼的幻觉所左右。当我透过窗户、或者说凝视着窗户时，那种悚怖之感愈演愈烈，直到——尽管我下了万般决心，尽管我不断笃信无论什么都不能叫我背过身去逃避这恐惧的根源——我仍被一种既无从解释、又无法克制的情绪深深地*攫住*，以至于感到头发在头顶上根根竖起，仿佛它们自身便怀着一份独立的惊恐——这在我的经历中是绝无仅有的一回。——在这种状态之下，感官神经无论受内里还是外在的作用都太容易被牵动，以至于一切确信都归于消散。

===Original===
I saw two fiery eyes looking in at the window, huge, and wide apart. Next, I saw the outline of a horse’s head, in which the eyes were set; and behind, the dimmer outline of a man’s form seated on the horse. The apparition faded and reappeared, just as if it retreated, and again rode up close to the window. Curiously enough, I did not even fancy that I heard any sound. Instinctively I felt for my sword, but there was no sword there. And what would it have availed me? Probably I was in more need of a soothing draught. But the moment I put my hand to the imagined sword-hilt, a dim figure swept between me and the horseman, on my side of the window—a tall, stately female form. She stood facing the window, in an attitude that seemed to dare the further approach of a foe. How long she remained thus, or he confronted her, I have no idea; for when *self*-consciousness returned, I found myself still gazing at the window from which both apparitions had vanished. Whether I had slept, or, from the relaxation of mental tension, had only forgotten, I could not tell; but all fear had vanished, and I proceeded at once to make up the sunken fire. Throughout the time I am certain I never heard the clanking shoe, for that I should have remembered.

The rest of the night passed without any disturbance; and when the first rays of the early morning came into the room, they awoke me from a comforting sleep in the armchair. I rose and approached the bed softly.

===Chinese===
我看见两只火一般的眼睛正从窗户朝里张望，硕大无比，分得很开。接着，我看见一个马头的轮廓，那两只眼睛便嵌在上面；再往后，是一个骑在马上的人影，轮廓更为模糊。那幻影时隐时现，就仿佛它退去了，又重新驰近窗前。奇怪的是，我甚至不曾觉得自己听见了任何声响。我本能地去摸腰间的剑，可那里并没有剑。况且剑于我又有什么用呢？多半我更需要一服镇定的药剂。然而就在我的手搭上那柄想象中的剑柄的一瞬间，一个朦胧的身影从我这一侧掠到了我与骑马人之间——一个高挑、庄严的女性身形。她面朝窗户站着，那姿态仿佛在向敌人挑衅，看他敢不敢再上前一步。她这样站了多久，他与她对峙了多久，我全然不知；因为当*自我*意识重新回来时，我发现自己仍凝望着那扇窗，而两个幻影都已从中消失了。我是睡着了，还是仅仅因为心神松弛而忘却了一切，我说不准；但一切恐惧都已烟消云散，我随即起身去添那快要熄灭的炉火。这段时间里我敢肯定，我从未听见那叮当作响的马蹄铁（the clanking shoe）——因为那声音我是不可能不记得的。

这一夜余下的时光平静无事；当清晨最初的光线照进屋子时，把我从扶手椅上那场安稳的睡眠中唤醒了。我起身，轻手轻脚地走到床前。

===Original===
Margaret lay as still as death. But having been accustomed to similar conditions in my Alice, I believed I saw signs of returning animation, and withdrew to my seat. Nor was I mistaken; for, in a few minutes more, she murmured my name. I hastened to her.

“Call Janet,” she said.

I opened the door, and called her. She came in a moment, looking at once frightened and relieved.

“Get me some tea,” said Margaret once more.

After she had drunk the tea, she looked at me, and said,

“Go home now, Duncan, and come back about noon. Mind you go to bed.”

She closed her eyes once more. I waited till I saw her fast in an altogether different sleep from the former, if sleep that could in any sense be called.

===Chinese===
玛格丽特纹丝不动地躺着，如同死去。但因我早已在我的爱丽丝小姐身上见惯了类似的情形，我自以为看出了生机复苏的迹象，便退回到座位上。我并没有看错；因为又过了几分钟，她喃喃地唤出了我的名字。我赶紧走到她身旁。

"叫珍妮特来，"她说。

我推开门叫她。她片刻便到了，神色又是惊惶又是宽慰。

"给我弄点茶来，"玛格丽特又说了一遍。

喝过茶之后，她望着我，说道，

"现在回去吧，邓肯，中午前后再来。记着上床睡一觉。"

她再次合上双眼。我直等到看见她沉入一场与先前截然不同的酣睡——如果那也算是睡眠的话。

===Original===
As I went, I looked back on the vision of the night as on one of those illusions to which the mind, busy with its own suggestions, is always liable. The night season, simply because it excludes the external, is prolific in such. The more of the marvellous anyone may have experienced in the course of his history, the more sceptical ought he to become, for he is the more exposed to delusion. None have made more blunders in the course of their revelations than genuine seers. Was it any wonder that, as I sat at midnight beside the woman of a hundred years, who had voluntarily died for a time that she might discover what most of all things it concerned me to know, the ancient tale, on which, to her mind, my whole history turned, and which she had herself told me in this very cottage, should take visible shape to my excited brain and watching eyes?

I have one thing more to tell, which strengthens still further this view of the matter. As I walked home, before I had gone many hundred yards from the cottage, I suddenly came upon my own old Constancy. He was limping about, picking the best grass he could find from among the roots of the heather and cranberry bushes. He gave a start when I came upon him, and then a jubilant neigh.

But he could not be so glad as I was. When I had taken sufficient pains to let him know this fact, I walked on, and he followed me like a dog, with his head at my heel; but as he limped much, I turned to examine him; and found one cause of his lameness to be, that the loose shoe, which was a hind one, was broken at the toe; and that one half, held only at the toe, had turned round and was sticking right out, striking his forefoot every time he moved. I soon remedied this, and he walked much better.

But the phenomena of the night, and the share my old horse might have borne in them, were not the subjects, as may well be supposed, that occupied my mind most, on my walk to the farm. Was it possible that Margaret might have found out something about *her*? That was the one question.

===Chinese===
一路走着，我回首夜里那番异象，只当作是心智忙于自我暗示时极易陷入的种种幻觉之一。夜间之所以特别容易滋生此类，恰恰是因为它隔绝了外在的万物。一个人一生中所经历的奇事愈多，便愈应当多一分怀疑，因为他愈加容易受到蒙蔽。在种种启示之中出差错最多的，莫过于真正的先见之人。当我午夜坐在那位百岁老人身旁——她为了替我探明那件我最该知晓的事，甘愿暂时死去——而那个在她看来关系着我全部命途、且正是她在这间小屋里亲口讲给我听的古老传说，竟然借我亢奋的头脑与守望的双目幻化出可见的形相，这又有什么可奇怪的呢？

还有一件事要讲，它更能佐证我对这件事的看法。我往回走的路上，离开小屋还没走几百码，忽然撞见了我那匹老马忠贞（Constancy）。它正一瘸一拐地东走西逛，从石楠和越橘丛的根缝间挑着最嫩的草吃。冷不丁见我走近，它猛地一惊，旋即发出一声欢腾的嘶鸣。

可它再怎么欢喜，也比不上我。等我费了一番工夫让它明白了这个事实，便继续赶路，它像条狗似的跟着我，脑袋贴在我的脚后跟旁；但它跛得厉害，我便转身查看；发现它跛脚的一个缘由，是那只松脱的马蹄铁——后蹄上的一只——在蹄尖处断裂了；其中一半只连着蹄尖，整个翻了过来直直地戳向外侧，它每动一步便磕在前蹄上。我三下两下把它弄好，它走起来便顺当多了。

然而，夜里那种种异象、以及我那匹老马在其中可能扮演了什么角色，诸位可想而知，并不是我走向农庄这一路上最占据心神的话题。玛格丽特有没有可能打听到了关于*她*的什么消息？这才是唯一要紧的问题。

===Original===
After removing the anxiety of my hostess, and partaking of their Highland breakfast, a ceremony not to be completed without a glass of peaty whisky, I wandered to my ancient haunt on the hill. Thence I could look down on my old home, where it lay unchanged, though not one human form, which had made it home to me, moved about its precincts. I went no nearer. I no more felt that that was home, than one feels that the form in the coffin is the departed dead. I sat down in my old study-chamber among the rocks, and thought that if I could but find Alice she would be my home—of the past as well as of the future;—for in her mind my necromantic words would recall the departed, and we should love them together.

Towards noon I was again at the cottage.

Margaret was sitting up in bed, waiting for me. She looked weary, but cheerful; and a clean white mutch gave her a certain *company*-air. Janet left the room directly, and Margaret motioned me to a chair by her side. I sat down. She took my hand, and said,

“Duncan, my boy, I fear I can give you but little help; but I will tell you all I know. If I were to try to put into words the things I had to encounter before I could come near her, you would not understand what I meant. Nor do I understand the things myself. They seem quite plain to me at the time, but very cloudy when I come back. But I did succeed in getting one glimpse of her. She was fast asleep. She seemed to have suffered much, for her face was very thin, and as patient as it was pale.”

===Chinese===
解除了女房东的忧虑，又用过他们那顿高地早餐——这仪式少不了一杯泥煤味的威士忌才算圆满——我便信步走向山上那处我从前流连的旧地。从那里可以俯瞰我旧日的家，它依然是从前的模样，只是曾经让那地方成为我家的那些人影，再没有半个在它四周走动。我没有再走近。我对那里是"家"的感觉，恰如人对棺中那副形骸是"逝者"的感觉一样，已荡然无存。我在乱石间那间旧日的石室书房中坐下，心想：只要我能找到爱丽丝小姐，她便是我的家——过去的家，也是未来的家；——因为在她的心智里，我那通灵的言语将唤回逝者，我们将一同去爱他们。

将近正午，我又来到了小屋。

玛格丽特在床上撑坐起来等着我。她面有倦色，神情却愉悦；一顶洁白的睡帽给她添了几分*待客*时的郑重。珍妮特立刻退了出去，玛格丽特示意我坐到她身旁的椅子上。我坐下。她握住我的手，说道，

"邓肯，我的孩子，我怕是帮不了你多少忙；可我会把我知道的全告诉你。要是我想用言语去描述那些我为了接近她而不得不经历的遭遇，你也听不懂我说的是什么。连我自己也不明白那些事。当时在我眼里清清楚楚，可一回来便如坠云雾。但我确实成功瞥见了她一眼。她正熟睡着。她似乎吃了不少苦，因为她的脸很消瘦，而又和她的苍白一样满是忍耐。"

===Original===
“But where was she?”

“I must leave you to find out that, if you can, from my description. But, alas! it is only the places immediately about the persons that I can see. Where they are, or how far I have gone to get there, I cannot tell.”

She then gave me a rather minute description of the chamber in which the lady was lying. Though most of the particulars were unknown to me, the conviction, or hope at least, gradually dawned upon me, that I knew the room. Once or twice I had peeped into the sanctuary of Lady Alice’s chamber, when I knew she was not there; and some points in the description Margaret gave set my heart in a tremor with the bare suggestion that she might now be at Hilton Hall.

“Tell me, Margaret,” I said, almost panting for utterance, “was there a mirror over the fireplace, with a broad gilt frame, carved into huge representations of crabs and lobsters, and all crawling sea-creatures with shells on them—very ugly, and very strange?”

===Chinese===
"可她在哪里？"

"这个我只能留给你从我的描述里去推断了，你要是能推断出来的话。可是，唉！我所能看见的，只是紧贴着那些人四周的地方。至于他们在何处，或者我走了多远才到达那里，我说不上来。"

随后她相当细致地向我描述了那位小姐所卧之处的房间。尽管其中大多数细节我并不熟悉，一个念头——或者至少是一线希望——却渐渐在我心中明晰起来：我认得那间房。有一两回，趁我知道爱丽丝小姐不在的时候，我曾偷偷窥望过她闺房那片圣地；而玛格丽特描述中的几处细节，光是暗示她此刻也许正在希尔顿庄园（Hilton Hall），便已叫我的心颤栗不已。

"告诉我，玛格丽特，"我说，激动得几乎喘不过气来，"壁炉上方是不是有一面镜子，镜框又宽又是镀金的，上面雕满了巨大的螃蟹和龙虾，还有各种各样带壳的爬行海族——非常丑陋，也非常古怪？"

===Original===
She would have interrupted me before, but I would not be stopped.

“I must tell you, my dear Duncan,” she answered, “that in none of these trances, or whatever you please to call them, did I ever see a mirror. It has struck me before as a curious thing, that a mirror is then an absolute blank to me—I see nothing on which I could put a name. It does not even seem a vacant space to me. A mirror must have nothing in common with the state I am then in, for I feel a kind of repulsion from it; and indeed it would be rather an awful thing to look at, for of course I should see no reflection of myself in it.”

(Here I beg once more to remind the reader, that Margaret spoke in Gaelic, and that my translation into ordinary English does not in the least represent the extreme simplicity of the forms of her speculations, any more than of the language which conveyed them.)

“But,” she continued, “I have a vague recollection of seeing some broad, big, gilded thing with figures on it. It might be something else, though, altogether.”

===Chinese===
她先前几次想打断我，可我不容她插话。

"我必须告诉你，亲爱的邓肯，"她答道，"在这种种出神之中——或随你怎么叫它——我从来没有看见过镜子。以前我就觉得奇怪，在那种时候镜子对我来说竟是一片绝对的空白——我看不见任何能叫得出名字的东西。它在我眼中甚至不像是一处空缺。镜子必定与我当时所处的状态毫无瓜葛，因为我对它有一种说不出的排斥；说真的，去照那东西倒是件颇可怕的事，因为我在里面当然看不见自己的倒影。"

（这里我再次恳请读者留意：玛格丽特说的是盖尔语（Gaelic），而我译成寻常英语的文字，完全不能传达她思索时所特有的那种极其质朴的形式，一如那承载思索的语言本身之质朴。）

"不过，"她接着说，"我依稀记得看见了什么又宽又大、镀金的、上面有图形的东西。可那也许完全是另一码事。"

===Original===
“I will go in hope,” I answered, rising at once.

“Not already, Duncan?”

“Why should I stay longer?”

“Stay over tonight.”

“What is the use? I cannot.”

“For my sake, Duncan!”

“Yes, dear Margaret; for your sake. Yes, surely.”

“Thank you,” she answered. “I will not keep you longer now. But if I send Janet to you, come at once. And, Duncan, wear this for my sake.”

===Chinese===
"我怀着希望去了，"我答着，霍然起身。

"这就走了吗，邓肯？"

"我还有什么理由再留下呢？"

"再留一晚吧。"

"有什么用呢？我不能。"

"看在我的分上，邓肯！"

"好，亲爱的玛格丽特；看在您的分上。好的，我留下。"

"谢谢你，"她答道。"这会儿不再留你了。可要是我派珍妮特来找你，你立刻就来。还有，邓肯，看在我的分上，把这个戴上。"

===Original===
She put into my hand an ancient gold cross, much worn. To my amazement I recognised the counterpart of one Lady Alice had always worn. I pressed it to my heart.

“I am a Catholic; you are a Protestant, Duncan; but never mind: that’s the same sign to both of us. You won’t part with it. It has been in our family for many long years.”

“Not while I live,” I answered, and went out, half wild with hope, into the keen mountain air. How deliciously it breathed upon me!

I passed the afternoon in attempting to form some plan of action at Hilton Hall, whither I intended to proceed as soon as Margaret set me at liberty. That liberty came sooner than I expected; and yet I did not go at once. Janet came for me towards sundown. I thought she looked troubled. I rose at once and followed her, but asked no questions. As I entered the cottage, the sun was casting the shadow of the edge of the hollow in which the cottage stood just at my feet; that is, the sun was more than half set to one who stood at the cottage door. I entered.

===Chinese===
她把一枚古旧的黄金十字架放到我手里，已被岁月磨得斑驳。令我惊奇的是，我认出它与爱丽丝小姐一直佩戴的那枚一模一样。我把它贴在心口。

"我是天主教徒；你是新教徒，邓肯；可不要紧：这记号对我们俩是一样。你别把它丢了。它在我们家已经传了许多许多年。"

"只要我活着就不会，"我答道，便走了出去，怀着几乎狂乱的希望，走进了凛冽的山间空气里。那空气拂在我身上，何其醉人！

整个下午我都在盘算到了希尔顿庄园该如何行事，只等玛格丽特一放行我便即刻启程。那放行来得比我所料的更早；可我并没有马上动身。将近日落时分，珍妮特来找我。我看她神色不安。我立刻起身随她走去，却没有问任何话。我走进小屋时，太阳正把小屋所在那片凹地边缘的影子投在我脚边；也就是说，若有人站在小屋门口，太阳已沉下了大半。我走了进去。

===Original===
Margaret sat, propped with pillows. I saw some change had passed upon her. She held out her hand to me. I took it. She smiled feebly, closed her eyes, and went with the sun, down the hill of night. But down the hill of night is up the hill of morning in other lands, and no doubt Margaret soon found that she was more at home there than here.

I sat holding the dead hand, as if therein lay some communion still with the departed. Perhaps she who saw more than others while yet alive, could see when dead that I held her cold hand in my warm grasp. Had I not good cause to love her? She had exhausted the last remnants of her life in that effort to find for me my lost Alice. Whether she had succeeded I had yet to discover. Perhaps she knew now.

I hastened the funeral a little, that I might follow my quest. I had her grave dug amidst her own people and mine; for they lay side by side. The whole neighbourhood for twenty miles round followed Margaret to the grave. Such was her character and reputation, that the belief in her supernatural powers had only heightened the notion of her venerableness.

When I had seen the last sod placed on her grave, I turned and went, with a desolate but hopeful heart. I had a kind of feeling that her death had sealed the truth of her last vision. I mounted old Constancy at the churchyard gate, and set out for Hilton Hall.

===Chinese===
玛格丽特倚着枕头坐着。我看出她身上已起了某种变化。她向我伸出手来。我握住了。她微微一笑，合上双眼，随着那太阳一同沉下了夜之山坡。然而沉下夜之山坡，在另一片国土便是升上晨之山坡；毫无疑问，玛格丽特很快就发觉，那边比这边更是她的家。

我坐着，握着那只已然冰冷的手，仿佛其中尚存某种与逝者相通的联结。也许，这位生前比旁人看得更多的人，死后也能看见我正用温暖的掌心握着她冰冷的手。我难道没有充足的理由去爱她吗？为了替我寻找我那失落的爱丽丝小姐，她已耗尽了生命中最后一点残余。她究竟有没有成功，我尚待查明。也许她现在已经知道了。

我略略加快了葬礼的进程，好去继续我的寻访。我把她的坟茔安置在她的族人与我的族人之间；因为他们本就比邻而眠。方圆二十英里的乡邻全都来为玛格丽特送葬。她的为人和声望便是如此——人们对她超自然之能的信仰，只不过愈发加深了对她那可敬之德的景仰。

待我亲眼看着最后一抔泥土盖上了她的坟头，我转身离去，怀着一颗凄凉却又满怀希望的心。我隐隐有一种感觉：她的死，已为她最后那番异象的真实性盖上了印信。我在墓园门口跨上老马忠贞，动身前往希尔顿庄园。

## XXI. Hilton / XXI. 希尔顿

===Original===
It was a dark, drizzling night when I arrived at the little village of Hilton, within a mile of the Hall. I knew a respectable second-rate inn on the side next the Hall, to which the gardener and other servants had been in the habit of repairing of an evening; and I thought I might there stumble upon some information, especially as the old-fashioned place had a large kitchen in which all sorts of guests met. When I reflected on the utter change which time, weather, and a great scar must have made upon me, I feared no recognition. But what was my surprise when, by one of those coincidences which have so often happened to me, I found in the ostler one of my own troop at Waterloo! His countenance and salute convinced me that he recognised me. I said to him:

===Chinese===
我到达名叫希尔顿（Hilton）的小村时，正值一个阴沉、飘着细雨的夜，那村子距希尔顿庄园不过一英里。我知道在靠近庄园的那一侧有一家体面的二等客栈，园丁和其他仆人晚间惯于上那儿去歇脚；我想自己也许能在那里碰上点什么消息，更何况这家老式客栈有一间大厨房，形形色色的客人都聚在其中。想到岁月、风霜，再加上一道大伤疤，必然已令我面目全非，我便一点也不担心被人认出。可叫我惊奇的是——凭着我身上屡屡发生的那类巧合——我竟在那马夫身上认出滑铁卢（Waterloo）一役时我自己队里的一名士兵！他的脸色和敬礼都叫我确信：他认出我来了。我对他说道：

===Original===
"I know you perfectly, Wood; but you must not know me. I will go with you to the stable."

He led the way instantly.

===Chinese===
“我认得你清清楚楚，伍德（Wood）；可你不能认得我。我跟你到马厩去。”

他当即领路在前。

===Original===
"Wood," I said, when we had reached the shelter of the stable, "I don't want to be known here, for reasons which I will explain to you another time."

"Very well, sir. You may depend on me, sir."

"I know I may, and I shall. Do you know anybody about the Hall?"

"Yes, sir. The gardener comes here sometimes, sir. I believe he's in the house now. Shall I ask him to step this way, sir?"

"No. All I want is to learn who is at the Hall now. Will you get him talking? I shall be by, having something to drink."

"Yes, sir. As soon as I have rubbed down the old horse, sir—bless him!"

"You'll find me there."

===Chinese===
“伍德，”等我们进了马厩、有了遮蔽，我说道，“我不想在这里被人认出来，缘故改日再向你说明。”

“好的，先生。您尽可信赖我，先生。”

“我知道我可以，也一定会。庄园里头的人，你可有认识的？”

“有的，先生。园丁有时上这儿来，先生。我看他这会儿就在屋里呢。要我去请他到这边来一趟么，先生？”

“不必。我所想知道的，不过是此刻庄园里都有些什么人。你能让他打开话头么？我就在旁边，要点东西喝着。”

“好的，先生。等我把这匹老马擦抹舒齐了就来，先生——上帝保佑它！”

“你会在那儿找到我。”

===Original===
I went in, and, with my condition for an excuse, ordered something hot by the kitchen-fire. Several country people were sitting about it. They made room for me, and I took my place at a table on one side. I soon discovered the gardener, although time had done what he could to disguise him. Wood came in presently, and, loitering about, began to talk to him.

===Chinese===
我走进去，以自己一路劳顿为由，在厨房炉火边要了点热的。几个乡下人围炉坐着。他们给我腾出地方，我便在一边的一张桌旁坐下。不多时我就认出了那个园丁，尽管岁月已尽力地替他改换了模样。伍德随后也进来，在四下里磨蹭着，同他攀谈起来。

===Original===
"What's the last news at the Hall, William?" he said.

"News!" answered the old man, somewhat querulously. "There's never nothing but news up there, and very newfangled news, too. What do you think, now, John? They do talk of turning all them greenhouses into hothouses; for, to be sure, there's nothing the new missus cares about but just the finest grapes in the country; and the flowers, purty creatures, may go to the devil for her. There's a lady for ye!"

===Chinese===
“庄园上头近来有什么新鲜事，威廉（William）？”他说。

“新鲜事！”老人答道，带几分不悦，“那上头从来就不缺新鲜事，而且尽是些稀奇古怪的新花样。你倒想想看，约翰（John）？他们正商量着把那些温室统统改成暖房呢；因为说实在的，那位新太太除了全国最上等的葡萄，什么都不放在心上；至于那些花儿，娇滴滴的小东西，随它们下地狱去吧，她才不在乎。好一位夫人哪！”

===Original===
"But you'll be glad to have her home, and see what she's like, won't you? It's rather dull up there now, isn't it?"

"I don't know what you call dull," replied the old man, as if half offended at the suggestion. "I don't believe a soul missed his lordship when he died; and there's always Mrs. Blakesley and me, as is the best friends in the world, besides the three maids and the stableman, who helps me in the garden, now there's no horses. And then there's Jacob and—"

===Chinese===
“可她回了家，你总该高兴能瞧瞧她是个什么模样吧？那上头如今怪冷清的，不是么？”

“我不知道你所谓的冷清是什么意思，”老人答道，像是这话有几分触怒了他，“勋爵归天的时候，我相信没有一个人惦记他；再说，布莱克斯利太太（Mrs. Blakesley）和我一直都在，我俩是世上顶要好的朋友，此外还有三个女佣和一个马夫——既然没了马，那马夫就在园子里替我搭把手。再加上雅各（Jacob），还有——”

===Original===
"But you don't mean," said Wood, interrupting him, "that there's *none* o' the family at home now?"

"No. Who should there be? Least ways, only the poor lady. And she hardly counts now—bless her sweet face!"

===Chinese===
“可你总不至于说，”伍德打断他，“如今家里*一个*主子都不在了吧？”

“没了。还能有谁呢？顶多只余下那位可怜的小姐罢了。可她如今也算不得数啦——上帝保佑她那张甜甜的脸！”

===Original===
"Do you ever see her?" interposed one of the by-sitters.

"Sometimes."

"Is she quite crazy?"

"Al‑to‑gether; but that quiet *and* gentle, you would think she was an angel instead of a mad woman. But not a notion has she in *her* head, no more than the babe unborn."

===Chinese===
“你可见过她么？”旁边坐着的一个插嘴问。

“有时见。”

“她当真疯了么？”

“彻—底疯了；可她又是那般安静*又*温柔，你会当地是一位天使，而不是个疯女人。只是*她*脑子里再没有半点念头，和那未出世的婴孩没什么两样。”

===Original===
It was a dreadful shock to me. Was this to be the end of all? Were it not better she had died? For me, life was worthless now. And there were no wars, with the chance of losing it honestly.

===Chinese===
这对我犹如一记骇人的重击。难道这一切，落到的便是这般结局？她倒不如死了的好吧？于我而言，生命如今已一文不值。偏又没有战事，给我一个机会将它光明磊落地丢掉。

===Original===
I rose, and went to my own room. As I sat in dull misery by the fire, it struck me that it might not have been Lady Alice after all that the old man spoke about. That moment a tap came to my door, and Wood entered. After a few words, I asked him who was the lady the gardener had said was crazy.

"Lady Alice," he answered, and added: "A love story, that came to a bad end up at the Hall years ago. A tutor was in it, they say. But I don't know the rights of it."

===Chinese===
我起身，回到自己房中。当我满怀着沉闷的苦楚坐在炉边时，忽然灵光一闪：那老人所说的，也许根本不是爱丽丝小姐（Lady Alice）也未可知。就在那一刻，房门上传来轻轻一叩，伍德走了进来。寒暄了几句，我便问他，园丁口中那个疯了的女子究竟是谁。

“爱丽丝小姐，”他答道，又补一句，“一段情事，好些年前在庄园里头落了个凄凉的收场。据说其中牵涉一个家庭教师。但详细的底里，我就不得而知了。”

===Original===
When he left me, I sat in a cold stupor, in which the thoughts—if thoughts they could be called—came and went of themselves. Overcome by the appearances of things—as what man the strongest may not sometimes be?—I felt as if I had lost her utterly, as if there was no Lady Alice anywhere, and as if, to add to the vacant horror of the world without her, a shadow of her, a goblin *simulacrum*, soulless, unreal, yet awfully like her, went wandering about the place which had once been glorified by her presence—as to the eyes of seers the phantoms of events which have happened years before are still visible, clinging to the room in which they have indeed *taken place*. But, in a little while, something warm began to throb and flow in my being; and I thought that if she were dead, I should love her still; that now she was not worse than dead; it was only that her soul was out of sight. Who could tell but it might be wandering in worlds of too noble shapes and too high a speech, to permit of representation in the language of the world in which her bodily presentation remained, and therefore her speech and behaviour seemed to men to be mad? Nay, was it not in some sense better for me that it should be so? To see once the pictured likeness of her of whom I had no such memorial, would I not give years of my poverty-stricken life? And here was such a statue of her, as that of his wife which the widowed king was bending before, when he said:—

"What fine chisel
Could ever yet cut breath?"

This statue I might see, "looking like an angel," as the gardener had said. And, while the bond of visibility remained, must not the soul be, somehow, nearer to the earth, than if the form lay decaying beneath it? Was there not some possibility that the love for whose sake the reason had departed, might be able to recall that reason once more to the windows of sense—make it look forth at those eyes, and lie listening in the recesses of those ears? In her somnambulic sleeps, the present body was the sign that the soul was within reach: so it might be still.

===Chinese===
他离去之后，我呆坐着，陷入一种冰冷的麻木，其间那些心思——若是还配称作心思的话——兀自来去自如。我被事物的表象压倒了——最强的人又何尝不曾有时被压倒呢？——我觉得自己仿佛已将她彻底失去，仿佛这世上哪里都不再有爱丽丝小姐其人；又仿佛，为了给我那没有她的世界那空荡荡的恐怖更添一分，她的一道影子，一个妖灵般的幻影（simulacrum），无魂、不真，却可怖地酷肖于她，仍在那个曾因她的临在而熠熠生辉的所在游荡徘徊——一如在先知的眼中，多年前已然发生之事的幻影依旧可见，盘桓在它们当真*发生*过的那间屋子里不肯散去。然而不多时，一股暖意开始在我生命深处搏动、流淌；我想，纵使她已死去，我仍会爱她；而此刻她并不比死去更糟，不过是她的灵魂隐没在了视线之外。谁能断言，那灵魂不是正游荡在形体过于高贵、言语过于渊深的世界里，以至无法用她肉身所寄留的这个尘世的语言来加以呈现，于是她的言谈举止，在世人眼中便显作癫狂？不，从某种意义上说，事情如此，于我岂不反倒更好？为了能见上哪怕一次那个我再无别的纪念的她的画像般的容颜，我难道不肯舍去我贫薄生命里的好些年岁？而这里，便正有这样一尊她的雕像，恰如那位鳏居的君王俯身其前、他亡妻的那尊雕像——他当时说道：——

“何等精妙的凿刀
竟能凿出呼吸？”

这尊雕像，我是能够见到的——如那园丁所言，“看起来就像一位天使”。而且，只要这可见的羁绊尚未断绝，灵魂岂不在某种意义上，比那形体腐烂于黄土之下时，离这人间更近一些？难道没有一线可能——那曾令理智为之出走的爱情，能够把那理智再一次唤回到感官的窗前——令它从那双眼睛里向外张望，又卧在那两只耳朵的幽深处静静聆听？在她梦游般的昏睡里，现存的肉身正是灵魂近在咫尺的征兆：那么如今，或许依然如此。

===Original===
Mrs. Blakesley was still at the lodge, then: I would call upon her tomorrow. I went to bed, and dreamed all night that Alice was sitting somewhere in a land "full of dark mountains," and that I was wandering about in the darkness, alternately calling and listening; sometimes fancying I heard a faint reply, which might be her voice or an echo of my own; but never finding her. I woke in an outburst of despairing tears, and my despair was not comforted by my waking.

===Chinese===
布莱克斯利太太原来仍住在门房那里：那我明日便去拜访她。我上了床，整夜梦见爱丽丝坐在某一处“满布幽暗群山”的国度的某个地方，而我在黑暗里四处游荡，时而呼唤，时而谛听；有时恍惚觉得听见一声微弱的回应，那也许是她的声音，也许不过是我自己的回声；可我始终寻她不见。我在一阵绝望的泪水中惊醒，而醒来也并未给我的绝望带来半点慰藉。

## XXII. The Sleeper / XXII. 沉睡者

===Original===
It was a lovely morning in autumn. I walked to the Hall. I entered at the same gate by which I had entered first, so many years before. But it was not Mrs. Blakesley that opened it. I inquired after her, and the woman told me that she lived at the Hall now, and took care of Lady Alice. So far, this was hopeful news.

I went up the same avenue, through the same wide grassy places, saw the same statue from whose base had arisen the lovely form which soon became a part of my existence. Then everything looked rich, because I had come from a poor, grand country. In all my wanderings I had seen nothing so rich; yet now it seemed poverty-stricken. That it was autumn could not account for this; for I had always found that the sadness of autumn vivified the poetic sense; and that the colours of decay had a pathetic glory more beautiful than the glory of the most gorgeous summer with all its flowers. It was winter within me—that was the reason; and I could feel no autumn around me, because I saw no spring beyond me. It had fared with my mind as with the garden in *The Sensitive Plant*, when the lady was dead. I was amazed and troubled at the stolidity with which I walked up to the door, and, having rung the bell, waited. No sweet memories of the past arose in my mind; not one of the well-known objects around looked at me as claiming a recognition. Yet, when the door was opened, my heart beat so violently at the thought that I might see her, that I could hardly stammer out my inquiry after Mrs. Blakesley.

===Chinese===
那是一个明媚可爱的秋日清晨。我步行前往庄园。我从许多年前初次踏入时所走的那同一扇门进去。然而来开门的并不是布莱克斯利太太。我向人打听她的下落，那妇人告诉我，她如今就住在庄园里，照看着爱丽丝小姐。至少这一点，算是个叫人宽慰的消息。
